Linea Directa Aseguradora · LDA-ES · the new Memo conveyor's card of 25 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Linea Directa Aseguradora

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 55,102 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Judgement: externally, Linea Directa is in a mature, still-favourable phase: the market's repricing created a price umbrella that the company is converting into above-market volume, the Spanish economy supports demand and higher yields support investment income; but the forces that matter most for the 2027 margin are turning less favourable, namely energy-led claims inflation (with the 2027 Baremo update as the dated test around December 2026) and a mutual competitor that has chosen retention over margin.

  • The external forces explain both halves of the stock's situation: earnings are rising (umbrella, growth, yields) while the multiple sits at the 10th percentile (the market judges the margin to be at a peak and the earnings to be "shoppable", in a risk-off regime that penalises small, illiquid names).
  • For the model: market premium growth of 4% to 6% a year, Linea Directa's share gain adding 2 to 4 points of growth, a broadly flat CR in 2027 as the central case, with a downside case in which claims severity of 5% to 6% against company pricing of 2% to 3% lifts the CR by 1 to 3 points before pricing catches up (the company re-priced within about four to six quarters in 2023 to 2024).
  • The one external change that would most improve the case is a confirmed moderation of Spanish inflation and a 2027 Baremo update at or below 3%; the one that would most damage it is an update above 4% coinciding with Mutua extending its freeze.
1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0248C-0540C-0752C-0952C-3560C-3956
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C-488th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is rising modestly, driven by one actor (Mutua Madrileña), while the rest of the industry remains margin-disciplined because the industry is still barely profitable; the direction is up but a destructive price war is not the base case for the next 6 to 12 months.

  • The aggressor. Mutua Madrileña, a mutual with no shareholders and the largest auto insurer since 2025 (18.6% auto share), widened its tariff freeze from 30% of auto policyholders in 2025 to over 40% (stated in April 2026) and then 50% (July 2026), grew auto premiums 13.17% in H1 26 (twice the sector's 6.57%), targets a combined ratio of about 97% to protect a 90% retention rate, is spending EUR 80m on AI and data in 2025 to 2026, and bought the parts distributor Reynasa in May 2026 to lower its repair costs.
  • The disciplined majority. Mapfre is shedding unprofitable fleets (Iberia auto premiums +1.7% in H1 26; 170,000 to 180,000 policies shed; "will not go crazy chasing volume"; average auto premiums +6% in Q1 26), Allianz Spain pushed 9.6% renewal increases, and Grupo Catalana Occidente raised motor premiums 9.6% in 2025 without losing customers.
  • Why a price war is unlikely near term. The sector motor CR was about 98.4% to 99% in 2025 and the market average was 98.2% (cited by Mutua itself); Mutua's own 97% target leaves it little room to cut further. An industry near breakeven has no margin to give away.
  • What it means for Linea Directa. It sits between the two camps: it has the lowest motor CR among the large writers (91.1% in H1 26 against Mapfre Iberia auto 93.7%) and prices at about 2% against the market's 5% to 6%, so it is taking share from the incumbents that are repricing and losing volume. The risk is relative: Mutua's freeze narrows the price gap Linea Directa relies on to win customers.
  • Skew. Mildly negative over 12 months; the cone widens in 2027 if claims inflation forces everyone to reprice except Mutua.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

D4013th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the net effect is turning from a tailwind in 2025 to H1 26 into a headwind for 2027; claims severity inputs are re-accelerating with energy-led inflation while the only clear tailwind, cheaper reinsurance, is small for this company.

  • Claims inputs (the cost base that matters: the loss ratio is about 71% of earned premium). Spare parts drove a 6.9% repair-cost increase in 2023; electric-vehicle repairs cost 15% to 24% more than combustion vehicles; Spain has an estimated 25% shortage of skilled trades, raising construction and home-repair costs by 8% to 12% and lengthening claims times; medical cost inflation and hospital tariffs pressure health; and the Brent price of USD 111 and Spanish inflation of 4.5% (August 2026) feed parts, labour and, with a lag, the Baremo (C-04). Mutua's Reynasa purchase shows the largest competitor attacking parts cost directly.
  • Mitigants specific to Linea Directa. Owned CAR workshops (over 12,000 repairs a year), more than 1,000 partner bodyshops, multi-year inflation-insulated paint contracts, a claimed repair cost EUR 200 below the market average, and a diversified supplier base (576 non-claims suppliers, 91% domestic). Supplier concentration is low. The weak point is that customer redirection to the owned network fell from 71% (2021) to 50% (2022) with no later figure disclosed.
  • Reinsurance (small tailwind). Property catastrophe pricing fell 10% to 20% at 2026 renewals and retrocession 12% to 20%; the Insurance Sector Digest notes retail buyers keep the saving. Linea Directa buys excess-of-loss cover for motor and home and cedes 50% of health under a quota share that has paid no commissions since a 2023 addendum, so the saving is a small number.
  • Net. For 2027, model claims severity of 4% to 6% in motor (Baremo plus parts plus labour) against company pricing that has recently run at about 2%; the gap must be closed by pricing, selection or frequency, or the loss ratio rises.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

B+523rd of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro is supportive for the company's demand and investment income, mildly negative for its claims costs in 2027, and bites hardest at the level of the equity market's discount rate and risk appetite, which is where the stock's 10th-percentile multiple is being set.

  • Company level. Spain is Europe's growth outperformer (GDP 2.8% in 2025; 2.3% to 2.4% forecast for 2026 and 1.9% for 2027; real pay per employee +4.5% since 2019 against +1.9% in the euro area; employment growth 1.9% in 2026). This supports policy counts and lapses. Inflation is the negative: energy-led (Brent USD 111), it reaches claims severity through parts, labour and the Baremo. Higher yields help: the EUR 1.05bn bond portfolio yields about 2.6% to 3.1% with a duration of about 2.9 to 3.25 years, so reinvestment at higher rates adds a few million euros a year (the financial investment result rose 5.2% in H1 26).
  • Sector level (insurance). The Portfolio Fitness for Fighting digest is Overweight insurance (higher yields raise reinvestment income; defensive cases), but its Momentum tilt moved to Underweight for weak-price-momentum stocks and its Fragility tilt is Double Underweight for small, less liquid names. Linea Directa is simple (one country, one dominant line) but small (about USD 1.1m to 1.3m a day traded, free float about 30% to 38%), so it sits on the wrong side of both tilts in a risk-off market.
  • Industry level (financials). The industry digest's rule is to sort stories by whether earnings can be shopped; motor insurance earnings can be (annual contracts, price comparison), which keeps the market cautious on the cohort (motor and home-led retail non-life: Admiral at the 9th percentile of its range, Linea Directa at the 10th).
  • Where it bites hardest. On valuation (discount rate and risk appetite) now; on claims cost in 2027. Solvency sensitivity: +100 basis points in rates costs about 9.6 points of solvency, +100 basis points of credit spread about 5.0 points; a Spanish sovereign-spread shock (French stress has spread to Italy) would reduce solvency but not threaten the dividend from 196%.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–52

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: four further dynamics matter: European insurance consolidation (a latent positive), the Bankinter stake (a latent overhang that could become a float-widening positive), the Solvency II capital handover (a modest positive), and the risk-off market regime (a negative for a small, illiquid, weak-momentum stock).

  • Consolidation. The sector is in an active deal phase: Aviva bought Direct Line (a direct motor insurer) for about GBP 3.7bn; Mapfre bought Safety Insurance for USD 1.54bn at 17 times earnings; Mutua bought Reynasa and Colombian insurers; the Insurance Sector Digest notes that a bid reprices a whole tier. Linea Directa is the only listed pure direct insurer in Spain, at a 10th-percentile multiple, with 38% held by two long-term blocks (Cartival 20.55% to 20.75%, Bankinter 17.42%). A bid would need those blocks, so it is optionality, not a base case.
  • Bankinter's stake. Deutsche Bank identified Linea Directa as the main source of volatility in Bankinter's capital; Bankinter has a new CEO (Gloria Ortiz). A sale of the 17.42% block (about EUR 230m at today's price, roughly 190 days of average traded value) would pressure the price through a placement discount but would widen a free float of about 30% to 38%, which would improve index eligibility and liquidity.
  • Capital handover. Revised Solvency II from 30-Jan-27 and dividend declarations in February to March 2027 are dated sector catalysts; for Linea Directa the uplift is small (see C-04) but the declaration season is when a higher payout could be announced.
  • Regime. Risk-off, defensives leading cyclicals on all windows, breadth weak, US 10-year yield about 5.10%; small, illiquid names are de-rated regardless of fundamentals (the Fitness for Fighting Fragility tilt).
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

C+606th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the entry threat is low in the 18-month horizon; adjacent entrants (banks, retailers, car makers) are the most real medium-term threat to distribution, standard entrants have failed to scale, and orthogonal entrants (AI agents, embedded insurance) are the most important long-term threat.

  • Adjacent. Banks distributing insurance (Santander with Mapfre; banks buying out bancassurance partners for capital relief); retailers (Carrefour with Mapfre); car makers embedding insurance (Tesla, GM OnStar elsewhere; electric-vehicle makers with telematics data). Threat: medium over 3 to 5 years, as they capture the customer at the point of purchase; low over 18 months in Spain.
  • Standard. Admiral Seguros (since 2006, 0.46m customers, loss-making), Verti (Mapfre's digital brand, CR about 102.6%), insurtechs Prima and LeoCare. Threat: low; the direct market has been hard to scale for newcomers, and Solvency II capital plus DGSFP licensing raise the bar.
  • Orthogonal. AI shopping agents and app-integrated products (Tuio in ChatGPT), white-label API aggregators that cannibalise direct insurers' deals (Cover Genius-type platforms), and in the long term autonomous vehicles shifting liability to manufacturers. Threat: low near term, medium to high for the terminal value.
  • For the model: no new-entrant effect on 2026 to 2028 volumes; carry the orthogonal threat in the multiple, not in the forecasts.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

B565th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are mostly a support (an owned repair network, a dispersed and domestic supplier base, highly rated reinsurers, softening reinsurance prices); the sources of risk are trade-labour scarcity for home repairs, medical providers in health, and the unexplained fall in the use of the owned repair network.

  • Support. CAR (Madrid 6,625 and Barcelona 5,525 repairs in 2025) and over 1,000 partner workshops; multi-year inflation-insulated paint contracts; LDA Reparaciones' own plumbers, masons and painters for home claims; 576 non-claims suppliers with EUR 172m of orders, 91% domestic; supplier payment period 25.19 days; excess-of-loss reinsurers rated AA on average (minimum A-), with collateral below AA-; property reinsurance prices falling.
  • Risk. A 25% shortage of skilled trades with 8% to 12% construction inflation and waiting lists of weeks (home severity and service); medical inflation and the July 2026 switch of the health network to HNA (execution); the 2023 addendum that removed reinsurance commissions from the 50% health quota share (running to December 2029), which keeps health's reported CR high; customer redirection to the owned and partner networks fell from 71% (2021) to 50% (2022) with no later figure, which questions how much of the claimed EUR 200 per-claim saving is captured.
  • For the model: home severity 5% to 8% a year; motor parts and labour 4% to 6%; no reinsurance cost increase.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0162C-0352C-0447C-0862C-3262C-3748C-4362
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

D6210th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: demand is healthy and highly visible in volume, but its value growth is cyclical and slowing as the market's repricing cycle matures; no two-year cyclical swing in volume is in prospect.

  • What is changing. Spanish non-life premiums grew 7.79% to a record EUR 49.96bn in 2025 (motor +8.38% to EUR 14.29bn, home +7.42%, health +12.08%), but the market slowed to +5.4% to 5.5% in H1 26, with motor at +6.57%. Most of this is price, not new cover: Allianz's research describes Spanish P&C premium growth as inflationary pass-through rather than deeper insurance penetration, and the vehicle fleet grew only 2.3% in 2025. The market's value growth is therefore tied to the claims-inflation and repricing cycle and is decelerating as that cycle matures.
  • What is structural. Motor third-party cover is compulsory, so the volume of demand is close to non-discretionary. Structural supports are real but slow: net migration of about 2 million people over three years, a growing housing stock, a household savings rate of 11.8% against a 2015 to 2019 average of 7.3%, non-life penetration of 2.7% of GDP against 3.6% in the euro area, and health insurance growing at double digits as the public system strains.
  • Visibility. Volume visibility is high (annual, recurring, compulsory contracts; Spanish GDP forecast at 2.3% to 2.4% for 2026 and 1.9% for 2027). Value visibility is medium: if claims inflation re-accelerates in 2027 (Spanish inflation was 4.5% in August 2026, the highest of the four large euro-area economies), the market will keep raising prices and nominal premium growth will hold up; if inflation fades, market premium growth falls toward 4% to 5%.
  • For Linea Directa specifically, demand is better than the market because its price is below the market's and the affordability squeeze pushes customers toward cheap direct cover: 290,400 new policyholders in 2025 (a record) and about 278,000 net additions in the twelve months to June 2026.
  • For the model: take market premium growth of about 4% to 6% a year in 2027 to 2028 and add Linea Directa's share gain on top; do not extrapolate the 2025 market growth of 7.8%.
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

D5213th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three disruption forces are live; the efficiency force (AI in service and claims) is strengthening and acts in the company's favour now; the distribution force (aggregators and AI shopping agents) is strengthening as a narrative and as a long-term threat but is not visible in the company's numbers; autonomous vehicles are irrelevant to the 18-month horizon but matter for the multiple.

  • Efficiency (positive, strengthening). Linea Directa's chatbot resolves 60% to 64% of queries, AI cut average claims processing times by about 30%, 65.7% of roadside requests and over half of motor and home claims are filed digitally, and 92% of customers are digital. A digital-native, single-site insurer without legacy branches captures AI savings faster than branch-based incumbents. Admiral targets GBP 100m of AI efficiency by 2028; Allianz expects 30 basis points a year off its expense ratio.
  • Distribution (negative, strengthening as a narrative). Price comparison sites take about 30% of Spanish direct volumes; AXA's surveys project generative-AI shopping in insurance rising from 10% (2025) to 31% (2029); Tuio has a ChatGPT-integrated home product; DNB Carnegie estimates AI agents could trim some insurers' EPS by 3% to 15%; the Insurance Sector Digest names shopping agents a new, early theme and Nordic P&C stocks fell 6.9% over three months partly on that worry. Against this, Linea Directa transacts mostly on its own websites (with Mutua, the exception in Spain), its acquisition is at record levels, and 69% of Spanish consumers want human validation of AI financial decisions. For a low-cost provider, a world where agents shop on price is not obviously worse than today; the loss is the brand-loyalty part of retention.
  • Technology-spend gap (negative, slowly). Mapfre spends over EUR 1bn a year on technology and Mutua EUR 80m on AI; Linea Directa's intangible capital expenditure was EUR 8.34m in FY25 (although much of its technology spend runs through operating expenses). The gap erodes the pricing-agility edge over three to five years.
  • Autonomous vehicles. Admiral's 45% de-rating against the sector shows the market's fear; Admiral itself expects automated vehicles to be 4% of the UK car parc by 2035. Not an earnings issue before 2030; a multiple issue now.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

D4713th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: regulation is a mild headwind on claims cost and a mild tailwind on capital; the one item that matters for the model is the 2027 Baremo update, due around December 2026, which is likely to be higher than 2026's 2.7% because it follows Spanish inflation.

  • Baremo (bodily-injury compensation scale). Annual updates were +4.1% (2022), +8.5% (2023), +3.8% (2024), +2.8% (2025) and +2.7% (2026); bodily injury is about 30% of Spanish motor claims cost (Mapfre). Updates track inflation with a lag, and Spanish CPI ran at 3.2% (May 2026) to 4.5% (August 2026). A 2027 update above 4.0% would, in the Earnings Review's own framing, require aggressive tariff action. Settlements are paid on the scale of the payment year, so a higher update also raises the cost of open claims (a reserving effect, not only a new-business effect).
  • Solvency II revision (from 30-Jan-27). The risk-margin cost of capital falls from 6.00% to 4.75%; the sector estimate is about 8 percentage points of solvency uplift on average. For a short-tail non-life book the risk margin is small, so expect a smaller uplift for Linea Directa (a few points); it supports, but does not transform, the dividend capacity.
  • Compliance costs. DORA (from January 2025), the EU AI Act (most provisions from 2-Aug-26), CSRD and the Insurance Recovery and Resolution Directive (transposition by 29-Jan-27) raise fixed overheads, which weigh more on smaller insurers.
  • Structural positive. Spain's Insurance Compensation Consortium absorbs extreme weather losses (about 95% of them, per the Mapfre file), which shields the home book from catastrophe volatility.
  • Politics. A snap general election on 29-Nov-26 could bring a centre-right coalition (Jefferies); no insurance-specific policy is at stake. Tax audits for 2020 to 2022 (corporate income tax and VAT) began in 2024; a one-off risk of unknown size.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

D6210th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: yes, and it is the single most important external driver of the case: the incumbents' repricing creates a price umbrella under which Linea Directa wins customers; the driver is still working but is no longer accelerating, and Mutua's freeze partly closes the umbrella.

  • Mechanism. The market raised motor prices by about 5% to 6% on average (late 2025 to Q1 26; Mapfre +6% in Q1 26, +8.4% in 2025; Allianz Spain +9.6% on renewals; motor insurance CPI +9.5% at one point) while Linea Directa raised by about 2%. A low-cost direct insurer whose CR is several points below the market's can let the gap widen and convert it into customers: motor policies +8.5% to 2.85m (+222,174 in twelve months), market share gain of roughly 0.2 to 0.3 points a year from about 6.5% to 7%.
  • Supporting structural driver. The European direct P&C market grew at a 7% compound rate in 2019 to 2024 against 4% for P&C overall (AXA); affordability pressure (26% of Spaniards at risk of poverty, per AXA's research) pushes price-sensitive customers online.
  • Is it accelerating? No. Mapfre's clean-up is well advanced (its Iberia auto CR is already 93.7%), so its volume shedding will slow; Mutua is freezing prices for half its customers; Linea Directa's own premium growth slowed from about 10% in Q1 to about 8% in Q2. The driver is mature but intact.
  • What would re-accelerate it. A claims-inflation step-up in 2027 that forces the market to raise prices again, which widens the umbrella if Linea Directa can keep pricing below the market thanks to its cost advantage. That is the paradox to model: inflation hurts Linea Directa's margin and helps its volume.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

C627th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer demand strongly supports the volume part of the thesis (record acquisition, rising retention signals); the support is fragile because the customers are being won on price, and price-won customers leave when prices rise.

  • Evidence. Total policyholders 3.73m (FY25, +8.5%), 3.80m (Q1 26), 3.86m (H1 26, +7.8%); 290,400 net additions in 2025, a record; motor 2.85m (+8.5%, +222,174); health 128,196 (+10.4%); home 788,332 (+3.7%); other 91,949. Motor churn about 14% (retention about 86%, against Occident's 87.8% and Mutua's 90%); NPS up 5.79 points to 36.83; seven in ten customers use the app.
  • Why demand favours the company now. Household budgets are squeezed (motor insurance CPI rose 9.5% and health 10.3% at one point), so customers shop; a low-priced direct insurer is the beneficiary.
  • Fragility. The Business Foundations report rates retention as structurally low: switching costs on comparison sites are low, NPS lags Admiral's 50-plus by about 15 points, and the early-warning signal it names is a loss of more than 5% of policyholders in a rate-adjustment quarter. When the company raised prices in 2023 to 2024, it lost about 4.5% of customers within months. Management has not disclosed cohort retention curves or lifetime value by vintage.
  • For the model: policyholder growth of about 7% to 8% in FY26 and 5% to 7% in FY27 if prices stay low; if the company raises average premiums by 4% to 5% to meet claims inflation, cut policy growth to 2% to 4% for two to four quarters.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

D4812th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators are, on balance, slightly negative for margins (Baremo), positive for barriers and capital (Solvency II, licensing, the Consortium), and neutral politically; the position is moderately fragile because the Baremo is inflation-linked and its 2027 update is likely to rise.

  • Hurting. The Baremo updates (C-04) raise bodily-injury severity every year and apply to open claims; Deutsche Bank calls the Baremo tables a pivotal risk for Spanish non-life insurers; the 2016 Baremo overhaul previously pressured the whole sector. DORA, the EU AI Act and CSRD raise fixed compliance costs, which weigh more on a 2,400-person insurer.
  • Helping. Solvency II barriers to entry; the DGSFP composite prohibition; revised Solvency II (risk margin cut) from January 2027; the Consortium absorbing extreme weather; Spanish transposition of the Solvency II revision is on track (draft bill approved November 2025).
  • Political. The 29-Nov-26 election; budget rollover paralysis; no policy targets insurance pricing. A price-regulation intervention (for example on renewal pricing) has not been raised in the files.
  • Fragility. The Baremo is symmetric across the sector, so it hurts absolute but not relative profitability; its fragility for Linea Directa is that the company is pricing below the market when the update lands.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

D6210th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the addressable market is large relative to the company (it holds about 2.3% of Spanish non-life, 6.5% of motor, 2.6% of home, 0.3% of health), so market size does not limit growth over the model horizon; the realistic constraint is the direct channel's share of each market, which allows company growth of about 6% to 9% a year in the mid term (market growth of 4% to 6% plus share gain), not double digits indefinitely.

  • Markets (2025). Spanish non-life EUR 49.96bn (+7.79%), motor EUR 14.29bn, health EUR 11.97bn, home EUR 6.45bn, plus SMEs and commercial lines. Penetration 2.7% of GDP against a euro-area 3.6% gives the market structural headroom.
  • Realistic TAM. The direct and aggregator channel is about 27% to 30% of motor (about EUR 4bn); Linea Directa has roughly a quarter of it. The direct channel grows faster than the market (7% against 4% a year in Europe, 2019 to 2024). In home, where banks control much of distribution through mortgages, the direct share is lower, and the company's home growth has slowed to about 2%. Health is a large, fast-growing market (+12%) in which the company is tiny but loss-making.
  • Mid-term growth arithmetic. Motor market 4% to 6% nominal, plus 2 to 3 points of share gain = 6% to 9%; home 2% to 5%; health 12% to 18%; other lines high growth from a tiny base. Group 6% to 9%, consistent with management's "high single-digit motor premium growth" and with the Buy-rated brokers' FY27 revenue growth of about 8% (Bestinver, JB Capital).
  • Long run. The 30% channel ceiling binds after 2030 unless the company adds partnerships or intermediated distribution, which would raise its expense ratio.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0652C-1052
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

D5211th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: written premium growth peaked in 2025 and is gently decelerating; earned revenue growth is still accelerating and will peak in H2 26; the margin cycle driven by the company's own repricing peaked in 2024 and the margin is now held up by selection, frequency and expense rather than price.

  • Written growth: FY25 +11.3%, Q1 26 +10.2%, Q2 26 about +8.2% (derived). Motor: Q1 +10.6%, Q2 about +9.1%. Home: FY25 +6.3%, Q1 +2.6%, Q2 about +1.7%. Health: Q1 +20.1%, Q2 about +11.8% (health renews mainly in Q1).
  • Earned growth (FactSet revenue): FY24 +3.7%, FY25 +8.3%, H1 26 +10.6%. Because earned premium lags written premium by about six months, earned growth should run near 9% to 10% in H2 26 and converge toward the written rate (about 7% to 9%) during 2027.
  • Customers: +8.5% in Q1 26 and +7.8% in H1 26; the rate of net additions (about 278,000 over twelve months) is steady, so the slowing of premium growth is mostly a slowing of average premium, not of customer acquisition.
  • Margin cycle: Linea Directa's own large price rises came in 2023 to 2024 (it described them as the highest average increases in the Spanish motor sector and accepted a 4.2% to 4.5% fall in customers). Since 2025 average motor price changes have been about 1% to 2%. The CR still improved from 92.6% (FY25) to 91.1% (H1 26), which means the improvement is now coming from risk selection, earned-through of older rate, moderate frequency and expense, not from new pricing. The Insurance Sector Digest's rule (a year of rate above claims inflation gives about six quarters of rising earned margin) implies the earned-margin tailwind from the 2024 repricing is largely exhausted by end-2026.
  • For the model: a cycle turning from "acceleration plus margin recovery" to "steady growth at a stable margin"; FY27 written growth of about 7% to 8% with a broadly flat CR is the central case.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

D5213th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the external picture is balanced to mildly positive for 2026 and turns balanced to mildly negative for 2027; tailwinds (demand, the incumbents' price umbrella, macro growth, yields) are moderate and mature; headwinds (claims inflation, Mutua, the shopping-agent narrative) are building rather than fading.

Sector themes (Insurance Sector Digest, six stories), taken in turn:

  1. January 2027 capital release (Solvency II): small tailwind; short-tail non-life gains least from the risk-margin change.
  2. Motor in the UK and Spain: neutral to mild tailwind; the digest itself says to treat Spain as a stable rather than expanding margin because Mutua caps it. That matches this analysis.
  3. Cheaper reinsurance kept by retail buyers: small tailwind; Linea Directa buys excess-of-loss cover, so the saving is small in euros.
  4. Specialty bids: not applicable directly; indirectly a reminder that bids reprice tiers (optionality, C-09).
  5. Life narrowed to Aviva's gap: not applicable.
  6. Shopping agents (new, early): narrative headwind for any motor insurer whose earnings can be shopped; not yet visible in Linea Directa's acquisition data.

Industry themes (Financials Industry Digest): "sort by whether earnings can be shopped" (headwind: motor can be shopped); "a worry about a price war does not clear on a print" (headwind for the re-rating: the Mutua worry is of that kind); "separate cheap against peers from cheap against its own history" (Linea Directa is cheap against its own history, at the 10th percentile, which needs proof of durable margins to re-rate).

Value-chain level: demand moderate tailwind (C-01, C-08); competition mild headwind (C-02); disruption balanced (C-03); regulation mild headwind on claims, mild tailwind on capital (C-04); inputs turning into a headwind (C-05).

Overall. Tailwinds are worth roughly the company's above-market growth (share gain of a few points a year in a market growing 4% to 6%); headwinds threaten roughly 1 to 3 points of CR in 2027 if pricing is not raised. The external picture supports the volume part of the base case and puts the margin part at modest risk.

Internal change forces/risks

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Judgement: the internal story is continuity of a programme that has worked: an internally promoted, operator-type management turned the company around with fast, painful repricing in 2023 to 2024 and is now using the restored margin and its cost advantage to grow above the market; the internal change force is solid but narrowing, and its next leg depends on capital returns and disclosure rather than on operations.

  • What changed in the last 6 to 12 months: guidance raised twice (in words), the dividend stepped up, health provider switched, Cartival adding to its stake. What did not change: leadership, strategy, perimeter, M&A stance, capital policy, disclosure.
  • The incentive structure (70% weight on written premiums in annual targets) and the observed behaviour (pricing at about 2% against a market at about 6%) point the same way: model the company choosing volume over margin, with a broadly flat CR. The risk is the false-friend pattern the Risks report highlights (premium growth masking weaker selection), which cannot be ruled out because frequency, severity and reserve development are not disclosed.
  • The best internal upside for the model is the payout: capital generation supports 70% to 80% without solvency falling below 180%, against consensus that assumes about 60%.
2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement52C-1462C-8640C-5962C-8155D-0344
Group judgement

Risks

A521st of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: the risk profile is that of a simple, well-capitalised insurer with one dominant risk, the motor CR, and one dominant way it can go wrong: a claims-inflation step in 2027 while the company prices below the market; sudden fundamental tails are low-probability, while sentiment risks are higher-probability and amplified by size, float and momentum.

  • The Risks report's competing-hypotheses verdict ("false friend", zero hard inconsistencies) should be read with care: the Wisdom Library's "the vaguest explanation wins a fewest-contradictions contest" warns that a vague hypothesis is hard to contradict. The specific, testable version of the false-friend risk is that premium growth is being bought with sub-inflation pricing and supported by undisclosed reserve releases; Q3 26 and FY26 loss ratios, the 2027 Baremo update and any disclosure of reserve development are the tests.
  • Growth deceleration (C-80) is gentle and expected; it is a risk to the multiple only if motor growth falls below about 7%.
  • Valuation provides a partial cushion: the multiple is near its trough, so the downside comes mainly through earnings (C-83).
  • For the model: carry a downside case with FY27 CR 93.5% to 94.5% (net income about EUR 85m to 95m) and a probability that is not trivial given 2026 Spanish inflation.
C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

C-628th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: operational standards are clearly improving (loss ratio, expense ratio, digital penetration, capital); governance and control standards are not improving and on one measure are deteriorating.

  • Improving. Loss ratio 72.6% (FY24) to 71.5% (FY25) to 70.9% (H1 26); expense ratio 22.1% to 21.1% to 20.2%; fully digital new-policy sales 8% to 9% (2025) to 12% (Q1 26); AI chat resolution 64% with conversation volume up 1.2 times to 175,000 a month; digital roadside requests 65.7% (+5.1 points); NPS +5.79 to 36.83; solvency 182.6% to 196.3%; headcount down 0.9% to 2,411 while customers grew 8%; managers cut from 79 to 74; employee turnover improved to 13.0%; temporary contracts 0.09%. Reserving uses a stochastic Merz and Wüthrich method with an 85th-percentile risk adjustment.
  • Not improving. Internal control weaknesses rose from 28% of evaluated systems (2024) to 37% (2025), with 12 unresolved at year-end; peers typically resolve over 85% within the year and Admiral disclosed none. The board seat has been left vacant. The H1 26 analyst call dropped public Q&A. CEO pay rose about 31% to EUR 718,000 in 2025.
  • Read-through. Execution standards are the investment-relevant ones for the 18-month model, and they are good. The control-weakness trend is the kind of item that becomes a sentiment risk if a reserving or IT incident occurs; it should be carried as a governance watch item, not as a forecast adjustment.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

B403rd of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the negatives are individually modest but they cluster around the same weakness, a company that is pushing volume with incentives weighted to premiums while its controls, disclosure and investor engagement are thin; the most model-relevant negatives are the stalling of the Q2 expense-ratio improvement, home premium deflation and the opacity of reserve releases.

  • Expense ratio. The H1 26 improvement (21.2% to 20.2%) came entirely in Q1 (19.0%, which the CFO said benefited from lower marketing, media, IT and acquisition costs); Q2 was about flat year on year at about 21.4% (derived). The CFO also said technology expenses should rise. The sub-20% target is therefore not yet evidenced on a full-year basis.
  • Home. Premium growth slowed from 6.3% (FY25) to 2.6% (Q1) and about 1.7% (Q2) while policies grew 3.7%, so the average premium is falling; the 86.6% CR (83.9% in Q2) is flattered by benign weather.
  • Reserving opacity. Management lowered the provisioning confidence level from the 95th percentile (stepped up in 2023) back to the 85th; prior-year reserve development is not disclosed. Part of the 2024 to 2026 CR improvement may have come from releases, which cannot be verified.
  • Incentives. Annual targets weight written premiums at 70%; long-term incentives weight premium growth at 40%. These reward volume (see the Wisdom Library's "incentives drive behaviour").
  • Governance. Control weaknesses at 37% of systems; CEO pay +31%; CEO holds only 32,509 shares pending delivery; a related-party commission flow to Bankinter (EUR 4.56m in 2025); the CEO approved participation in a Bankinter subordinated debt issue without full board review; the board seat left vacant.
  • Health. Still loss-making after three years; network switch execution risk.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

B623rd of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: the culture is congruent around one idea, an efficient, digital, direct insurer that passes savings to customers, and the operating system (single site, own repairs, digital service, lean staffing, low prices) fits it well; congruence is weaker in governance and incentives (volume-weighted pay, a CEO who does not face analysts, controlling shareholders on committees) and in the health adjacency.

  • Congruent. Goals (profitable growth), strategy (direct, digital), culture (Top Employer and EFR certifications; turnover 13.0% against Grupo Catalana Occidente's 12.5%; temporary contracts 0.09%; employee share plan with a 5% discount), execution (expense ratio, AI deployment, CAR) and decisions (pricing below the market at the best CR) point the same way. It resembles, in miniature, the Ryanair-type low-cost system: costs low, prices low, volume up.
  • Less congruent. Annual targets weight written premiums at 70% and long-term targets weight premium growth at 40%, which pulls toward volume more than the "margins before growth" message; 30% of the CEO's 2025 variable pay is tied to sustainability and satisfaction metrics; health has been tolerated as a loss-making adjacency; control weaknesses rising do not fit an outlier-standards culture.
  • For the model: the system is coherent enough to keep producing low cost and above-market growth; the incentive tilt argues for assuming volume growth over margin expansion.
C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

A551st of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: the sudden, high-magnitude risks are few and mostly low-probability; the most plausible high-impact one is a claims-severity step (a Baremo or judicial shock combined with energy-led repair inflation) that raises the CR by several points and forces reserve strengthening; operational failure of a single-site, all-digital insurer is the least monitored.

  1. Bodily-injury severity shock (Baremo update above 4%, a judicial reinterpretation, or a Baremo reform like 2016). Plausibility: low to medium over 18 months (Spanish inflation 3.2% to 4.5% in 2026). Size: a 2 to 4 point CR rise plus reserve strengthening on open claims (paid on the payment-year scale); EUR 15m to 40m of net profit, 15% to 35% of FY27 consensus. Precedent: 2023 (Baremo +8.5%, CR 104.1%).
  2. Reserve inadequacy revealed (prior-year releases reverse). Plausibility: low (stochastic reserving, monthly claims committee, external audit; the risk adjustment is still at the 85th percentile). Size: several points of CR in one year and a credibility shock, since releases are not disclosed and the provisioning level was already lowered from the 95th percentile.
  3. Operational or cyber failure at the single site. Plausibility: low. Size: potentially large (all sales, service and claims are digital and centralised at Tres Cantos; DORA compliance and new cyber units mitigate). Reputational damage would hit acquisition.
  4. Price war triggered by Mutua and followed by Mapfre or Allianz. Plausibility: low within 12 months (sector CR near 98%), medium over 24 to 36 months if claims inflation fades. Size: policy growth to near zero or a 2 to 3 point CR rise.
  5. Sovereign or credit spread shock. Plausibility: low to medium (French stress has spread to Italy). Size: +100 basis points of spread costs about 5 points of solvency; +100 basis points of rates about 9.6 points; earnings largely unaffected under IFRS 17 because liability discounting goes through other comprehensive income. Dividend safe from 196%.
  6. Tax audit outcome (2020 to 2022, corporate income tax and VAT). Plausibility: medium that something is assessed; size likely small, unknown.
  7. Catastrophe. Low impact: the Consortium covers about 95% of extreme weather losses; excess-of-loss reinsurance protects the rest.
  8. Health network transition failure (HNA from July 2026). Low impact on group earnings (health is about 3% to 5% of premiums, 50% ceded).
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

F447th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the drivers are highly concentrated: one ratio (the combined ratio) on one line (motor, 81% of premiums) in one country generates about four-fifths of the variance in the next 18 months' earnings, and within that ratio the difference against the last four to six years comes from a single mechanism, the gap between the company's earned price and its claims cost per policy, which in turn is set by two external numbers (Spanish CPI, through the Baremo and parts and labour) and one competitor's choice (Mutua's tariff freeze). The differences in level against FY20 to FY25 (revenue growth of about 7% against 3%; a CR of about 92% against an average of about 97%; a pre-tax margin of about 11% against a range of -0.7% to 19.6%) are not generated by new drivers but by the old ones settling at new values. Concentration of this kind cuts both ways for predictability: it makes the case simple to model and to track (D-05, D-06) and it leaves no diversification when the one driver moves (the 2023 precedent: a 13-point CR move erased all the profit). The two-sided test the Consumer should apply is that the concentration is in a mean-reverting variable (management reprices, competitors reprice, the Baremo is formula-bound) rather than in a trending one, which is why the second set modelled a band rather than a trend.

The differences against history, attributed to drivers (EUR m, approximate; second set's model and FactSet history).

Difference, FY27 base against the FY20 to FY25 average Size Driver generating it Share of the FY27 earnings variance (my estimate)
Revenue growth 7.4% against 2.9% (FY19 to FY24) about +EUR 55m of earned premium a year The price gap to repricing incumbents (share gain of 2 to 3 points a year on a market of 4% to 6%) about 10% (the revenue cone is narrow: FY27 GWP growth 5.5% to 8.5% is about EUR 4m of net income)
CR 92.0% against an average of 96.9% (FY22 to FY25) and about 90% (FY19 to FY21) about EUR 60m of technical result against FY22 to FY25 Earned price against claims cost per policy in motor (Baremo 30% of motor claims cost; parts about 50% of physical damage; labour about 30%); home weather; health about 80% (a 91.0% to 93.8% range is EUR 27m of net income)
Investment result EUR 46.5m against about EUR 40m +EUR 6m Portfolio size (+5% to 6% a year), rental income about 5% (plus or minus EUR 2m)
Expense ratio 20.5% against 21.1% to 22.1% +EUR 7m Fixed-cost leverage retained about 5% (20.0% to 21.0% is EUR 6m pre-tax, but it is partly management's choice, so it is a buffer that reduces variance rather than adds to it)
Tax 25%, share count flat, no interest none none none

Within the CR's 80%, the split for FY27 is roughly 55% motor price against cost (the scissors: about 1 to 1.5 points of motor loss ratio before seasoning), 25% home weather (87% to 96% is plus or minus EUR 8m to 10m of pre-tax profit on a EUR 180m line), 10% health (EUR 2m to 6m), and 10% reserving and large claims (a Q3 25-type EUR 5m event is 0.4 points of annual CR; a Mapfre-type 2 to 3 point reserve addition would be EUR 25m to 38m).

Why the concentration is structural, not a modelling choice. Spain is 99.9% of premiums; motor is 81%; the company holds motor risk net (no quota share); there is no life, no international, no asset management, no debt; the health quota share halves a line that is 3.5% of premiums. The first set (C-79) called the perimeter "very narrow and stable"; the Q3 26 segment table repeats it (motor 81.6% of nine-month GWP). The AlphaSense D2 report's conclusion that "the company's financial trajectory ... will be dictated primarily by a narrow set of motor-related variables rather than diversified earnings streams" is the same finding from 150 sources.

The historical dispersion of the driver (what concentration has cost before). Spanish motor CRs ran 90% to 105% between 2014 and 2023 (Admiral: 96% in 2014, 99% in H1 15, 104% to 105% in 2016; GCO: about 102% in 2023). For Linea Directa, in the year after its written price fell below claims inflation, the motor loss ratio rose 8.3 points (2022, to 77.7%) and the motor CR a further 9.5 points (2023, to 104.5%); the group pre-tax profit swung EUR 186m (FY20 to FY23) on revenue that moved 9%. Against that, 2027 is a different configuration: the price-cost gap is about 1.5 to 2 points, not 8; the starting CR is 91.3%, not about 90% with stale tariffs; the Baremo is 3.3% to 3.5%, not 8.5%; and the company has 1 to 1.5 points of expense buffer it did not have in 2022 (expense ratio then about 22%). The concentration is the same; the amplitude of the driver's expected move is about one-fifth of 2022 to 2023.

The two drivers that are not in the company's hands, and their current state.

  • Spanish inflation. CPI 4.9% in September and October 2026 (core 3.1%); the pension formula has already captured most of it for the 2027 Baremo (3.3% to 3.5%); the 2028 Baremo will follow 2027 inflation (Bank of Spain 3.7%, BBVA 3.2%, CaixaBank 2.5% to above 3.0%). Parts +5.1% and repair +3.7% (September 2026). The driver is elevated but its 2027 value is largely known; its 2028 value is the open question.
  • Mutua's tariff policy. 50% frozen in 2026, 97% CR ceiling, 297% solvency, no stated trigger, a warning that prolonged inflation would pressure margins. The driver is binary-ish (extend or narrow in 2027) and unobservable until Mutua's own announcements (its 2026 results in March 2027; H1 27 in July 2027).

Diffuseness that does exist, and why it matters less. There are many small positives (health, rental income, expense leverage, digital sales, new lines, payout) that the first set (C-20, C-78) and second set (P-03) listed; together they add about EUR 12m to 15m of pre-tax profit by FY28. They are diffuse and individually small, which makes them reliable in aggregate but unable to offset a 2-point CR move (EUR 25m). The case is therefore "one big driver, many small cushions".

For predictability. Concentration lowers the score because a single external step can move earnings 15% to 35% and there is no offset; it is partly redeemed because the driver is identifiable, partly known for 2027, mean-reverting through pricing, and visible each quarter in the segment CRs. I place the score below neutral.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F5616th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy has not changed; the priority has shifted within it, from margin restoration (2023 to 2024) to profitable volume growth using the cost advantage (2025 to 2026); the shift is clearly articulated in behaviour and moderately well resourced; the one genuine change of course is in health.

  • The shift. In 2023 management restricted underwriting and raised prices hard ("we continue to put margins before growth"). Since 2025 it has pursued above-market growth while holding the CR: "we do not need to deteriorate combined ratios to drive commercial volume growth"; "will leverage cost efficiency to offer competitive pricing"; no hard volume targets, but the aim is to outgrow the market. Incentives match: annual targets weight written premiums at 70% and the long-term plan weights premium growth against the sector at 40% and the average CR at 50%.
  • Organisation. Motor and Home were merged into Commercial and Client divisions (2022); a dedicated Risk Area centralises the risk map; health has been sold under the Línea Directa brand since 2023.
  • Health, the real change. In July 2026 the company switched its health provider network to HNA (from a DKV-based network of 51,000 professionals) to improve service and lower claims cost, and continues to shift the book to complete products (68% of the portfolio). Management has not disclosed a corrective plan or a breakeven date beyond an implicit "late 2027".
  • Resourcing. Financially well resourced (no debt, 196% solvency). Technology resourcing is modest in absolute terms (intangible capital expenditure EUR 19.6m in 2024, EUR 8.3m in 2025) relative to Mapfre and Mutua, but proportionate for a digital-native insurer.
  • For the model: strategy implies growth above the market at a held CR, not CR maximisation. Do not model the company harvesting margin through price; model it reinvesting margin in volume.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

C+666th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: guidance has been raised twice in 2026, in words rather than numbers, and the company is ahead of its new ranges; there is no capital markets day and no numerical earnings or premium guidance.

  • Sequence of the CR target. The post-2023 target was 94% to 95% (FY24 delivered 94.7%). In Q3 25 the CFO said a 92% ratio was "too good to be true" on a consistent basis. In February 2026 he said the group was "comfortable" with 92% to 93% over the medium term. In the Q1 26 call (April 2026), after 91.7%, management spoke of being comfortable in the "low 90s". H1 26 delivered 91.1% and Q2 90.6%.

An expense ratio below 20% over the medium term (FY25 21.1%; H1 26 20.2%, but about 21.4% in Q2 on this analyst's derivation); more than 4 million customers (3.86m in June 2026; at about 278,000 net additions a year this is reached around end-2026 or early 2027); net profit above EUR 100m (consensus FY26 EUR 103m); solvency not below 150% with about 180% as the dividend reference.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

D5512th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: it is entering only small, adjacent product markets that share its customers, channel and brand, which is sensible and low-risk; it is not entering new geographies.

  • Products. Pet (2024, with tele-veterinary services), business multi-risk for SMEs and the self-employed (2025), solar-panel protection, personal mobility; together "other" lines grew 57.5% to EUR 3.3m in H1 26. Health (Vivaz) is the larger adjacency, growing 14.7% in 2025 and 17.7% in H1 26, but loss-making.
  • Distribution. A digital distribution agreement with Evo Banco (700,000 digital customers) and home insurance sold through Bankinter (EUR 4.56m of mediation commissions in 2025).
  • Geography. Portugal is residual (EUR 69,000 of premiums in 2025). There is no plan to replicate the model abroad; management says it does not plan mergers or acquisitions.
  • Sensibleness. On the Wisdom Library's "core versus adjacency" ring, these moves are core or close: they draw on the same customers, the same digital channel and the same brand. The risk is not capital (small) but focus and the multiplication of subscale lines that cannot carry their own marketing; none will matter to group earnings before 2028.
  • For the model: treat new lines as about 1% of premiums by 2028, breakeven at best.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

D4810th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: real price rises happened in 2023 to 2024; today the company is pricing below claims inflation and below the market by choice, so current rises are merely nominal; but there is meaningful latent pricing headroom because its price sits several points below the market's while its margin is the best in the market, and management increasingly says it will use it if inflation rises.

  • Real rises (2023 to 2024). The company says it executed the highest average premium increases in the Spanish motor sector and accepted a temporary 4.2% to 4.5% fall in customers; the CR fell from 104.1% (FY23) to 94.7% (FY24).
  • Nominal now (2025 to 2026). Average motor price changes were below 2% in 2025; in Q1 26 average increases were about 2% on new business and renewals against a market average of about 6%; in H1 26 motor GWP rose 9.8% on 8.5% more policies, an average premium rise of about 1.2%. The company "raised premiums to fully pass on the 2.7% Baremo" while the market averaged about 6%. Home is weaker: premiums +2.0% on 3.7% more policies, so the average home premium fell.
  • Latent. The gap to the market (about 4 points) plus a CR 2 to 3 points below Mapfre's and 6 to 7 points below the sector's means the company could raise prices to the market's level without losing its price position. Management's language: "if inflation worsens they will adjust premiums to protect margins"; it expects the Spanish market to keep raising average premiums, "allowing Línea Directa to adjust its own tariffs without accelerating churn".
  • For the model: for 2026 model average motor premium growth of about 1% to 2%. For 2027, if claims severity rises to 4% to 6%, model a lag of two to four quarters before average premium growth rises toward 4% to 5%, with policy growth slowing by 1 to 3 points as a consequence (the 2023 to 2024 precedent).
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

D5010th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: not at all; the company is purely organic and says it does not plan acquisitions; this is neutral for the 18-month model and, given sector deal prices, probably sensible.

  • No acquisitions or disposals since the 2021 listing; management states it "does not plan mergers or acquisitions" and will grow through cross-selling, direct marketing and new products.
  • Context: sector deals are expensive (Mapfre paid 17 times earnings for Safety Insurance; Renta 4 called that well above the 11 to 13 times range), and Linea Directa's model (single site, single brand, own repair network) does not lend itself to integrating branch- or broker-based books.
  • The relevant M&A question is the reverse one: Linea Directa as a target (see C-09), which depends on Cartival and Bankinter.
  • For the model: no acquisitions, no goodwill, no acquisition spend.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

C626th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: shareholder returns are stepping up steadily through dividends, supported by strong organic capital generation; the balance sheet is under-used (no buybacks despite a 10th-percentile multiple and 196% solvency); management talks about capital strength supporting dividends but does not moot buybacks or special dividends.

  • Record. Dividends were suspended in 2023, resumed with a EUR 15m interim in mid-2024, reached EUR 45m for FY25 (53% payout; EUR 0.0414 a share), and the first FY26 interim rose to EUR 18m (EUR 0.016548 a share, paid 7-Jul-26), 20% above the prior first interim.
  • Capacity (derived, approximate). Eligible own funds were EUR 440.4m at 182.6%, so the solvency capital requirement is about EUR 240m. At 196.3% the surplus is roughly EUR 110m over 150% and about EUR 40m over 180%. Net income of about EUR 100m adds about 40 points of solvency a year before dividends; premium growth of 8% to 9% raises the requirement by about EUR 20m a year. A 60% payout therefore roughly holds the ratio flat and a 75% payout lets it drift down by a few points a year from 196%. The company could sustain a payout of 70% to 80% with solvency above 180%.
  • What consensus already assumes. Dividend per share EUR 0.055 (FY26), 0.065 (FY27), 0.069 (FY28), about 58% to 63% of consensus EPS (FactSet dates dividends by payment period, so treat these as approximate). The upside versus consensus is therefore a step to 70% plus, or a buyback, perhaps signalled with FY26 results (late February 2027) after the Solvency II change.
  • Treasury activity is only for employee plans (1.16m shares bought in 2025, 0.11% of capital) and a liquidity contract with Alantra.
  • For the model: payout 55% to 60% in FY26, 60% to 65% thereafter; no buyback in the base case; flat share count of 1,088.4m.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–58

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: several small positives add up to a few percent of earnings each, the largest being health moving toward breakeven, investment income rising with yields and the return of rental income in 2027; none is transformational.

  • Health toward breakeven. CR improved 8.6 points year on year in H1 26 (Q2 124.0%); the HNA network switch (July 2026) and the mix shift to complete products (68%) target lower claims cost; moving from a loss of about EUR 5m to breakeven by 2028 is worth about 4% to 5% of pre-tax profit.
  • Investment income. Net investment income EUR 38.8m to 41.8m in 2025; the financial investment result +5.2% in H1 26; the fixed-income yield (2.61% in 2024, 3.12% cited for Q4 25) rises as a duration of about three years rolls at higher rates. Real estate income fell 94.1% in H1 26 during the Madrid building renovation and resumes in June 2027.
  • Digital loop closure. Over 60% of sales enquiries start online but most close by phone; raising fully online sales from 12% lowers acquisition cost.
  • Loyalty. The "Por Ser" programme (up to 10% of partner purchases credited against renewal premiums) and multi-product holdings support retention.
  • Capital. Solvency II revision and a possible payout step (C-19).
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

D509th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: they have indicated none; the controlling blocks are passive supporters of the current strategy; the only plausible source of change is a decision by Bankinter to exit its stake, which has not been signalled.

  • Cartival (Botín family) increased its stake to 20.75% in mid-2026, a vote for continuity; its chairman chairs Linea Directa and sits on both key committees.
  • Bankinter has kept 17.42% throughout 2024 to 2026 with no board seats; Jefferies previously flagged the stake as a potential technical overhang if Bankinter chose to crystallise value; Deutsche Bank called the stake the main source of volatility in Bankinter's capital. Bankinter's new CEO is a possible trigger for a review but nothing has been said.
  • Cartival is itself a significant Bankinter shareholder, so the two blocks are linked; a sale by Bankinter to a third party would need to be acceptable to the Botín family.
  • No activist and no short campaign exists.
  • For the model: no change in strategy from shareholders; treat a Bankinter sale as a two-sided event (placement discount near term; wider float and a possible change of control premium longer term).
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

F5615th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the positive corporate changes are real, well resourced financially and consistently executed, but narrow: they amount to the continued execution of one programme (direct-model efficiency plus profitable growth), with no new lever comparable to the 2023 to 2024 repricing.

  • Strength. The programme delivered: CR from 104.1% to 91.1% in about three years, record customer growth, a dividend rebuilt from zero to a 53% payout, solvency from about 183% to 196%. Management raised its own targets twice in 2026.
  • Breadth. Narrow: the earnings drivers from here are volume growth, a little more expense leverage, health losses narrowing and higher investment yields. The corporate-change sweep in the July memo found guidance raised, returns up and strategy refocused, but no M&A, no perimeter reduction and no CEO change.
  • Resourcing. Capital is ample and growth is self-funding; human and technology resources are lean (2,411 employees, modest absolute technology spend) and the board is a member short.
  • Missing levers that would broaden it. A capital-return step (payout to 70% plus or a buyback), a quantified health plan, disclosure that lets investors verify the margin's durability (frequency, severity, reserve development), and CEO-led investor engagement.
  • For the model: corporate change supports the base case (growth at a held margin) but does not provide upside beyond it, except through the payout.
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

F4816th of 19

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the staircase is short and low in novelty: the next six months offer milestone confirmations (Q3 and FY26 results, the EUR 100m profit and 4 million customer marks, the dividend declaration after the Solvency II change), but also the most important negative test (the 2027 Baremo update); months 6 to 18 offer health progress and the return of rental income; months 18 to 36 offer health breakeven and digital efficiency; the only step with real re-rating novelty would be a capital-return step or a change in the shareholder structure, neither of which is signalled.

Next 6 months (to April 2027)

  • Q3 26 results, 28-Oct-26. Motor growth and the summer CR (Q3 25 carried high-severity summer claims, so the comparison is easier). Novelty low; well-founded.
  • 2027 Baremo update, around December 2026. A negative risk if above 4%; a relief if near 3%. Novelty medium (it is the market's main debate); well-founded as a dated event.
  • Spanish general election, 29-Nov-26. Little direct effect.
  • Revised Solvency II in force, 30-Jan-27. A small solvency uplift for a short-tail book. Novelty low.
  • FY26 results and final dividend, late February 2027. Net income above EUR 100m (consensus EUR 103m), likely over 4 million customers (3.86m in June 2026, about 278,000 added a year), dividend growth; a possible payout increase toward 60% to 70% (the capacity exists, C-19, but nothing is announced). Novelty: medium if the payout steps up, low otherwise.
  • 2026 AGM (spring 2027). Possible co-optation to fill the vacant board seat.

Months 6 to 18 (to April 2028)

  • Health CR trend under the HNA network (target breakeven late 2027, implicit); rental income from the renovated Madrid building from June 2027; H1 27 results showing whether the CR holds through the 2027 severity step; possible new strategic targets (the 2026 to 2028 incentive plan implies internal targets); a decision by Bankinter on its 17.42% stake (two-sided). Novelty: low to medium; well-founded only for rental income.

Months 18 to 36 (to late 2029)

  • Health breakeven; expense ratio below 20%; fully online sales rising; possible higher payout or buyback; potential consolidation interest in the only listed Spanish pure direct insurer. Novelty: medium; foundation weak (none is dated or committed).

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

D5411th of 19

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

D5411th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: the Spanish motor value chain is moderately supportive for Linea Directa: a concentrated, high-barrier, compulsory market in which the listed incumbents are repricing and the company's cost advantage lets it take share at the best margin in the market; the support is fragile in two named places, the pricing behaviour of Mutua Madrileña and the claims-inflation cycle, and is slowly eroding in a third, distribution (comparison sites, AI agents, incumbents' technology spend).

  • The value chain explains why the base case is growth at a stable margin: Linea Directa wins on cost and price, not on scale or service, so its share gains continue while the market is repricing and slow when it is not.
  • Precedent (C-84) puts the company in the "repricing peaked, margin stable" phase of the motor cycle, which historically delivers earnings growth plus dividends rather than a re-rating, unless margins prove durable through an inflation test or capital returns step up.
  • For the model: market premium growth of 4% to 6%; company policy growth of 5% to 8% in 2026 to 2027 with a price gap to the market of 2 to 4 points; a downside case with the gap closed (competitors stop raising, or the company must raise to meet claims inflation) and policy growth of 2% to 4%.
3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?C627th of 19full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F5515th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the pure direct route is the source of the cost advantage and is helping today; it also confines the company to roughly 30% of the motor market and exposes it to the gatekeepers of digital attention (search engines, comparison sites, and possibly AI agents), which is a slow-moving, medium-fragility constraint.

  • Helping. No broker commissions (Spanish broker commissions about 8.5% of premium; bank channels 25% to 30% or more); a single site; over 90% of interactions digital; 2.4 million monthly digital visits, 2.7 times telephone volume. Linea Directa (with Mutua) transacts mostly on its own websites rather than through aggregators.
  • Hindering. Brokers and agents control about 70% of Spanish premiums; experts say direct and aggregator penetration in Spanish motor is capping out at 27% to 30%. Customer acquisition requires heavy mass-media and search spend (about EUR 50m a year; a top-ten Spanish advertiser) whose cost rises with search-engine prices. Most digital originations still close by telephone (only 12% fully online in Q1 26). The Bankinter and Evo Banco agreements are small.
  • Fragility. The 30% ceiling is a constraint on long-run share, not on the next 18 months: at roughly a quarter of the direct channel, Linea Directa can keep growing by taking share within it and as the channel grows (7% a year in Europe against 4% overall). The bigger fragility is the cost of attention: if AI agents or comparison sites become the default front door, acquisition costs and churn rise for all direct insurers.
  • For the model: hold marketing at about 4% to 5% of premiums; do not assume acquisition-cost leverage.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

C+586th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: on balance competitors' behaviour helps: the large listed incumbents are raising prices and shedding volume, which hands share to Linea Directa; Mutua's freeze hurts by narrowing the price gap; the company's position is robust on margin and fragile on growth.

  • Helping. Mapfre shedding fleets and raising average motor prices (8.4% in 2025, about 6% in Q1 26); Allianz Spain +9.6% on renewals; Admiral Seguros moving toward brokers and higher-margin segments (direct channels below a 90% CR but customers flat); Grupo Catalana Occidente repricing.
  • Hurting. Mutua freezing prices for half its auto book and growing 13%; Mutua's Akur8 pricing models and EUR 80m AI budget narrow Linea Directa's pricing-agility edge; Mutua's Reynasa purchase lowers its parts cost; Mapfre expanding offices (300 new) and the Carrefour partnership (10 million loyalty members) and Santander bancassurance compete for the same price-sensitive customers.
  • Fragility. Linea Directa's motor CR (91.1%) is 2.6 points better than Mapfre's Iberia auto (93.7%) and about 6 points better than Mutua's target (97%), so it can match any rational competitor on price and still earn a better margin. Its growth, not its margin, is what is exposed: the growth depends on the gap between its prices and theirs.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?D4812th of 19full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

F4516th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption acts most on the demand and distribution end (customers and the interface that reaches them), where it lowers switching costs and could raise acquisition costs; it also acts on competitors (closing the technology gap) and on suppliers (electric-vehicle repair complexity, parts integration); it leaves Linea Directa's cost advantage intact near term but its growth model moderately fragile over three to five years.

  • Demand and distribution. Price comparison and AI agents lower switching costs and could make the insurer a commodity supplier behind an interface it does not own (the Wisdom Library's "unbundle, capture, rebundle" pattern, with Expedia "working for Google"). Linea Directa's own-website model resists this best among Spanish insurers, but its brand-based retention would weaken.
  • Competitors. Mutua's Akur8 pricing modules and synthetic data, Mapfre's 126 AI solutions in Spain and over EUR 1bn of annual technology spend, AXA's Photon rating engine: incumbents are closing the pricing-agility and cost gap that a digital-native insurer once owned. The Wisdom Library's "AI claims are operating basics, not moats" applies: Linea Directa's AI wins are necessary, not differentiating.
  • Suppliers. Electric-vehicle and driver-assistance repair complexity raises severity (repairs 15% to 24% dearer); standardised estimating software (Audatex) helps contain parts cost; Mutua's vertical integration into parts distribution gives it a severity edge.
  • Regulators. The AI Act constrains automated underwriting and claims decisions (logging, human review), a small cost.
  • Net. The fragility is in growth and retention, not in margin, and it is a three-to-five-year issue.

3b

Industry structure
