Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)D5411th of 19full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

D5213th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Linea Directa is the leading pure direct insurer in Spain but a mid-sized player in its core motor market (fifth or sixth, about 6.5% to 7% share), roughly 10 to 12 points behind the two leaders; in home and health it is subscale.

  • Motor. EUR 924m of 2025 motor premiums in a EUR 14.29bn market is about 6.5% (Jefferies cites about 7%, fifth; the annual report says sixth). The leaders are Mutua Madrileña (18.6% of auto by late 2025, having overtaken Mapfre for the first time) and Mapfre (about 17% to 19%), followed by Allianz (about 13% on Jefferies' older numbers) and AXA (about 9%).
  • Home. 13th, about 2.5% share (EUR 167.8m in a EUR 6.45bn market).
  • Health. About 0.3% share (EUR 38.8m in EUR 11.97bn), 21st.
  • Group. 13th among Spanish non-life groups; non-life share about 2.3%.
  • Implication. Linea Directa does not have the scale of the leaders and will not reach it organically within the model horizon (gaining roughly 0.2 to 0.3 points of motor share a year). Its advantage is cost structure, not size (C-41, C-42). Its position within the direct channel (about 30% of motor volumes) is larger: roughly a quarter of that channel.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

D5612th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: concentrated enough to support rational pricing (top five non-life groups 48.3% in 2024; motor top three about 45% to 50%), but not steep: the distribution flattens quickly after the top two, and the long tail of mutuals, bancassurers and direct players keeps competition alive.

  • Top five non-life groups held 48.3% of the Spanish market in 2024 (Mutua 16.1%, Mapfre 13.3%, Allianz 6.8%, and others; Grupo Catalana Occidente is eighth at 5.8%). The motor market's Herfindahl index is 651, against 979 in Italy and 209 in the UK; Spain sits between the fragmented UK and the oligopolistic Italian triopoly.
  • Steepness: the files do not give shares for numbers six to ten precisely, but with Grupo Catalana Occidente eighth at 5.8% and Linea Directa around 2.3% of non-life, numbers six to ten plausibly hold about 15% to 20%; the top five are therefore about 2.5 to 3 times the next five, well short of the 5 times benchmark.
  • In motor, two players hold about 35% and a third about 13%; behind them sit AXA, Generali, Catalana Occidente, Linea Directa, Reale, Admiral and others with single-digit shares.
  • Implication. Pricing discipline depends on the behaviour of the two leaders. When both raise prices (2024 to early 2025), the market follows; when one of them chooses retention (Mutua since 2025), discipline weakens at the margin. The structure is supportive but leader-dependent.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

C-627th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: shares are stable and slowly concentrating at the top; new entrants have taken negligible share; the recent movement is a reshuffling among incumbents (Mutua and Linea Directa up, Mapfre down) driven by pricing choices.

  • Top-five concentration crept up from 45.0% (2014) to 48.3% (2024).
  • Mutua overtook Mapfre in auto in 2025 and raised its non-life share to 16.8% in H1 26; Mapfre's non-life share slipped from 13.7% (2024) to 13.3% (2025) as it shed unprofitable fleets (Iberia auto premiums +1.7% in H1 26, Spanish insured vehicles -0.7% year to date).
  • Linea Directa lost customers in the 2023 to 2024 repricing (about 4.2% to 4.5%) and then gained a record 290,400 in 2025, so its share is rising again.
  • New entrants: Admiral Seguros has been in Spain since 2006 and has only 0.46m customers and losses; insurtechs (Prima from Italy, LeoCare from France, Tuio) are small; no new branch-based or telesales entrant was found. Regulatory barriers (Solvency II capital, the DGSFP licensing regime, DORA) are high.
  • Implication. Share shifts in Spanish motor come from relative pricing between incumbents, not from entry. This is good for predictability: a share model can be built on the price gap between Linea Directa and the market.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D6210th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: share consolidates slowly toward the largest groups and, in this cycle, toward the two most advantaged on price: Mutua (advantaged by its mutual status, which lets it accept a 97% CR) and Linea Directa (advantaged by its cost structure); Linea Directa is among the advantaged on cost but not among the largest.

  • The structural pull toward size is weak and slow (three points of top-five share in ten years). The cyclical pull toward cost advantage is stronger: in a repricing cycle, the insurer with the lowest expense ratio can price lowest at an acceptable margin. Linea Directa's expense ratio (20.2% in H1 26; about 16% in auto per JB Capital, against about 20% for the main competitors) and Mapfre's 27.8% show the gap.
  • JB Capital's David Barma estimates Linea Directa can price about 10% below the industry average while keeping its profitability; it is taking share at roughly 2 to 3 times the market's growth rate.
  • The countervailing advantage of size is technology and data spend (Mapfre over EUR 1bn a year; Mutua EUR 80m for AI in 2025 to 2026), physical distribution (Mapfre's 3,100 offices; brokers control about 70% of the market) and parts procurement (Mutua's Reynasa).
  • Implication. Linea Directa is a consolidator of share within the direct, price-sensitive segment, and will keep gaining as long as its cost gap persists. It is not positioned to consolidate the intermediated 70% of the market.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

D5413th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the listed incumbents put margin first today; the market leader by auto share (Mutua) puts retention and volume first and is explicitly willing to accept lower returns; history shows Spanish motor prices did not rise for years despite claims inflation, so the industry has a record of tolerating poor returns.

  • Margin first. Mapfre: "never promise to be the cheapest", refuses price wars, shed 170,000 to 180,000 low-profit fleet policies, Iberia auto CR from 98.5% to 93.7% in a year. Allianz Spain: 9.6% renewal increases, CR 96.4% in 2025. Grupo Catalana Occidente: motor +9.6% with a CR of 94.7%. Admiral Seguros: pricing for profit, customers flat.
  • Volume and retention first. Mutua: freezes tariffs for up to 50% of auto policyholders, targets a CR of about 97% "rather than passing rising costs entirely to policyholders", 90% retention, 13.17% auto growth. As a mutual it answers to policyholders, not shareholders, which makes this stance durable rather than tactical.
  • History. Spanish average motor premiums in 2022 were broadly at 2015 levels despite years of claims inflation; the industry motor CR reached about 100% in 2023. The industry tolerated losses for a long time before repricing, which is evidence of weak, slow-moving discipline.
  • Do prices fall when volume falls? In 2025 to 2026 Mapfre accepted falling volume rather than cutting price, which is the disciplined answer. Mutua's response to its own volume is to hold prices flat rather than cut, which is aggressive but not destructive.
  • Implication. The industry is benign today because it is still repairing returns (sector CR about 98%); the attitude is margin-first by default but with a large, non-shareholder actor that will not follow price rises.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D4512th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years; do competitors show unpredictable behaviour, and would they launch a price attack if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: the disciplined attitude is recent (since 2023) and therefore unproven through a full cycle; Mutua's behaviour has changed within the last eighteen months from following the market to freezing prices, and it widened that freeze twice; a deliberate price attack is unlikely while industry returns are thin, but the attitude is fragile if claims inflation fades and margins recover.

  • Change over 18 to 36 months. 2023: industry-wide repricing after losses. 2024 to 2025: discipline restored, sector motor CR down to 98.4%. 2025 to 2026: Mutua freezes 30%, then over 40%, then 50% of auto policyholders; Mapfre continues to price up. The direction of change in attitude is toward more competition, driven by one actor.
  • Unpredictability. Mutua is situation-dependent (its freeze grows as its margin allows) but transparent: it publicly announces its policy and its 97% CR ceiling. That is predictable aggression, not erratic behaviour.
  • Price attack risk. The main risk scenario is not an attack on Linea Directa's weakness but a softening once claims inflation eases and the sector CR falls below 95%, when the incumbents might chase volume back. UK motor showed this pattern: rate cuts in 2025 (market pricing fell about 10%) followed by industry losses (expected CR 106% to 111% in 2026). The Wisdom Library's "money war" model applies only if a well-funded player prices below cost to gain share; Mutua is pricing at a thin profit, not below cost.
  • Implication for the 18-month model. Keep the base case of rational pricing, but carry a scenario in which the price gap that drives Linea Directa's growth narrows by 2 to 3 points (competitors stop raising), cutting its policy growth to about 3% to 4%.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

D5412th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: moderately supportive: concentrated enough for rational pricing, protected by high entry barriers and a compulsory product, with a slow-moving share structure; the support is fragile in one specific way, its dependence on the pricing behaviour of a mutual leader that has chosen retention over margin and on the claims cycle staying in the "repair" phase.

  • Supportive elements. Top five 48% (C-25); negligible entry (C-26); compulsory motor cover; catastrophe losses absorbed by the Consortium; disciplined listed incumbents (C-28); sector returns still being repaired, so the incentive to compete on price is low.
  • Fragile elements. A history of tolerating poor returns (2015 to 2022); Mutua's widening freeze; the direct channel's exposure to comparison and AI shopping, which lowers switching costs; consolidation (Aviva and Direct Line in the UK) showing that subscale direct players can be squeezed.
  • Net for Linea Directa. Its cost position makes it a winner in a disciplined market and a survivor in a soft market (it can price lower than others at a profit), so the structure's fragility hurts its growth more than its solvency. The industry would need to fall back to a sector CR near 100% for Linea Directa's own CR to be pushed above about 95%.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?C+606th of 19full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

A662nd of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitutes are a minor threat today; motor cover is compulsory, home cover is usually tied to mortgages, and the only live substitution is trading down to minimum cover; the long-term substitutes (autonomous and embedded insurance) are slowly strengthening.

  • Motor: third-party liability is legally required; the substitute is downgrading from comprehensive to third-party cover when budgets are squeezed, which lowers the average premium rather than the policy count. The demand for comprehensive cover is still growing in peers (Unipol's comprehensive +8.2%).
  • Home: banks bundle home insurance with mortgages, a competing channel rather than a substitute.
  • Health: the public system is the substitute; its waiting lists drive private-insurance growth (sector +12% in 2025), so the substitute is currently pushing demand toward the product.
  • Long term: autonomous vehicles could cut accident frequency by up to 90% (Swiss Re studies) and shrink the personal motor pool (Morgan Stanley's illustrative 30% erosion by 2050); manufacturer-embedded insurance.
  • For the model: no substitution effect before 2029 beyond some mix trading down in a downturn.

Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D589th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: Linea Directa's competitive advantage is real, narrow and cost-based: a structurally lower expense base than any large Spanish competitor, shared with customers through lower prices, which wins share in a price-sensitive, shoppable market; it is not a fortress, because switching costs are low, the softer advantages (data, agility, repair integration) are narrowing as incumbents digitise, and pricing power is relative to the market.

  • The advantage supports the base case of share gains at a stable, market-leading margin and protects solvency in a soft market (the company can price lower than others at a profit).
  • It does not support a case for margin expansion through price, nor a premium multiple on moat grounds (Admiral, with a stronger version of the same advantage plus capital-light reinsurance, trades at 13 times and was de-rated 45%).
  • For the model: keep a 6 to 8 point expense-ratio advantage to the incumbents through 2028, a CR advantage of 2 to 3 points to Mapfre's Spanish motor, and do not assume the gap widens.
C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

C-628th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Linea Directa has one strong, durable advantage (a structurally lower cost base from its branchless, brokerless, single-site direct model) and several softer ones (brand, pricing agility from proprietary data, an owned repair network); the cost advantage is stable, the soft advantages are narrowing as incumbents digitise; there is no customer lock-in.

  • Production and scale-based, cost (strong, stable). Expense ratio 20.2% (H1 26) against Mapfre's Iberian non-life 27.8% (a 7.6-point gap) and about 16% against about 20% for the main competitors in auto (JB Capital). It lets the company price about 10% below the industry average at an acceptable margin. It is structural (no branches or brokers to remove) and the gap has held through the cycle. Peers cannot copy it without abandoning their distribution.
  • Customer-related, brand (moderate, stable). A top-ten Spanish advertiser and one of the most recognised brands; it lowers the cost of cross-selling home and health into 2.85m motor customers. It is a search fee more than a loyalty asset (the Wisdom Library's "when brand is just a search fee").
  • Culture and knowledge, pricing agility and data (moderate, narrowing). Individual risk-based pricing from proprietary claims databases, adjusted faster than broker-based peers; it allowed the fastest repricing in the sector in 2023 to 2024. Narrowing because Mutua (Akur8 pricing, EUR 80m AI budget), Mapfre (over EUR 1bn of annual technology spend, 126 AI solutions in Spain) and AXA are closing the gap.
  • Production, vertical integration in repair (moderate, under-used). CAR plus over 1,000 partner bodyshops; a claimed EUR 200 per-claim saving; but redirection fell from 71% to 50% (2021 to 2022) and Mutua is integrating into parts distribution.
  • Regulatory (sector-wide, not relative). Solvency II and licensing protect all incumbents equally.
  • Switching costs (weak). Annual contracts, comparison shopping, NPS of 36.83 against Admiral's 50-plus; the "Por Ser" loyalty programme helps at the margin.
  • Net. A Level-3-type advantage (operating performance from a structurally different cost position), not a fortress. It is sufficient to win share in a disciplined market and to survive a soft one.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

D5012th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Linea Directa is small against the leaders (roughly one-tenth of Mapfre's Iberian premiums, a third to a half of Mutua's non-life premiums), but it is the scale leader within the Spanish direct channel; its scale confers single-site fixed-cost absorption, efficient brand spend and a large motor database, but not technology, procurement or distribution scale.

  • Size. GWP EUR 1.13bn (FY25); 3.86m policyholders; 2,411 average employees (2,120 on another count) against Grupo Catalana Occidente's 8,706; Mapfre Iberia about EUR 6bn of premiums a half (Spain over EUR 5.7bn); Mutua non-life EUR 4.61bn in H1 26; Allianz group EUR 186.9bn.
  • What it confers. (1) Fixed-cost leverage: one site and one platform for 3.86m customers; the expense ratio fell from 22.1% (FY24) to 21.1% (FY25) with headcount down. (2) Brand efficiency: national advertising amortised over a large motor book. (3) Data: 2.85m motor risks for pricing. (4) Capital self-sufficiency at its size.
  • What it does not confer. Absolute technology budget (the "100 times" gap to Mapfre's cumulative spend is overstated because much of Linea Directa's technology is expensed, but the absolute gap is real), parts purchasing (Mutua's Reynasa), physical reach (Mapfre's 3,100 offices), reinsurance in-house (Mapfre Re).
  • Implication. Scale is enough for the model's economics but not a widening advantage; Deutsche Bank's key success factor ("scale-driven procurement savings in claims repair determine which Spanish operators maintain a CR below 93%") is one the company meets through CAR rather than size.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

D6011th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: on price the company is demonstrably not inferior and probably leading; on service and claims experience it is not inferior to the incumbents but is behind Mutua and well behind the best international direct insurers; its surplus utility is therefore price-led, which is valuable but less sticky than service-led utility.

  • Price. Pricing about 10% below the industry average (JB Capital), average 2026 increases of about 2% against a market at about 6%; repair cost per claim claimed EUR 200 below the market average; record acquisition (290,400 in 2025) in a market where households are shopping.
  • Service. Over 90% digital interactions, 65.7% digital roadside requests, AI chat resolving 64% of queries, 100% digital repair centres with online scheduling of repairs and replacement cars, and processing times cut about 30%. NPS rose 5.79 points to 36.83 but lags Admiral's group average of over 50; Mutua ranks first for customer satisfaction and value for money in the Stiga index for Spanish auto insurance; retention is about 86% against Mutua's 90% and Occident's 87.8%.
  • Inferiority tests (to disprove). It is not inferior on price, digital service or claims speed; the evidence that customers stay after price rises (the 2024 recovery from a 4.5% fall) suggests the offer is good enough. The gap to Mutua on satisfaction is the main caveat.
  • For the model: price-led utility supports share gains while the price gap persists; do not assume retention improvements without disclosure.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

C+555th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: there is demonstrated, moderate pricing power: in 2023 to 2024 the company applied the sector's largest motor increases, lost about 4.2% to 4.5% of customers temporarily and recovered them within about a year, restoring the CR by about 10 points; but it is a direct insurer in a shoppable market, so its pricing power is relative (it can price up with the market, not against it), and it is currently choosing not to use it.

  • Applied. 2023: underwriting restricted and prices raised individually; CR 104.1% to 94.7% (FY24) to 92.6% (FY25); customers fell about 4.2% (2023 underwriting year) and 4.5% (early 2024), then grew 8.5% in 2025. Management asserts churn stayed better than Spanish non-life peers during the increases.
  • Limits. The Risks report's diagnostic ("average motor premium expansion of under 2% is inconsistent with structural pricing power") and the Business Foundations report's view that aggregator exposure caps pass-through during high claims inflation are fair: in 2022 the company priced late (combined ratio 96.3%, then a 2023 loss), which shows it does not lead the market up. Home average premiums are falling now.
  • Now. Pricing about 2% (motor) against a market of about 6%, so the company holds unused headroom (C-17).
  • For the model: pricing power is best modelled as "can follow the market within two to four quarters at a churn cost of about 3 to 5 points of policy growth", not as the ability to raise prices above claims inflation at will.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

A702nd of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: strongly and explicitly: management states that the direct model lets it pass operational savings to customers, it prices below the market, and it says it will use cost efficiency to offer competitive prices rather than weaken its combined ratio; this is the core of its strategy and is visible in its numbers.

  • Stated. "This centralised structure allows management to pass operational savings directly to customers" (CFO, Q1 26); "leveraging digital efficiency to pass competitive prices to policyholders" (CEO reorganisation, 2022); "will leverage cost efficiency to offer competitive pricing" rather than weaken the CR to drive retention.
  • De facto. Average price increases of about 2% against the market's about 6% while holding the best CR in the market; JB Capital's estimate of pricing about 10% below the industry average; AI claims savings rebated into prices (a pattern also cited at Allianz).
  • Why it matters. It is the Wisdom Library's "scale economies shared" pattern: lower costs fund lower prices, which bring volume, which lowers unit costs further. It favours volume growth over margin expansion in the model and is a reinforcing loop (see C-47).
  • Caveat. Sharing works only while the cost gap is maintained; if incumbents close it with technology, the company must choose between price and margin.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

D5511th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: some mechanical forces work in the company's favour (fixed-cost absorption on one site, digital migration of customers, accumulating claims data, and a cost-led share-gain loop), but the underwriting margin, which drives most of the earnings, depends on repeated active choices (pricing against claims inflation, reserving, competitive positioning); the Business Foundations report's verdict of "continuous heroic management intervention rather than mechanical compounding" is right for the margin and too harsh for the cost base.

  • Mechanical. (1) Expense ratio falls as premiums grow on a largely fixed single-site base: the 2-point fall in Q1 26 (partly seasonal) and 1 point in FY25 show it; roughly 1 point of expense ratio per 10% premium growth if half the cost base is fixed. (2) Digital migration lowers service cost each year (digital users up to 92%; app users +15.4% in 2025). (3) Claims data accumulates with 2.85m motor risks. (4) The loop of lower cost to lower price to more volume to lower cost (C-46).
  • Heroic. The CR depends on pricing ahead of the Baremo and repair inflation, on risk selection and on reserving judgement; the company failed this test in 2022 to 2023 and passed it in 2023 to 2025. Each inflation cycle requires management action.
  • Against peers. The mechanical cost advantage is maintained, not widened, because incumbents are investing to close it.
  • For the model: assume mechanical expense leverage of about 0.3 to 0.5 points a year at 7% to 9% growth, partly reinvested in prices and technology; assume no mechanical improvement in the loss ratio.

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

D5811th of 19

Judgement: the strategy is smart and right for the business (exploit the cost advantage, grow organically, digitise, cross-sell), and it proved dynamic in 2023 when it switched hard to margin; it is not bold, it does not widen the moat against incumbents' technology spend, and its one diversification bet (health) is weakly executed.

  • Right. The direct model is the moat; the strategy is built entirely around it (pricing below the market, AI in service and claims, owned repairs). It works in a disciplined market (share gains) and survives a soft one (lowest cost).

  • Range of environments. Strong in high-inflation repricing markets (once it reacts) and in disciplined markets; weaker if price comparison and AI agents erode brand-led retention.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

D5012th of 19

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement: it is a prudent and disciplined allocator (organic growth at about 23% returns, no value-destroying M&A, capital rebuilt before distributions), but not an enlightened or dynamic one: it does not buy back shares even at a 10th-percentile multiple with 196% solvency, it has tolerated a loss-making health adjacency, and its distribution policy is a conventional dividend.

  • Good. Growth capital earns about the group ROE (C-50, C-51); no acquisitions at the elevated prices seen in the sector; capital expenditure small and falling; dividend suspended when capital was at risk, then restored and grown (EUR 15m interim in 2024; EUR 45m for FY25; EUR 18m first FY26 interim).
  • Not dynamic. Treasury purchases only for employee plans (1,157,198 shares in 2025 at EUR 1.13, 0.11% of capital); a liquidity contract rather than a buyback; no special dividend despite surplus capital of roughly EUR 110m over the 150% floor; health losses funded for three years without a disclosed plan.
  • Opportunity. At 11 times earnings (an earnings yield of about 9% against a bond yield of about 3%), a buyback of EUR 50m, about 3.7% of the market value, would lift EPS by roughly 2% to 3% after the investment income lost on the cash; the Solvency II revision in January 2027 and FY26 results in February 2027 are the natural moment for a capital policy update. Nothing in the files indicates one is planned.

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

B634th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: economically, Linea Directa is a high-return (ROE about 23%), self-funding, debt-free, simple insurer whose earnings are recovering toward, but not above, their pre-2022 level; EPS growth of about 20% in 2026 gives way to about 7% to 10% a year from 2027, driven by volume at a broadly stable CR, with most earnings distributed; the main weaknesses are cyclicality of the CR and opacity on reserve releases.

  • What the model should look like. Earned premiums +9% to 10% (FY26), +7% to 8% (FY27), +6% to 8% (FY28); CR 91% to 91.5% (FY26) and 91% to 92% (FY27 to FY28); investment income about EUR 42m rising to EUR 46m to 50m; tax 25%; flat share count; payout 55% to 65%. That lands near consensus (net income EUR 103m, 114m, 122m).
  • Where the economics could surprise. Upside from a payout step and from the CR staying near 90.6% (Q2 run-rate); downside from a 2 to 3 point CR rise in 2027 if claims inflation steps up before prices follow.
  • Valuation read-across. At 11 times forward earnings and about 3 times book for a 23% ROE with 7% to 10% growth and a 4% to 5% yield, the economics justify a higher multiple if the CR proves stable through the 2027 inflation test.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

C607th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is scope to deploy capital at about the current ROE (above 20%) in organic Spanish growth for three to five years, but the capital needed is small, so most earnings must be distributed; the business is a high-return, self-funding, low-reinvestment compounder, which justifies a mid-teens multiple in a stable cycle, above today's 11 times, but not a growth-stock multiple.

  • Capital need. Growth of 8% to 9% raises the solvency requirement by about EUR 20m a year and needs about EUR 36m to 40m of own funds to keep the ratio at 180% to 196% (derived), against net income of about EUR 100m. Retained earnings of 40% are enough; capital expenditure is about EUR 12m a year (FY25, fixed assets and intangibles).
  • Return on that capital. Incremental premiums of about EUR 100m a year at a CR of about 91% plus investment income on float earn roughly EUR 9m to 10m net, about 23% to 27% on the extra capital, similar to the average ROE (22.9% FY25).
  • Operating spend. Marketing (about EUR 50m) and technology spend buy customers at an acceptable lifetime value while the price gap persists; health and new lines have earned negative returns so far.
  • Multiple. A justified price-to-book of (ROE - g) / (cost of equity - g), with ROE 23%, growth 4% to 5% and cost of equity 9% to 10%, gives about 3.2 to 4.5 times, equivalently roughly 14 to 19 times current earnings; even the low end is above today's about 3.0 times FY26 book (EUR 0.41 a share) and 11 times forward earnings. The discount reflects the market's doubt about the durability of the 23% ROE (that is, of the CR), not a lack of reinvestment; a fair multiple on a normalised CR of 92% to 93% is about 12 to 14 times.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

B+603rd of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How clean is the earnings base: the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; one-offs or capitalised costs; tax rate or other non-obvious leakage?

Judgement: the earnings base is clean on the usual tests (no adjustments, normal tax, negligible capitalisation, no acquisitions) but opaque on the one test that matters most for an insurer, the contribution of prior-year reserve releases, which is not disclosed; treat earnings as good quality with an unquantified reserve-release component.

  • Clean. Reported and FactSet-adjusted EPS are identical (0.079 FY25); tax rate 24.9% (FY25), 22.9% (FY24), consistent with Spain's 25%; capital expenditure and capitalised software are small; no goodwill; one-offs are minor (an Atos bond impairment of EUR 1.3m; the temporary loss of rental income during the Madrid building renovation until June 2027, which depresses rather than flatters earnings); foreign-exchange effects about EUR 1m.
  • Opaque. (1) The provisioning confidence level was raised to the 95th percentile in 2023 and brought back to the 85th, which released prudence into 2024 to 2025 results; the size is not disclosed. (2) Prior-year reserve development by line is not published; management said technical teams set opening provisions "to ensure favourable reserve releases in future quarters". (3) Technical provisions under IFRS differ from the Solvency II balance sheet by over EUR 298m. (4) Alternative performance measures are unaudited. (5) IFRS 17 discounting runs through other comprehensive income, so rate moves do not hit the income statement but do move equity.
  • Other leakage. Tax audits for 2020 to 2022 (corporate income tax and VAT) are open. Internal control weaknesses rose to 37% of evaluated systems.
  • Context. Admiral's H1 26 beat rested on reserve releases of 17.3% of premium against 12.0% expected; reserve-release dependence is the sector's known earnings-quality trap and is on the Insurance Sector Digest's "check before buying" list.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

C+725th of 19

Judgement: there is no financial debt and gearing does not amplify EPS; the relevant leverage is insurance leverage (premiums and investments relative to equity), which is moderate and comfortably covered by a 196% solvency ratio; the balance sheet is conservative and offers neither EPS gearing nor material risk.

Under IFRS 17, rate effects on liabilities go through other comprehensive income.

Pricing power

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

D469th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price will contribute little to revenue in 2026 (about 1% to 1.5% of the 8.3% written growth) and a rising share from 2027 (about 2% of 7.0% growth in FY27 and about 3% of 6.6% in FY28), as the company raises motor prices with a two-to-four-quarter lag toward a market that is rising 5% to 6%; but price is a net drag on margin through H2 27, because the earned price in 2027 is set by the 1% to 2% written in 2026 while claims cost per policy rises about 3.5% to 4.5%, and it becomes a small margin tailwind (about 0.3 points) in FY28. The insight that matters for the cone of outcomes is that the company's pricing power is relative and self-correcting in an inflation step: a mutual competitor targeting a 97% CR cannot keep freezing prices if claims inflation rises, so the market umbrella widens just when Linea Directa needs it; the dangerous condition for its pricing is not inflation but a soft market after margins recover (2028 to 2030), as the UK showed in 2025.

What price is doing now (evidence).

  • Motor average written premium: H1 26 motor GWP +9.8% on policies +8.5%, an average premium rise of about 1.2%; the company cited about 2% on new business and renewals in Q1 26 against a market of about 6%. In FY25 motor GWP rose 11.8% on policies +8.5%, so about +3%. The written price has therefore slowed from about 3% (2025) to about 1% to 2% (2026).
  • Home: H1 26 premiums +2.0% on policies +3.7%, an average premium fall of about 1.6%, after a rise of about 3.9% in 2024; promotional acquisition pricing.
  • Health: average premium rising with the mix shift to complete products.
  • Market: Mapfre Iberia raised average motor premiums 4.3% in H1 26 (6% in Q1 26); AXA Spain personal lines +6.0% in H1 26; Allianz Spain high single digits on renewals; Grupo Catalana Occidente auto +6.9% in H1 26 at a 92.8% motor CR. Mutua froze tariffs for 50% of its auto customers in 2026 and raised its motor CR ceiling from 95% to 97%.

The margin arithmetic of price against cost (my derivation). Because earned premium lags written by about six months, the motor loss ratio in a year moves with (earned price) minus (claims cost per policy).

2025 2026 2027 (base) 2028 (base)
Written motor average premium change about +3% about +1.5% about +2.5% (repricing from H1 27) about +3%
Earned average premium change (lags written) about +3% about +2.5% about +2% about +3%
Claims cost per policy (frequency times severity) about +2.5% (implied by a flat motor loss ratio) about +3.5% to +4.5% about +3%
Effect on the motor loss ratio improving flat (72.9% against 72.8%) about +1 to +1.5 points before selection and mix, about +0.5 net about -0.3
  • The 2027 claims-cost estimate: bodily injury is about 30% of motor claims cost and the Baremo is revalued with pensions, which follow average consumer inflation; with Spanish inflation at 3.2% to 4.5% in 2026, a 2027 update of about 3.5% (against 2.7% for 2026) is the central expectation, and it also raises open claims because settlements use the scale of the payment year. Parts and labour are rising with energy-led inflation (Brent about USD 111); electric-vehicle repairs cost 15% to 24% more. Frequency has been benign and I assume it flat.
  • Net of the seasoning of a slower-growing book (new business runs a higher loss ratio than renewals; P-05) and continued digital claims savings, I put the motor CR at about 91.8% in FY27 against about 91.3% in FY26.

How price contributes to revenue (base).

FY26E FY27E FY28E FY29E FY30E
GWP growth +8.3% +7.0% +6.6% +5.8% +5.4%
of which policies (average count) about +7.3% about +4.8% about +3.6% about +3.4% about +3.2%
of which average premium about +1.0% about +2.1% about +2.9% about +2.3% about +2.1%
Policyholders at year end (m) about 3.99 about 4.17 about 4.32 about 4.47 about 4.61
  • The repricing slows policy growth: the 2023 to 2024 precedent cost 4.2% to 4.5% of customers when the company applied the market's largest increases; this time the increase is smaller (to about 3% to 4% written in motor, still below the market's 5% to 6%), so the cost is slower growth (net additions falling from about 278,000 a year to about 180,000 to 200,000), not a fall. The price gap to the market narrows but stays positive.
  • This is the reason revenue is predictable: price and volume trade off against each other, so a faster repricing gives more price and less volume and leaves written growth in a band of about 6% to 8% either way. The Consumer should carry revenue with a narrow cone (FY27 GWP growth 5.5% to 8.5%) and the CR with a wide one.

Why inflation is not the worst condition for Linea Directa's pricing.

  • Mutua's 97% CR ceiling leaves it about 1 to 2 points of room; it warned in April 2026 that a prolonged Middle East conflict and energy-led inflation would squeeze insurers' margins. A 3% to 4% severity step would push it above its ceiling, so the freeze is likely to narrow, and Mapfre (which "never promises to be the cheapest") and the others are already pricing up. The umbrella therefore widens in an inflation step, which lets Linea Directa raise prices without losing its relative position. This is the reverse of 2022, when the whole market priced late.
  • The worse condition is a soft market: once the sector motor CR (about 98% in 2025) falls toward 95%, the incumbents can stop raising prices and chase volume back (Mapfre has said it will focus on growth once margins normalise). Then the umbrella closes from above, Linea Directa's growth slows to 3% to 4% and its CR is not helped. The UK showed this sequence: rate cuts in 2025 after the 2023 to 2024 recovery, then expected losses (Jefferies forecasts a 107% UK motor CR in 2026). In the model this is why the CR drifts up to 92.2% by FY30 and growth slows to 5.4%.
  • A regulatory wildcard: a UK-style ban on renewal price walking has not been proposed in Spain; I do not model it.

Home price. The falling home average premium is a genuine weakness (home growth about 2% against Mutua's +8.6% and Occident's multi-risk +9.4%); I model home GWP +2.5% (FY26), +3.5% (FY27), +4% (FY28) as the company stops discounting, with the home CR normalising toward the CFO's "normal" 90% to 91% (February 2026) from 86.6%.

Operating leverage

C-7555P-0152
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

C-558th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: fixed-cost operating leverage is modest (about 1 point of expense ratio per 10% of premium growth, worth about 9% to 10% of pre-tax profit); the much larger source of operating leverage is the combined ratio itself, where each point moves net profit by about 8% to 10%, so the range of outcomes is set by the loss ratio, not by revenue.

  • Cost structure. Net operating expenses about EUR 209m (FY25, the 21.1% expense ratio), of which acquisition costs (including about EUR 50m of mass-media and search marketing) are the largest discretionary item; a single site and about 2,400 staff are largely fixed in the short term. If roughly half of operating expenses are fixed, 10% premium growth lowers the expense ratio by about 1 point, worth about EUR 11m to 12m pre-tax (derived).
  • Underwriting leverage. At a CR of 91%, the underwriting margin is about 9% of earned premium; a 1-point change in CR changes the technical result by about 11% and net profit by about EUR 7.8m to 10.5m (company sensitivity and July memo), and a 100-basis-point shift in the motor loss ratio changes pre-tax earnings by about EUR 8m.
  • Range of outcomes (next 18 months). CR between 89% and 95% is plausible (89% if Q2's 90.6% persists and expenses fall; 95% in a claims-inflation step with delayed pricing). Against a consensus that implies a CR of about 91.5%, that spans net income of roughly EUR 80m to 135m for FY27 (consensus EUR 114m), with the central case near consensus. The 2022 to 2023 precedent (CR from about 90% to 104%) shows the tail.
  • For the model: drive profit from earned premium times (1 minus CR) plus investment income; flex CR, not expenses.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

D528th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: fixed-cost operating leverage is real but small, worth about 0.1 point of expense ratio for every 1% of premium growth before reinvestment, and in this business it behaves less as an earnings driver than as a management buffer: the leverage it creates (about 0.6 to 0.8 points a year at 7% to 8% growth) is mostly spent on price, marketing and technology, and the discretionary part of the cost base (marketing of about EUR 50m) is the lever management will pull to hold the reported CR in the "low 90s" when the loss ratio deteriorates. It will therefore shape the line items by damping, not by driving: it lowers the expense ratio by only 0.1 to 0.2 points a year net (21.1% in FY25 to about 20.1% by FY30), adding about EUR 2m to 3m of pre-tax profit a year, while the underwriting leverage of the CR (1 point is about 9% of net income) sets the range of outcomes. Over FY20 to FY25 the same was true: pre-tax profit swung by EUR 186m (EUR 180m in FY20 to EUR -6.5m in FY23) on revenue that moved by only 9%, all of it through the loss ratio.

How much is fixed, and what the record shows.

  • Operating expenses at the expense-ratio level were about EUR 227m in FY25 (21.1% of EUR 1,076.7m earned premium); by nature, total operating expenses (which include claims-handling staff) were EUR 357.4m in FY25 against EUR 344.8m in FY24 (+3.7%) while earned premium grew 8.6%. Staff costs, the single site at Tres Cantos and the core platforms are fixed in the short term; headcount fell from 2,433 (2024 average) to 2,373 (H1 26) while customers grew 7.8%.
  • The FY24 to FY25 fall in the expense ratio (22.1% to 21.1%, one point on 8.6% premium growth) implies about 0.12 points per 1% of growth, which is what a cost base about 55% fixed would produce. That is the gross leverage.
  • The net leverage is lower and is a choice. In Q1 26 the expense ratio was 19.0% (down about 2 points) because marketing, media, IT and acquisition spend was seasonally low (CFO, 27-Apr-26); in Q2 26 it was about 21.4%, flat year on year on about 8% premium growth (derived in the first set). Q2 shows the company reinvesting the whole quarter's leverage. The CFO has said technology spend will rise; the European DORA and AI Act regimes add fixed compliance cost; and the company is in scope of the sustainability reporting directive.

Why the leverage acts as a buffer, not a driver (the insight for the third set).

  • The marketing budget (about EUR 50m, roughly 4% to 5% of premiums) is the one large discretionary line. Q1 26 shows what a quarter of lower marketing looks like: 2 points off the expense ratio. If the loss ratio rises by 1 to 1.5 points in 2027, management can offset part of it for two to four quarters by trimming acquisition spend, at the cost of policy growth.
  • Combine that with the other buffer, reserve prudence (the risk adjustment held at the 85th percentile, prior-year development not disclosed), and with incentives (50% of the long-term plan is on the average CR), and the reported CR is likely to be stickier than the underlying loss ratio. The practical implication for monitoring: the first visible sign of margin stress will be slowing policy growth and a falling expense ratio, not a rising CR. A quarter in which the expense ratio falls sharply outside Q1 while policy growth slows should be read as defensive, not as efficiency.

Reverse leverage. In the 2023 to 2024 repricing the customer base fell 4.2%; with half the cost base fixed, a 4% fall in premium would add about 0.5 points to the expense ratio. That is small beside the loss-ratio swing of that period (the CR rose from about 90% in 2021 to 104.1% in 2023).

How it shapes the line items (base case).

Line FY22 to FY25 (last four years) FY26E FY27E FY28E FY29E to FY30E
Expense ratio 22.1% (FY24) to 21.1% (FY25); 20.2% (H1 26); earlier years not in the files 20.6% 20.5% 20.3% 20.2%, 20.1%
Gross leverage at the forecast growth rate about 1.0 point about 0.8 about 0.7 about 0.6
Retained (net) improvement 1.0 point (FY25) 0.5 0.1 0.2 0.1 a year
Pre-tax value of the retained improvement about EUR 6m about EUR 1m about EUR 3m about EUR 1.5m a year
  • FY26 retains 0.5 points because H1 26 already printed 20.2%; H2 26 is modelled at about 21.0% (H2 marketing is heavier). FY27 retains little because technology spend rises and because I expect the company to keep marketing up to defend volume while it raises prices. The medium-term target of an expense ratio below 20% is reached, in this model, only after FY30; I treat it as a possibility for FY28 to FY29 only if marketing is cut.
  • Against the last four to six years: the period FY19 to FY24 had revenue growth of 1.5% to 4.3% a year, so fixed-cost leverage barely operated; FY25 to FY27 is the first period in which it works at 7% to 10% growth, which is why the expense ratio has fallen 1.9 points in eighteen months. Its contribution from FY27 is smaller because growth slows.

Range. The expense ratio is the narrowest-range line in the model: 20.0% to 21.0% for FY27 (about EUR 6m of pre-tax profit either side). The CR range (91.0% to 93.8% for FY27, see P-09) is about four times wider.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7672P-0260
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?C+725th of 19full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C-605th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: there is no financial gearing to amplify EPS; the leverage that matters is insurance leverage (about 2.7 times premiums to equity and 2.4 times investments to equity), which makes EPS roughly 9% sensitive to each point of CR and makes the balance sheet sensitive to interest rates through solvency, not through earnings. The more important finding is that the balance sheet is over-capitalised for its growth: at 7% premium growth the company needs to retain only about EUR 33m to 35m a year to hold solvency flat, so a payout of 65% to 70% holds the ratio at about 196%, a payout of about 70% to 75% would let it drift to the 180% reference, and there is a one-off surplus of about EUR 45m to 60m above 180% today. The balance sheet therefore shapes EPS through capital return, not borrowing: the base case raises the payout from 53% to 70%, which costs nothing in EPS, and the latent option is a buyback or special dividend of about EUR 50m (about 3.7% of the market value) that would add about 2% to 3% to EPS.

Financial leverage: none. Total debt is EUR 1.6m (leases) at 30-Jun-26; there is no interest expense to speak of, no refinancing and no hybrid capital. All own funds are unrestricted Tier 1.

Insurance leverage and its effect on EPS.

  • GWP of about EUR 1.23bn in FY26 against equity of about EUR 440m to 455m (2.7 times); a fixed-income portfolio of EUR 1,057m (EUR 490.6m government, EUR 566.6m corporate) within total investments of about EUR 1.26bn (2.8 times equity).
  • At FY27 earned premium of EUR 1,275m, a 1-point CR move is EUR 12.8m pre-tax and EUR 9.6m net (9% of net income). The same move on equity is about 2 points of ROE. This is the gearing the Consumer should carry.
  • A 1-point move in the investment yield on about EUR 1.3bn is about EUR 13m pre-tax, but yields move slowly through a 3.5-year duration: a 50-basis-point change in reinvestment yields changes FY27 investment income by only about EUR 2m (about 30% of the portfolio reprices a year).

Rates act on the balance sheet, not on earnings. Under IFRS 17 the discounting of claims liabilities runs through other comprehensive income, so rate moves change equity, not net income. Solvency sensitivities (2025 annual report): +100 basis points in rates costs 9.6 points of solvency, -100 basis points adds 10.4 points; +100 basis points of credit spreads costs about 5 points. Even a combined 200-basis-point shock leaves solvency above 175%.

The capital arithmetic (derived; the key output of this answer).

Mid-2026 FY26E FY27E FY28E
Eligible own funds (EUR m) 491.5 about 512 about 550 about 585
SCR (EUR m), growing with premiums 250.3 about 260 about 279 about 298
Solvency ratio, base payout (60%, 65%, 70%) 196.3% about 197% about 197% about 196%
Own funds needed each year to hold 180% about 34 about 34
Retained earnings at the base payout about 37 about 35
  • The revised Solvency II rules from 30-Jan-27 cut the risk-margin cost of capital from 6% to 4.75%; for a short-tail non-life book the uplift is small (a few points, about EUR 5m to 10m of own funds), not the sector's average 8 points.
  • The growth-payout trade-off is roughly one for one: each 1 point of extra premium growth consumes about EUR 4.7m of own funds a year (1% of an SCR of about EUR 260m, times 1.8), which is about 4.5 points of payout. At 5% growth the sustainable payout rises to about 75% to 78%; at 10% growth it falls to about 60%. This is why the CFO said in February 2026 that growth of 11% to 12% "puts pressure" on dividend capacity, and why the payout can rise as growth slows: the deceleration of premium growth in FY27 to FY30 is what releases the higher payout.
  • Equity and ROE (base): equity about EUR 442m (FY26), 479m (FY27), 514m (FY28), 550m (FY29), 588m (FY30), against consensus EUR 454m, 500m, 545m (consensus likely includes positive other comprehensive income). ROE about 24.8% (FY26), 23.1% (FY27), 23.5% (FY28), 22.8% (FY29), 21.9% (FY30). ROE slowly declines because the payout below 100% keeps adding equity faster than earnings grow once the CR stops improving.

EPS effect of the balance-sheet options.

  • Base case: no buyback; flat share count of 1,088.4m (treasury purchases of about 1.2m shares a year for employee plans are re-delivered; the Alantra liquidity contract bought 2.57m and sold 2.15m shares in Q3 26, which is trading support, not a buyback).
  • Option: a EUR 50m buyback at EUR 1.23 retires about 40.7m shares (3.7%) and forgoes about EUR 1.5m of after-tax investment income, adding about 2.5% to EPS. It would lower solvency by about 19 points to about 178%; it is affordable only with the Solvency II uplift and the slowing growth, which is why February 2027 (FY26 results, after the rule change) is the natural date. Nothing in the files says it is planned; management has never bought back shares and the controlling holders (Cartival about 20.75%, Bankinter 17.42%) would see their stakes rise.

What would change this. A claims-inflation step that pushes the CR above 94% (net income below about EUR 90m) cuts retained earnings and stalls the payout increase, but does not threaten the dividend from a 196% start; the 2023 suspension happened from a weaker starting position in an actual loss year.

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7750P-0350
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

D5013th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: very little of the cost base is cuttable without harming growth; claims (about 71% of earned premium) can be reduced only through selection, fraud control and the repair network; of operating expenses (about 21%), marketing is the one large discretionary line, and the rest is already lean; the realistic reduction is gradual expense leverage of 1 to 2 points over three years, not a cost programme.

  • Claims (net claims incurred about EUR 797m in FY25, including about EUR 131m of claims-handling costs). Reducible through risk selection, higher redirection to CAR and partner workshops (the claimed EUR 200 saving per claim applies to only about half of repairs), AI claims triage (about 30% faster processing), fraud detection and digital claims filing. Severity itself (Baremo, parts, labour) is not controllable.
  • Operating expenses (about EUR 209m). Marketing and acquisition is discretionary: cutting EUR 10m to 20m would raise margins but slow acquisition, contrary to the strategy. Staff is already lean (2,411; 0.09% temporary; managers cut from 79 to 74). Technology spend is expected to rise (CFO). Compliance (DORA, AI Act, CSRD) adds cost.
  • Reinsurance. The health quota share without commissions costs margin to 2029 (a renegotiation would help health's CR). Excess-of-loss costs are falling with the market.
  • Peers. Admiral's UK motor expense ratio is about 16.7%, Allianz Direct about 16%; Linea Directa's auto expense ratio is already about 16% (JB Capital), so its group expense ratio is held up by home, health and new lines.
  • For the model: expense ratio 20.5% to 21% in FY26 (H2 marketing is seasonally heavier), easing to 20% to 20.5% by FY28.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C-507th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: there is no large cost to eliminate; the realistic efficiency gains are several small ones that together add about EUR 12m to 15m to pre-tax profit by FY28 against FY26 (about 9% to 10% of FY26 pre-tax profit), of which about 45% comes from health moving toward breakeven, about 30% from the return of rental income, and about 25% from retained expense leverage; about EUR 4m to 6m of further gains are likely to be passed into price or absorbed by technology and compliance costs. Timing is back-loaded: little reaches FY27 (about EUR 3m to 5m), most arrives in FY28, and the health and rental items are one-off steps, not a recurring trend.

The items, sized and dated.

Item Mechanism Pre-tax gain against FY26 When Confidence
Health towards breakeven HNA network from Q1 2027 (56,500 points of care, 1,300 hospitals) lowers medical cost; mix shift to complete products (67.7% of policies); expense ratio already down from 50.1% to 41.3% about EUR 2m (FY27), EUR 5m to 6m (FY28) 2027 to 2028 Medium: three years of losses, profit sharing with Munich Re absorbs part of the gain
Rental income from the renovated Madrid building EUR 242,000 a month before July 2025; works end late 2026; rent resumes before June 2027 about EUR 1.5m (FY27), EUR 3m to 3.5m (FY28) mid-2027 High on timing, medium on the new rent

Digital claims and service (AI chat resolving 64% of queries, digital claims filing above half, processing times cut about 30%) lower claims-handling cost inside the loss ratio about EUR 1m to 2m a year gradual Low to medium: also offered by every incumbent; mostly passed to price
Owned repair network (CAR, over 12,000 repairs in 2025; a claimed EUR 200 per-claim advantage) severity control not modelled as new Low: redirection fell from 71% (2021) to 50% (2022), no later figure; CAR volumes fell from 13,418 (2024) to about 12,150 (2025)
Offsets: technology spend (the CFO said it will rise), DORA, AI Act, sustainability reporting fixed cost about EUR -2m to -4m a year from 2026 Medium

Why health is the largest item, and why it is not larger. The health technical deficit was EUR 2.8m in H1 26 (net of the 50% quota share), so about EUR 5m to 6m a year at today's run-rate. Moving the net health CR from about 123% (FY26) to about 106% (FY28) and 100% (FY29) recovers most of that. Two caveats: health grows 12% to 15% a year, so the deficit would widen in euros at a constant CR; and the 2024 addendum gives Munich Re a profit share once the technical result is positive, so the upside above breakeven is shared. The health CR has fallen 15 points in two years (140.3% in 2024 to 125.1% in H1 26), so the path is evidenced, but no breakeven date has been published.

What does not reach the line items.

  • Marketing cuts. Cutting EUR 10m to 20m of acquisition spend would add 1 to 2 points to the margin but slow acquisition; the strategy and the incentives (70% of annual targets on written premiums) argue against it except as a temporary buffer (P-01).
  • Large structural cuts. The cost base is already single-site and lean (2,373 staff, managers cut from 79 to 74); the precedents in the AlphaSense report (Direct Line's GBP 100m programme and repeatedly reset targets) show that announced programmes at direct motor insurers under-deliver.

Phasing into the model.

  • H2 26: nothing new beyond the seasonal expense ratio (about 21.0%).
  • FY27: about EUR 3m to 5m net (health about EUR 2m, rental about EUR 1.5m, expense about EUR 1m, offsets about EUR -1m). This is too small to offset the loss-ratio step, which is why FY27 earnings are flat.
  • FY28: a further EUR 8m to 10m (health, full-year rental, expense leverage), which is a large part of why FY28 net income grows about 10%.
  • FY29 to FY30: about EUR 2m a year (expense leverage only); health at breakeven adds nothing further.

Against the last four to six years. The FY23 to FY25 recovery of over EUR 120m of pre-tax profit was almost entirely pricing; efficiency added perhaps EUR 10m to 15m (the expense ratio fell about 2 points on about EUR 1bn). The forecast period's efficiency contribution is of similar size in euros but far larger as a share of the change in profit, because pricing will contribute little.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4755P-0550
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?D5511th of 19full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

D508th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: one reinforcing loop drives volume (lower cost, lower price, more policies, lower unit cost) and it is still working but weakening; almost every loop that acts on the margin is balancing. The system is therefore mean-reverting: it converges on growth of about 5% to 7% at a CR of about 92%, with deviations corrected within four to six quarters by management's repricing, by competitors' behaviour and by the capital requirement. For the line items this means a decelerating written-premium line (8.3%, 7.0%, 6.6%, 5.8%, 5.4% for FY26 to FY30), a CR held in a narrow band (91.4% to 92.2%) rather than trending, and EPS growth that is lumpy year to year (FY27 about +2%, FY28 about +10%) but about 7% to 8% a year over five years. The one loop that turns from headwind to tailwind as growth slows, the seasoning of a book with less new business, is what cushions FY28.

The loops, in order of importance for the model.

Loop Type How it works Evidence now (dated) Direction over the forecast Effect on the line items
1. Cost to price to volume Reinforcing Expense ratio 7 to 8 points below Mapfre's lets prices sit below the market at a better CR; volume spreads fixed cost Customers +8.5% (2025) to 3.73m, +7.8% to 3.86m (H1 26); expense ratio 22.1% (FY24) to 20.2% (H1 26) Weakening: growth slows as the company reprices and the umbrella narrows GWP growth +8.3% falling to +5.4%; expense ratio -0.1 to -0.2 points a year
2. Price-sensitive churn when price rises Balancing Customers won on price leave when price rises Customers -4.2% in 2023 when the company applied the sector's largest motor increases; motor churn about 14% Activates in 2027 as written motor price rises to about 3% to 4% Net additions fall from about 278,000 to about 180,000 to 200,000 a year; policy growth about +4.8% (FY27), +3.6% (FY28)
3. Price-cost scissors (earned price lags claims cost) Balancing on margin Earned price in a year reflects written price of the previous twelve months; claims cost reflects current inflation Motor loss ratio flat at 72.9% (H1 26) while written price runs about 1% to 2%; Spanish inflation 4.5% (August 2026); Baremo update for 2027 due around December 2026 Opens in 2027, closes in 2028 after repricing Motor CR about +0.5 point (FY27), -0.3 (FY28)
4. Market price umbrella Balancing at market level, two-sided Incumbents' repricing creates a gap Linea Directa fills; inflation widens it, margin recovery closes it Market +5% to 6%; Mutua freezing 50% at a 97% CR ceiling; Mapfre's Iberia auto CR 93.7% to 94.7% (H1 26) Widens in an inflation step (Mutua forced to reprice), narrows in a soft market (2028 to 2030) Supports growth in 2027; caps growth and lifts the CR in FY29 to FY30
5. New-business seasoning (selection dilution) Balancing while growing fast; becomes a tailwind as growth slows New business carries a loss ratio 5 to 15 points above renewals (former Root executive, expert calls of November and December 2025; Jefferies on Spain, August 2021) Gross new business is about a fifth of the book (about 290,000 net additions plus about 14% churn on about 3.6m) As net additions fall by about 90,000 to 100,000 a year, new business falls by about 2.5 points of the book About -0.25 point on the loss ratio in FY27 to FY28 (at a 10-point penalty); partly offsets loop 3
6. Capital consumption by growth Balancing on distributions Each point of premium growth consumes about EUR 4.7m of own funds a year (P-02) CFO, February 2026: growth of 11% to 12% pressures dividend capacity Eases as growth slows Payout rises from 53% to 60%, 65%, 70%
7. Reserve prudence Smoothing, one-off Provisioning confidence raised to the 95th percentile in Q2 2023, returned to the 85th by end-2023, held there under IFRS 17 Prior-year development not disclosed; CFO (February 2026): no voluntary releases The percentile release is spent; future releases depend on run-off No tailwind modelled; a buffer that lets the reported CR lag the underlying (P-01)
8. Data to selection Reinforcing, weak More risks give better pricing data 2.85m motor risks; but Mutua (Akur8 pricing, synthetic data), Mapfre (over EUR 1bn of technology spend a year) and AXA close the gap Neutral None modelled
9. Cross-selling Reinforcing, weak Motor base sold home and health Non-life lines above 18% of premiums (2025); but home growth slowed to 2% and average home premium falls; Admiral, the benchmark, has only 20% of UK customers on two or more products Slight Home +2.5% to +4% a year; health +12% to +15%
10. Cost of attention Balancing, slow Search, comparison and possibly AI-agent costs rise with competition Mapfre with Google cut its cost per sale 20% through value-based bidding; Santander added Verti car insurance to its offer (1-Oct-26); Mapfre bought 50.01% of Carrefour's brokerage (10.5m club members) Slowly against Marketing held at about 4% to 5% of premiums; no acquisition-cost leverage assumed

Which loop dominates, and why the system is mean-reverting.

  • Volume: loop 1 dominates for the next 18 months (growth stays above the market's 4% to 6%), but loops 2 and 4 are already slowing it (written growth +10.2% in Q1 26, about +8.2% in Q2 26).
  • Margin: balancing loops dominate. Loop 3 opens in 2027; loop 5 offsets about a third of it; management's repricing closes it within four to six quarters (the 2023 to 2024 response took about that long, from a much worse starting point). In the other direction, if the CR fell below about 90.5%, the incentive structure (70% of annual targets on written premiums) and the strategy ("leverage cost efficiency to offer competitive pricing") would spend the margin on price. The CR is held in a band from both sides; that is why I model 91.4% to 92.2% in every year rather than a trend.
  • The precedents agree. Admiral's cost loop ran for two decades because its cost gap was about 10 points and it ceded most risk; Direct Line's loop reversed when it refused comparison sites and priced late; Sabre's margin-first approach cut premiums 14.2% in 2025 to protect profit. Linea Directa sits between Admiral and Sabre: a cost loop strong enough to grow above the market, not strong enough to grow and expand margins at the same time.

The interplay that matters for predictability. Loops 1 and 2 couple growth and margin negatively: when the company reprices, policy growth slows and average premium rises, so written premium growth moves within a band of about 6% to 8%. Revenue is therefore predictable to about plus or minus 1.5 points a year; the CR (about plus or minus 1.5 points in FY27) carries the uncertainty. In euros the CR range (about EUR 26m of net income from best to worst case in FY27) is about six times the revenue range's effect (about EUR 4m).

What would break the pattern.

  • Toward a reinforcing downside: claims inflation of 6% or more for two years with Mutua still freezing (both loop 3 and loop 4 against the company at once), which is the 2022 configuration; the CR would reach 94% to 95% before repricing caught up.
  • Toward a reinforcing upside: a disinflation in which severity falls to 2% while market prices keep rising (Mutua forced to unfreeze for its own reasons), which would let the company grow and widen its margin together, as in 2025.

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

C586th of 19

Judgement: guidance is broadly in line with consensus for FY26 and slightly above it for FY27 if taken literally ("low 90s" CR and an expense ratio below 20%), because consensus already embeds a CR of about 91% to 91.5%; the gap is small because the company gives no numerical earnings guidance and the brokers anchor on its words.

  • Guidance. C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

D5213th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: for FY26 the guidance is conservative (H1 26 is already at 91.1%); for FY27 to FY28 it is about neutral against the last five years' best levels and the pre-2022 norm, but slightly demanding once adjusted for the input forces, which are turning less favourable (claims inflation, the Baremo update, Mutua) while the company prices below the market.

  • Against history. CR FY22 96.3%, FY23 104.1%, FY24 94.7%, FY25 92.6%, H1 26 91.1%; pre-2022 target about 90%. "Low 90s" is consistent with the recent run-rate and the historic norm; it is not stretched against history.

In 2024 to 2025 the CR benefited from the earned-through effect of the 2023 to 2024 price increases, moderate frequency and possibly reserve releases from the 95th-to-85th percentile change. In 2027 those tailwinds fade, while severity inflation (Baremo likely above 2026's 2.7%, parts and labour at 4% to 6%) rises and the company's own pricing runs at about 2%. Holding the low 90s then requires either higher prices (at a volume cost) or further selection and expense gains.
  • Expense target. Below 20% is not yet evidenced on a full-year basis (Q2 26 about 21.4%, derived).
  • For the model: base CR 91% to 92% in FY27, a downside of 93.5% to 94.5%; treat the sub-20% expense target as a 2028 possibility, not a 2027 input.

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

F4017th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: three things are uncommon and material: the CEO does not engage with analysts; sell-side coverage is very thin for a EUR 1.3bn listed insurer; and the free float is small with a liquidity contract supporting trading; together they explain part of the valuation discount and make the stock more volatile around news.

  • CEO absent. Results are presented by the CFO and the IR head; the CEO gives a separate press conference to Spanish media; the H1 26 call omitted public Q&A and delegated follow-ups to IR. Admiral's and Mapfre's CEOs personally engage analysts.
  • Coverage. FactSet shows five to six brokers with estimates (UBS, Bestinver, JB Capital, Renta 4, Santander, one restricted); eight firms in total at end-2025; international brokers rarely publish. Thin coverage widens the gap between fundamentals and price in both directions.

  • Reporting. Quarterly results (FactSet treats the company as semi-annual) and a reporting basis that does not disclose reserve development.
  • No capital markets day and no dated strategic plan; the 2026 to 2028 long-term incentive plan was approved, so internal targets exist but are not published.

To what extent have sell-side estimates been cut deeply and fully across all analysts, and has company guidance recently been rebased lower, or more aggressively kitchen-sinked or sandbagged?

Judgement: neither: estimates have not been cut (they are flat to slightly up), and guidance has been raised, not rebased; there is no "cleared base" cushion of the kind that protects a stock after a deep reset; the last deep cut and rebase were in 2023.

  • Revisions (FactSet). FY26 EPS mean +1.1% (one month), +3.1% (three months), +1.7% (six months); FY27 +0.1%, +0.3%, +0.9%, +1.4% (twelve months); FY28 +0.7%, +1.2%, +4.5%, +3.8%.   • Guidance. Raised twice in 2026 (C-13); no kitchen-sinking.
  • Read. Estimates are stable at a level that assumes the CR holds; the slight fall in target prices and buy share while EPS edges up points to multiple caution (margin-peak worry), not earnings worry. A downgrade cycle would start only if the CR deteriorates.

Judgement: the financial forecasts for the next 18 months are moderately predictable, with the predictability split in the way the second set described and the Q3 26 results have since confirmed: the top line and the capital line are highly predictable, the combined ratio is the one variable that matters and it carries a negative skew, and the next move in that ratio (up about 0.6 points in FY27) is predictable in direction and roughly in size because its two external inputs are now largely known for 2027 (a Baremo of 3.3% to 3.5%; parts and labour at 4% to 5%) and its internal input (a written motor price of about 1.5% through Q3 26) is visible in the company's own derived figures. The earlier sets are congruent with each other and with the first out-of-sample print; the expected change rests mostly on demonstrated strengths; the drivers are concentrated in one trackable ratio; the cone for FY27 earnings is about -17% to +11% around a base that is 7% below consensus; disclosure lets the Consumer see the outcome each quarter but not the drivers in advance; the trace to EPS is clear; and the price trades mostly on its own prints but its multiple sits with a de-rated cohort. The case is simple, well calibrated and slightly asymmetric to the downside in 2027: a predictable flat year below consensus, inside management's guidance, followed by a more predictable recovery in 2028 if the company writes 3% to 4% of price in 2027.

  • What is predictable (high confidence). Revenue (FY27 GWP growth 5.5% to 8.5%); FY26 net income (about EUR 105m, above consensus of EUR 103m); the direction of FY27 against consensus (below); the dividend for FY26 (payout 55% to 60%; no buyback); the Baremo for 2027 (3.3% to 3.5%); the dates and content of the tests (15 December, late February, late April).
  • What is predictable in direction but not in size (medium confidence). The FY27 CR (base 92.0%; range 91.0% to 93.8%); the timing of consensus cuts (after FY26 results at the earliest, after Q1 and H1 27 more probably); Mutua's 2027 stance (more likely to extend than to end, with no trigger disclosed).
  • What is not predictable (low confidence). Home weather; the 2028 Baremo (set by 2027 inflation, which the Bank of Spain puts at 3.7% and CaixaBank at 2.5%); the moment incumbents turn to volume (Mapfre's 2027 to 2029 plan says 2027); reserve development (the first number, a EUR 15.9m 2025 surplus, needs reconciling); sector sentiment shocks to a stock whose co-movement with the sector has just tripled.
  • The two changes to carry into the model and the memo. FY26 raised to about EUR 105.5m (expense ratio retained better than modelled; nine months at the top of the expected range); the capital surplus above 180% removed (solvency about 180% to 185% at end-2026 post-dividend after the Q3 rate hit), which keeps the payout path but ends the buyback option. FY27 to FY30 held: the better expense ratio and the slightly better underlying motor loss ratio in Q3 offset the higher 2027 inflation forecasts and the earlier soft-market signals, so the base is unchanged and the downside probability rises from 25% to 30%.
  • For the fourth set. The predictability findings imply a specific shape of events: beats and reassurance through February 2027 (FY26, the Baremo at 3.3% to 3.5%, a higher dividend), then a flat-to-down earnings year that consensus has not modelled and that management's "low 90s" guidance accommodates, so the gap will open between consensus and delivery, not between guidance and delivery.
