How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

A742nd of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the perimeter is very narrow (one country, one dominant line, three segments, one site) and stable, which makes the model simple and the drivers few; it also concentrates all risk on Spanish motor pricing and claims, so predictability is high in normal years and low in a claims-inflation shock.

  • Perimeter. Spain over 99.9% of premiums (Portugal EUR 69,000); motor 80.5% to 81.4%, home about 14% to 15%, health about 3% to 5%, other about 0.3% to 0.5%; all euros; no international, no life, no asset management; four wholly owned service subsidiaries. Regional concentration: Andalusia 24.27%, Catalonia 21.17%, Madrid 11.63%.
  • Change. Slight broadening through small new lines and health growth; motor's share fell only from 81.4% (FY25) to 80.5% (H1 26).
  • Predictability. High: two fulcrum drivers (motor policy growth and the motor CR), no currency, no M&A, no debt. Low in a shock: no geographic or product diversification to offset a Spanish motor downturn (the Risks report's concentration point), as 2023 showed.
  • For the model: forecast motor in detail (policies, average premium, frequency and severity proxies via the loss ratio); home and health can be modelled as growth and CR assumptions.
D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

C-624th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the expected change is modest and mostly already delivered, so it is highly plausible: the base case asks the company to keep doing in 2027 what it did in 2024 to 2026 (grow above the market on cost, hold the CR in the low 90s, build capital and pay it out), and every one of those is a strength demonstrated over the last eight quarters and, in the case of the cost advantage and the CR gap to the market, over a decade. The thesis leans on strengths the business would need to acquire in only three places, none of which is in the 18-month base case: repricing upward by 3% to 4% in 2027 while still growing above the market with Mutua frozen (the company has never done both at once; in 2023 to 2024 it repriced and lost 4.2% to 4.5% of customers), bringing health to breakeven (three years of losses, a provider network that only starts in Q1 2027), and running a thinner reserve buffer (85th percentile) through a Baremo step without strengthening. The base case needs the first to succeed only partially (written price 2.5% to 3%, policy growth slowing to about 5%, not falling), the second only to continue its trend (CR 124% to 115%), and the third not to fail. The precedents say partial success is the norm for a cost leader of this kind.

The expected change, reduced to its parts, and the strength each rests on.

Part of the expected change (model base) Demonstrated? Evidence (dated) What would need to be acquired
Above-market volume growth (GWP +7.0% FY27 against a market of 4% to 6%) Yes, for eight quarters Motor GWP +9.5% in 9M 26 against a sector +6.1%; policies +7.9%; 290,400 net additions in 2025 (record); market share gain 0.2 to 0.3 points a year Nothing for FY27; the model already slows policy growth to 4.8%

Reserve adequacy through a Baremo step Partly Stochastic Merz and Wüthrich reserving, independent annual review; 2025 motor TPL run-off surplus EUR 15.9m (OWN-3); CFO: no voluntary releases Prudence held at the 85th percentile (Admiral 94th) while bodily-injury severity rises 3.3% plus; Mapfre has just added 2 to 3 points of reserves for the same risk

Why the plausibility is high for the base case. Three quarters of the expected FY27 earnings change (about EUR 2m) is nothing more than volume at the current margin offset by the price-cost scissors; the company does not have to improve anything, it has to avoid losing about 1 point of CR. The FY28 step (+10%) rests on the repricing earning through, health improving and rental income returning, each of which has a dated mechanism already in motion (tariffs written from H1 27, the HNA contract signed in July 2026, building works ending late 2026). The first set's management judgement (targets raised only after delivery; a floor rather than a stretch) and the second set's record table (every stated range met since FY24) give the base case a management that does not over-promise.

Why the expected change is not fully demonstrated: the three strengths to acquire.

  1. Repricing into Mutua's freeze. The precedent set is mixed but instructive. Successes: Admiral raised UK rates by high single digits in H1 26 ahead of 5% to 7% claims inflation without losing retention (Morgan Stanley, September 2026), with a cost gap of about 11 points and most risk ceded; Occident raised mass premiums by more than 10% without higher cancellations, but through agents; Generali raised retail motor 8% in 2024 and average premium 6.4% in 2026 ahead of claims trends; Unipol grew its direct channel 18% in Q1 26 while containing severity through repair networks. Failures: Direct Line priced late in 2022 (CR 103%, dividend cancelled, then sold to Aviva); Sabre protected margin by shrinking 14.2% in 2025; Linea Directa itself in 2022 to 2023. What distinguished success: a structural cost gap large enough to price up and remain cheapest (Linea Directa has this, about 7 to 10 points), pricing ahead of inflation rather than after it (Linea Directa did not in 2022, and is not now: its written price is about 1.5% against claims cost of about 3% to 4%), and distribution that softens price sensitivity (Linea Directa does not have this: 100% direct, shoppable). The honest reading: Linea Directa can reprice (demonstrated) but cannot reprice without a volume cost (demonstrated the other way), so the base case's "slower growth, no fall" is the middle of the precedent range, not its best end. The market's own consumer evidence (LexisNexis 2026: price decides switching for 86% of shoppers; 72% open to moving) says the volume cost is real.
  2. Health breakeven. The improvement so far is all expense ratio (50.1% to 41.3%) with the loss ratio flat at 83.7%; the HNA network is the first lever on the loss ratio and is untested; Munich Re's profit share caps the upside above breakeven. Small in euros (EUR 4.1m nine-month technical loss, about 4% of group pre-tax profit), so a failure here costs about 2% of FY28 earnings, not the case.
  3. Reserve prudence at the 85th percentile through a Baremo step. The 2016 precedent (a 190 basis point hit absorbed at a 90% CR) and the 2025 run-off surplus say the book has been adequately reserved in normal years; 2020 (a EUR 25.1m motor liability deficit) and 2023 say it has not been in shock years. The 2027 step is a normal year by this standard (3.3% to 3.5%, not 8.5%), so the needed strength is modest; but Mapfre's Q3 26 reserve addition is a warning that a prudent incumbent sees severity above its prior estimates, and Linea Directa has given no such signal.

What the plausibility test says about the lean. The base case relies about four-fifths on demonstrated strengths; the FY28 to FY30 upside relies about half on strengths to be acquired. That matches the second set's weights (high for FY26, medium for FY27, low-to-medium thereafter) and supports leaning FY26 up (done), FY27 neutral to slightly down against consensus, and FY28 to FY30 at the model.

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

D485th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: for the next 18 months (to about May 2028, which covers FY26, FY27 and the Q1 28 print) the cone is narrow for revenue (FY27 GWP growth 5.5% to 8.5%, about plus or minus EUR 4m of net income), moderately wide for earnings (FY27 net income about EUR 88m to 118m around a base of EUR 106m, that is about -17% to +11%), and negatively skewed, because inflation steps are front-loaded and repricing is lagged while the upside is capped by a strategy that spends margin on price. Since the second set the cone has narrowed at the near end and widened at the far end: the 2027 Baremo is now effectively known at 3.3% to 3.5%, which removes the "above 4%" tail from FY27, while 2027 inflation forecasts have been revised up, Mapfre has strengthened reserves and announced a turn to volume, and AXA has set a double-digit direct-growth target for Spain, which together move the soft-market and 2028-Baremo risks closer. My probability weights move from 25/50/25 to 25/45/30 (upside/base/downside), the probability-weighted FY27 net income from EUR 104m to about EUR 103m, and the base-case input stays at 92.0%. The case is therefore moderately predictable: the direction of FY27 (flat, below consensus) is predictable with high confidence; the magnitude is predictable to about plus or minus 10% with the tails identifiable and dated.

The cone, by line and horizon (EUR m unless stated; base is the model after my FY26 change).

The three cases for FY27, updated for the new evidence.

Probability-weighted FY27 net income about EUR 103m (0.25 x 116 + 0.45 x 106 + 0.30 x 90). Against consensus of EUR 114m, the weighted view is about 10% lower and the base about 7% lower; against the model base the weighted view is about 3% lower, which is the measure of the skew.

What narrowed the cone (the evidence since the second set).

  • The Baremo formula. The 2027 revaluation is the December 2025 to November 2026 average of year-on-year CPI, known to within about 0.2 points today (3.33% estimated on 3-Nov-26; final on 15 December). The second set's most important single uncertainty for 2027 has become a near-known number at its central assumption. The remaining bodily-injury uncertainty for FY27 is judicial (how courts apply the scale) and reserving (open claims revalued at the payment-year scale), not the scale itself.
  • The Q3 print. Nine-month CR 91.3% with the expense ratio at 20.0%, motor loss ratio flat to slightly better underlying, health 122.3% in Q3, financial result +14%. The FY26 end-point is now fixed to within about EUR 3m, which fixes the base from which 2027 starts.
  • The policy count. Net additions of about 40,000 in Q3 confirm the model's deceleration of policy growth (7.3% in 2026 to 4.8% in 2027) rather than a sharper break; the revenue cone is unchanged.

What widened it.

  • 2027 inflation. The Bank of Spain's 3.7% (October 2026), BBVA's 3.2% and the PwC survey in which 52% of Spanish executives call inflation structural all point to claims-cost growth staying at 3.5% to 4.5% through 2027 and a 2028 Baremo of 3% plus, which moves the H2 27 and H1 28 CR risk up and makes the second set's "repricing closes the scissors in 2028" dependent on the company writing 3% to 4% of price in 2027, not 2.5%.
  • Competitors' stated plans. Mapfre's turn to premium and unit growth in 2027 to 2029 and AXA's target of more than 10% direct growth in Spain are the first dated evidence that the soft-market phase (the second set's FY29 to FY30 drift) could begin in 2027 to 2028, which would cap Linea Directa's price and volume at the same time. Mutua's freeze has no end date. Management's "customer acquisition slowed slightly" is the first company-side confirmation that the umbrella is narrowing.
  • Reserving signals from the incumbent. Mapfre's 2 to 3 point reserve addition in Iberia motor for Baremo prudence is a sector signal that bodily-injury severity is running above prior estimates. Linea Directa, at the 85th percentile with no such addition, either has a better book or a thinner buffer; the OWN-3 report cannot tell which.
  • Capital. Solvency at 191.5% in September and about 180% to 185% at year-end post-dividend means a bad FY27 would be visible in the dividend as well as in earnings (the 2023 precedent), which widens the cone for the dividend per share (the FY27 range above) even though the base is unchanged.

The skew, explained by mechanism rather than asserted. The downside case is EUR 16m to 18m from the base; the upside EUR 8m to 12m. Three asymmetries produce this. (1) Lag asymmetry: a severity step hits the loss ratio within a quarter, while repricing takes two to four quarters to earn through, so adverse news lands before the remedy. (2) Strategic asymmetry: if the CR falls toward 90.5%, the incentive structure (70% of annual targets on written premiums) and the stated strategy push the gain into price and marketing, so the reported upside is capped; if the CR rises, management can trim marketing (about 1 to 1.5 points) and lean on reserve prudence, so the reported downside is damped but not removed. (3) Weather asymmetry: home has had two benign years and the CFO has named the normal level; the distribution of the next year's weather is bounded above (about 86%) and long-tailed below (95.8% in 2023).

Predictability, stated plainly. Revenue: high. FY26 earnings: high (one quarter to go). FY27 earnings: medium, with the direction (flat to down against FY26's run-rate, below consensus) more predictable than the level. The sign of the next consensus revision: predictable (down for FY27). The share price over 18 months: low to medium, because 74% of its daily variance is idiosyncratic (D-07) and the multiple is at the 8th to 10th percentile of its range, so it responds to prints and to the cohort's sentiment rather than to the model's base.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

F447th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the disclosure is helpful on outcomes and unhelpful on drivers: every quarter the company publishes the figures that let an investor see whether the thesis is on track (GWP and policy counts by line, segment loss, expense and combined ratios, net income, solvency, investment return and duration), but it does not publish the three numbers that would tell the investor whether the thesis is about to go off track (the written price change on new business and renewals, claims frequency and severity, and prior-year reserve development), its guidance is verbal and outcome-only, and the CEO does not take analysts' questions. For the next six months this means the Consumer can track the margin only one quarter in arrears and must build the leading indicators from derived ratios and from external sources (the INE's CPI and the pension revaluation, Jefferies' claims-cost index, ICEA's sector growth, Mapfre's quarterly Iberia motor commentary, Mutua's press statements). The dated company events in the window (FY26 results and the final dividend in late February 2027; Q1 27 results in late April 2027, the first quarter with the 2027 Baremo and the HNA network) are the right tests, and the quarterly cadence is better than FactSet's semi-annual treatment implies; but the company gives the investor no early warning, so a miss would be learned from the print, not before it.

What the company discloses each quarter, and what it lets the Consumer check.

What it does not disclose, and why each gap matters for the next six months.

  • Written price on new business and renewals. The FY27 margin is set by the price written between now and mid-2027. The company gave a figure once (about 2% in the Q1 26 call) and otherwise leaves it to derivation from GWP over policies, which mixes new business, renewals and mix. The January 2027 renewal round is the single most important unobservable in the window.
  • Frequency and severity. The first set's Risks report named the lack of quarterly frequency data as the primary gap; nothing has changed. The Consumer will see the loss ratio, not its parts, so cannot tell a frequency improvement (durable) from a severity lull (not).
  • Prior-year reserve development. Not disclosed quarterly; the annual individual accounts (March 2027) and the SFCR (April 2027) are the only sources, and the OWN-3 report shows even those need reconciling (EUR 15.9m surplus against EUR 3.4m deficit in motor TPL for 2025). The 85th-percentile risk adjustment is stated, not quantified.
  • Retention and churn by vintage. Motor churn about 14% was the last figure; with Mutua freezing and the company about to reprice, retention is the number that would show whether "slower growth, not a fall" is holding.
  • Marketing spend. The buffer the second set identified cannot be seen; its use must be inferred from the expense ratio.
  • Guidance. "Low 90s", "below 20% over the medium term", "over 4 million", "above EUR 100m", solvency floors. No premium, EPS, payout, Baremo-impact or health-breakeven guidance. The CFO fronts results; the H1 26 call had no public Q&A.

The external monitors that fill the gaps (dated).

  • INE CPI monthly (October 4.9%); the preliminary November CPI in late November and the definitive figure on 15 December fix the 2027 Baremo. The pension revaluation decree follows (possibly delayed by the 29-Nov-26 election, but applied retroactively to 1 January).
  • Jefferies' claims-cost index (spare parts +5.1%, repair and maintenance +3.7% in September 2026; parts 50% and maintenance 30% of physical claims); Morgan Stanley's repair-cost series (+4.4%).
  • Mapfre's quarterly Iberia activity report (February 2027 for FY26; April 2027 for Q1 27): average motor premium change, fleet change, reserve commentary, volume stance. Mapfre is the best proxy for the market's price and for incumbent reserving.
  • Mutua's FY26 results and tariff announcement (March 2027): whether the freeze is extended to 2027 and for what share of the book.
  • ICEA sector premium growth (motor +6.1% for nine months 2026) and the sector CR (about 98% in 2025).
  • Sector narrative monitors: Morgan Stanley's and DNB Carnegie's AI-agent notes; the co-movement table (D-07).

Peers for context. Mapfre discloses average premium, fleet and reserve movements by quarter on a CEO-led call; Admiral discloses reserve releases as a share of premium and its risk-adjustment percentile; Sabre gives written margin guidance. Linea Directa is below all three on drivers and equal on outcomes. The I2 report's finding that the CEO briefs staff quarterly in open sessions but not analysts is the clearest statement of the gap: the information exists; it is not shared.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

D584th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the trace from drivers to EPS is unusually clear because the business is simple (one country, one dominant line, no debt, no minorities, a statutory tax rate, a flat share count), and every link in the chain except two is visible each quarter: customers times average premium gives written premium; written premium earns through with a six-month lag; earned premium times one minus the CR gives the technical result; plus the investment result and minus about EUR 7m of other items gives pre-tax profit; times 0.75 and divided by 1,088.4m shares gives EPS. The two links that are not visible are the ones inside the CR (the written price and the claims cost per policy), and that is precisely where the thesis lives. The external transmission is also clear and now largely quantified: Spanish CPI feeds the Baremo through a published formula (30% of motor claims cost; open claims revalued at the payment-year scale), energy feeds parts (50% of physical damage) and labour (30%), the Consortium boundary (wind under 120 km/h, rain through roofs) leaves ordinary weather with the home line, Mutua's tariff policy sets the price gap that drives policy growth, the sector CR sets the incumbents' volume stance, and yields reach the investment result slowly (duration 3.45 years) and solvency quickly (-9.6 points per 100 basis points). The weakness is not the mechanism but the company's end of it: what the company "says it will achieve" is a set of outcomes with no stated building blocks, so the trace runs from our model, not from the company's plan, and the milestones (4 million customers, EUR 100m of profit, an expense ratio below 20%) are all reached or nearly reached, which leaves nothing the company has committed to for 2027 except "low 90s".

  • "Over 4 million customers": link 1; reached about Q2 27; worth about EUR 1m of net income per 1% of policies, so a milestone, not a driver.
  • "Net profit above EUR 100m": link 9; reached in FY26.
  • "Solvency not below 150%, about 180% as the dividend reference": link 10; the reference becomes binding at the FY26 dividend.
  • "Outgrow the market", "profitable growth", "further improvement below 92% will come from expenses": qualitative; consistent with the model's mechanism. The milestones were set for the recovery and are all achieved or about to be; management has not yet restated them for the next phase. The 2026 to 2028 long-term incentive plan (average CR, premium growth against the sector) implies internal targets that are not published.

Where the trace is clear enough to be used as a dashboard, and where it is not. The Consumer can run links 1, 3, 4, 6 to 10 from published figures each quarter and will be right to within a few percent. Links 2 and 5's internals need the derivations in D-05 and the external monitors. The external chain is clear and, unusually for an insurer, mostly formula-bound (the Baremo) or disclosed by a peer (Mapfre's pricing and reserving). The one external actor whose transmission is clear in direction but not in timing is Mutua.

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

A421st of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: the stock is exposed to several sentiment shocks it cannot control, and its small size, low free float and weak price momentum amplify each of them; the most likely are a rerun of the "shopping agents" and "autonomous vehicles" de-ratings that hit Nordic P&C and Admiral, a Spanish motor price-war headline, a Bankinter placement and a general risk-off squeeze.

  • Theme de-ratings. AI shopping agents (Nordic P&C -6.9% over three months; DNB Carnegie's 3% to 15% EPS-at-risk estimate for some insurers; the sector digest's "new, early" story) and autonomous vehicles (Admiral de-rated 45% against the sector; Morgan Stanley's illustrative 30% premium-pool erosion by 2050). Linea Directa is 81% motor and 100% shoppable direct business, so it is a natural target of either narrative.
  • Read-across from UK motor softening. AXA notes UK motor pricing softening; the UK industry CR is expected at 106% to 111% in 2026. A UK-led "motor cycle has peaked" call could spill into Spanish motor names.
  • Price-war headlines. A Mutua announcement extending its freeze or a Mapfre change of tone would hit the stock on the day, whatever Linea Directa's numbers.
  • Bankinter placement. 17.42% (about EUR 230m) against about USD 1.1m to 1.3m a day traded: a block sale would need a discount and could take the price down 5% to 10% on the day.
  • Market regime. Risk-off, US 10-year yield about 5.10%, Brent USD 111, defensives leading; the Fitness for Fighting digest is Double Underweight on fragility (small, illiquid names) and Underweight on weak momentum; the stock's technical momentum is graded D.
  • Governance or IR surprise. The CEO's absence from Q&A and rising control weaknesses make the stock vulnerable to a credibility event (for example a restatement or a regulator's finding).

C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

C-609th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: management upgrades only after delivery and in small verbal steps; it is a cautious, honest tracker rather than a sandbagger with a precise hidden target; this makes its upgrades credible but means they lag the numbers, so they rarely surprise the market.

  • Record. 94% to 95% CR (set late 2024; FY24 delivered 94.7%) to "92% is too good to be true on a consistent basis" (Q3 25) to "comfortable with 92% to 93%" (February 2026, after FY25 at 92.6%) to "low 90s" (April 2026, after Q1 at 91.7%). The EUR 1bn premium target was reached in 2024 and the ambition raised ("we aim for more"). Solvency has always landed above floors.
  • Nature. Upgrades are reactive, verbal and hedged (weather, seasonality, technology costs). The CEO does not front them; the CFO does. Mapfre's raising of its ROE and CR targets in March 2026 is a more formal upgrade pattern by comparison.
  • Behaviour in a downturn. In 2023 the company did not pre-announce a rebased target; it missed and then reset to 94% to 95%.
  • For the model: treat the stated range as a floor for delivery in stable conditions; do not expect guidance to lead consensus up.
C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

D5611th of 19

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: over one year the record is consistent beats; over three years it is a large miss (FY23) followed by beats; over five years it includes two misses in a claims-inflation shock, which is the environment most likely to recur; the record is therefore good in stable conditions and poor in the comparable adverse condition.

  • Last year. FY25 net income +33.5% and "reported net profits frequently exceeded consensus prior estimates throughout the 2025 earnings recovery"; Q1 26 CR 91.7% against a 92% to 93% comfort range; H1 26 delivered a faster recovery than the sell-side modelled and a 19% profit increase.
  • Three years. FY23: net loss of EUR 4.4m and a CR of 104.1% against the historic target of about 90% (a severe miss; dividends suspended); FY24: CR 94.7% within the 94% to 95% target; FY25: CR 92.6%, beating it.
  • Five years. FY21 net income EUR 110m (the listing year); FY22: CR 96.3% and net income down 43% to EUR 63m as inflation outran tariffs; FY23 the loss. The sell-side adjusted slowly during the 2023 downturn and then upgraded rapidly in late 2024.
  • Comparable conditions. The current macro (energy-led inflation, Spanish CPI 3.2% to 4.5%) is a milder version of 2022. The company starts from a CR of about 91% rather than about 90% with weak tariffs, with a sharper pricing toolkit and a management that has lived through the lesson; but its current pricing (about 2%) is below the market, as it was in 2021 to 2022.
P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

D4812th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the record is good at delivering what management says once it says it (targets are set after delivery and have been met or beaten every period since FY24) and poor at anticipating a claims-inflation turn (FY22 and FY23 were missed badly); so the forecasts should carry high weight on revenue at every horizon, high weight on FY26 earnings, medium weight on FY27 earnings, and low-to-medium weight on FY28 to FY30 earnings. The lean is up against consensus on FY26 earnings (slightly) and on FY27 revenue (clearly), and down against consensus on FY27 and FY28 earnings, because consensus extrapolates a CR of about 91.2% into a year in which the company's 2026 pricing guarantees a weaker earned price-to-cost balance. Against my own base, FY26 is balanced, FY27 has a fatter left tail than right, and FY28 to FY30 lean slightly up (repricing and health could beat the model).

The direction of the lean, and its reasons.

  • FY26: lean slightly up (my EUR 104m against consensus EUR 103m). H1 26 delivered EUR 52.1m; H2 26 has an easy comparison, because Q3 25 carried about EUR 5m of severe bodily-injury claims (about 2 points on that quarter's CR). My H2 26 CR of 91.6% leaves room for normal autumn weather. The Q3 26 print on 28-Oct-26 should show nine-month net income of about EUR 77m to 80m and GWP growth of about 8.5% to 9%.
  • FY27: lean down on earnings (my EUR 106m against EUR 114m), up on revenue (my GWP +7.0% against +5.2%). The reasons are specific, not general caution: (1) the motor loss ratio is already flat, not improving (72.9% against 72.8%); (2) the 2027 earned motor price is fixed by 2026's written price of about 1% to 2%; (3) the 2027 Baremo update follows 2026 inflation and is likely to be about 3.5% against 2.7%; (4) home has had two benign weather years (CR 87.1% in FY25 and 86.6% in H1 26 against the CFO's "normal" 90% to 91%); (5) the investment return has fallen to 2.75%. Consensus at about 91.2% implicitly assumes none of these bites.
  • Why not lean further down: management has two buffers (marketing and reserve prudence, P-01), an incentive to use them (50% of the long-term plan is on the average CR), and a record of meeting the stated range. The reported CR is likely to stay in the "low 90s" even if the underlying moves; my 92.0% is inside that range.
  • FY28 to FY30: lean slightly up against my own base. The repricing in 2027 earns through in 2028, health reaches breakeven, and capital return can step up; the risks are the soft-market phase (P-04) and the long-term distribution threats, which I carry as a gradual CR drift to 92.2%.

For the third set. The most important asymmetry is between guidance and consensus: in my base case management meets its guidance in FY27 (92.0% is "low 90s") while consensus is cut by about 5% to 8%. A forecast miss for this company is therefore more likely to show up as a consensus downgrade after the Q4 26 and H1 27 prints than as a guidance cut.

7a

Growth rate

4 answers
Judgement50C-4962P-0858P-1050
Group judgement

Financial forecasts

D5010th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: the financial model for Linea Directa is simple, and its predictability is split sharply in two. The top line is one of the most forecastable in European non-life: compulsory annual contracts, a written-to-earned lag that fixes about half of next year's earned premium today, and a price-volume trade-off that keeps written growth in a band of about 6% to 8% whatever management chooses; I forecast earned revenue growth of 10.0%, 7.4%, 6.8%, 6.0% and 5.5% for FY26 to FY30 with a narrow cone. The margin is the opposite: a single ratio, the CR, set by the gap between earned price and claims inflation and by weather, carries about 9% of net income per point, and its next move is up, not down, because 2026 pricing (about 1% to 2% in motor) is below 2026 to 2027 claims-cost growth (about 3% or more), the home book has had two benign years, and the H1 26 gain came from home and health while motor held flat. The base case is therefore steady compounding with an air pocket: net income of EUR 104m (FY26), 106m (FY27), 117m (FY28), 121m (FY29) and 124m (FY30), EPS growth of about 7.6% a year from FY25 to FY30, ROE of 22% to 25%, a payout rising to 70% with solvency held at about 196%, and a FY27 that is about 7% below consensus on net income but inside management's own "low 90s" guidance.

  • What the Consumer should test for predictability (third set). The cone is narrow for revenue (FY27 GWP growth 5.5% to 8.5%) and wide and negatively skewed for earnings (FY27 net income EUR 89m to 116m, probability-weighted EUR 104m); the drivers are concentrated in one ratio and, within it, in three items (motor price against claims cost, home weather, health). The reported CR is likely to be stickier than the underlying because management has two buffers (marketing spend and reserve prudence) and an incentive to use them; the earliest warning signs will be slower policy growth and a falling expense ratio outside Q1, not a rising CR. The company's disclosure gives segment CRs, premiums and policy counts each quarter, which is enough to track the top line and the segment mix, but not frequency, severity, written price or reserve development, which are the margin drivers.
  • What the Consumer should test against consensus and narrative (fourth set). Consensus is on a different revenue basis (GWP) from FactSet's history; on like-for-like GWP it is too low for FY27 (+5.2% against my +7.0%) and its net income is too high (EUR 114m against my EUR 106m) because it implicitly carries the H1 26 CR into 2027. Guidance is more likely to be met than cut; consensus is more likely to drift down through 2027 than up, after the Q4 26 and H1 27 prints. The upside the market is not paying for is capital: slowing growth releases a payout of 70% or more and leaves room for a one-off return of about EUR 50m, and the margin plateau of about 92% sits about 5 points above (worse than) the company's pre-2022 history, so it is not a cyclical peak in the company's own terms.
  • Where this set disagrees with the first set. The first set's central FY27 case was a broadly flat CR and net income near consensus; this set puts the FY27 CR about 0.6 points higher and net income about 7% below consensus, for the reasons in P-04 and P-06. It also corrects the HNA timing (Q1 2027), the health reinsurance commission (restored in 2024 with profit sharing), the direction of the investment yield (down to 2.75%), and the basis on which consensus revenue is reported (GWP).

P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C585th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: below operating profit, almost nothing moves: tax at about 25% (the Spanish statutory rate, against 22.9% to 24.9% in recent normal years), no interest, no minorities, no associates, no buybacks and a flat share count, so EPS growth equals net income growth and both equal pre-tax profit growth to within a few tenths of a point. The only items that could change the picture are below the line in name only: one known unknown (the 2020 to 2022 corporate income tax and VAT audits), the rental income that returns in 2027 (inside the investment result), and the capital-return choice. For an insurer free cash flow is not the right measure; distributable capital is, and it rises from about EUR 63m of dividends for FY26 to about EUR 87m for FY30, a payout of 60% to 70%, against a history that ran from near-100% payouts under Bankinter (FY19 to FY22) to zero (FY23) and back to 53% (FY25).

Against FY19 to FY25 (EUR 107m, 135m, 110m, 63m, -4m, 64m, 86m), FY28 (EUR 116.6m) only matches the FY19 to FY21 average (EUR 117m), and FY30 is still below the FY20 peak (EUR 135m, a pandemic-frequency year) even though revenue is about 70% larger. That gap is the most important historical fact in the model: the pre-2022 CR was about 86% to 88% (Jefferies, 2020); the forecast CR is about 92%.

Free cash flow and distributable capital.

  • FactSet's free cash flow (EUR 134m in FY25, 156% of net income) is operating cash flow less fixed-asset capital spending, and for a growing insurer it includes the growth of claims reserves and unearned premiums, which are not free to distribute. I do not use it.
  • Distributable capital is governed by Solvency II (P-02). Dividends declared: about EUR 63m (FY26), 69m (FY27), 82m (FY28), 85m (FY29), 87m (FY30). Dividend per share EUR 0.058, 0.063, 0.075, 0.078, 0.080; at EUR 1.226 that is a yield of 4.7% (FY26) rising to 6.1% (FY28).
  • Against history: FY19 cash dividends of EUR 104m (97% of net income) and FY21's EUR 198m (180%, including capital extraction before the spin-off) show what this balance sheet distributed under Bankinter, when ROE was near 30% because surplus capital was stripped. The listed company holds solvency at about 196% rather than near the 150% floor; a return toward a 180% ratio is the source of the latent upside.
  • Capital spending (fixed assets and intangibles) was EUR 12.1m in FY25 and EUR 8.4m in H1 26; about 1% of premiums, inside operating costs for this form.

What could move EPS below the line. (1) The tax audit: a one-off charge of unknown size (I would treat anything up to EUR 5m as noise). (2) A buyback, which is the only below-line item that could lift EPS growth above net income growth. (3) A Bankinter placement of its 17.42% stake, which changes nothing in EPS but widens the free float.

P-10

Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

D5011th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the base case is a direct insurer compounding volume at a held margin: written premiums growing 8.3% (FY26) decelerating to 5.4% (FY30) as policy growth slows from about 7% to about 3% and average premium rises from about 1% to 2% to 3%; a group CR of 91.4% (FY26), 92.0% (FY27), 91.7% (FY28), 91.9% (FY29) and 92.2% (FY30), inside management's "low 90s" every year; an investment result rising from EUR 43.5m to EUR 55m on portfolio growth and the return of rental income, not on yield; and net income of EUR 104m, 106m, 117m, 121m and 124m (EPS EUR 0.096 to 0.114, about 7.6% a year from FY25), with the payout rising from 53% to 70% and solvency held at about 196%. Against consensus, FY26 is in line, FY27 is about 7% lower and FY28 about 4% lower in net income (7% in EPS); the whole gap is the CR (my 92.0% against an implied 91.2% for FY27), partly offset by higher premium growth (my +7.0% GWP against consensus +5.2%).

4. Against guidance. Management's words: a CR in the "low 90s" (April 2026, after "comfortable with 92% to 93%" in February 2026); an expense ratio below 20% over the medium term; more than 4 million customers; net profit above EUR 100m; solvency not below 150%, about 180% as the dividend reference; no premium or EPS guidance; "further improvement below 92% will come from the expense ratio". My base meets every one except the sub-20% expense ratio within the horizon (20.1% by FY30), and passes 4 million customers in Q1 27 and EUR 100m of net profit in FY26. Guidance is therefore more likely to be met than raised or cut in FY27; it could be raised in words at FY26 results (late February 2027) if Q4 26 is strong, which would then make FY27 consensus look safer than it is.

C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

D6013th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: there is plausible scope to add roughly 10% to 20% to pre-tax profit over three years beyond volume growth, from many small areas (health to breakeven, investment yield, rental income, expense leverage, digital sales, new lines), all congruent with the strategy; peer precedents show each is achievable, but the company's own strategy is to pass much of the gain to customers through prices, so the realised share will be lower.

  • Health to breakeven. About EUR 5m to 6m pre-tax (about 4% of profit) by 2028 if the CR falls from about 125% to 100%; Mapfre's Iberian health runs at about 92.9%, the sector at about 87%, so breakeven is plausible, but three years of losses argue for caution.
  • Investment yield. Reinvesting a EUR 1.05bn portfolio with a three-year duration at higher yields adds perhaps 20 to 40 basis points over two to three years, about EUR 2m to 4m; rental income from the renovated Madrid building returns from June 2027.
  • Expense leverage and digital sales. About 0.5 to 1 point of expense ratio, EUR 6m to 12m, if fully online sales rise from 12% and AI absorbs service volumes (Admiral targets GBP 100m of efficiencies by 2028; Allianz 30 basis points a year).
  • New lines. SME multi-risk, pet and mobility: small.
  • Capital. A higher payout or buyback (EPS-neutral or accretive at 11 times) rather than an earnings lever.
  • Congruence. Fully congruent ("profitable growth", "ever more digital", "multi-product insurer").
  • For the model: include health narrowing (EUR 1m to 2m a year) and investment income growth in the base case; treat expense gains as half retained, half passed into price.
C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

D559th of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: the growth engine has decelerated modestly from its 2025 peak but is still running well above the company's own normal rate; the input force (the price gap to repricing incumbents) is mature and slowly weakening, not breaking.

  • Deceleration. Written premium growth: FY25 +11.3%, Q1 26 +10.2%, Q2 26 about +8.2% (derived). Motor about +10.6% to about +9.1%; home +6.3% (FY25) to about +1.7% (Q2 26); health +20.1% (Q1) to about +11.8% (Q2). Policyholder growth +8.5% (FY25 and Q1 26) to +7.8% (H1 26).
  • Against the normal rate. Revenue growth was 1.5% to 4.3% a year in FY19 to FY24 (FactSet). Current written growth of about 8% to 10% and earned growth of 10.6% are about double the historical norm; the company is still in an above-normal growth phase.
  • The input force. Share gains rest on the gap between the market's price increases (about 5% to 6%) and the company's (about 2%). Two forces are narrowing it: Mapfre's clean-up is advanced, and Mutua is freezing prices for up to half its auto book while growing 13%. Average motor premium growth at Linea Directa is already only about 1% to 2%, so premium growth is now almost all volume.
  • Watch. Q3 26 (28-Oct-26) will show whether motor growth holds at 8% to 9%; the July memo's warning line is motor growth below 7% to 8% while Mutua grows double digits.
  • For the model: written growth of about 8% (H2 26) and 7% to 8% (FY27), not 10% to 11%.

D5010th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the next five years are a regime change in growth and a plateau in margin. Revenue grows about 7% a year (FY25 to FY30), more than twice the 2.9% of FY19 to FY24; the pre-tax margin settles at about 10.5% to 11.3% of revenue, far more stable than the 19.6% to -0.7% swing of FY20 to FY23 but about 5 points below the FY19 to FY21 level; and pre-tax profit and EPS grow about 7.5% to 8% a year, with all the growth from volume and investment income and none from margin after FY26. The net result is that FY26 is close to consensus, FY27 is about 7% below it (a flat year), and FY28 is about 4% to 7% below it; the shape is "steady compounding with an air pocket in 2027", not "margin peak followed by decline".

  • Consensus (EUR 114m) sits between my base and upside cases; it is reached only if the H1 26 run-rate holds with no weather or inflation normalisation. The skew is negative: the downside case is further from the base (EUR 17m) than the upside (EUR 10m), because inflation steps are front-loaded and repricing is lagged, while the upside is capped by the strategy of spending margin on price.
  • A 2022-type rerun (CR 96% to 98% for a year) is a tail below the downside case (about 5% probability; net income about EUR 55m to 70m).

Versus the last four to six years, the three net changes the Consumer should carry.

  1. Growth has stepped up and will stay above history. Revenue growth of 5.5% to 10% a year against 1.5% to 4.3% in FY19 to FY24: the company is taking share at about 2 to 3 points a year above a market growing 4% to 6%.
  2. The margin has stopped improving and will be range-bound. The CR improvement of 13.0 points from FY23 (104.1%) to H1 26 (91.1%) is over; the forecast range is 91.4% to 92.2%. The pre-tax margin of about 11% is about 5 points below the FY19 to FY21 level, because the market's structure has changed (the 2016 Baremo reform, a mutual leader willing to run at 97%, higher acquisition spend), not because the margin is at a cyclical peak. The market reads today's margin as a peak; the model reads it as a plateau below the company's own history.
  3. Earnings quality is more volume-driven and therefore more predictable than in FY20 to FY23, except for the one exposure that has not changed: a claims-inflation step while the company prices below the market.

Memo topic 7, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

C-474th of 8

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: the gap between our assessment and what guidance, consensus and the narrative reflect is a gap of timing more than of direction: company guidance already accommodates our forecasts (so it will be met, with the payout as the one item likely to be raised), consensus does not (FY27 about 7% too high, FY28 about 4.5% too high, dividends slightly too low), and the narrative knows the risks but not the arithmetic on either side. The next three to four months are favourable (a FY26 beat against consensus, a Baremo confirmed near 3.3% to 3.5% against fears of 4% plus, a higher dividend, a solvency uplift in January); the following nine to twelve months are unfavourable (the first year-on-year combined-ratio deterioration since 2023, consensus cuts of 4% to 8%, net breadth negative, Mutua's 2027 stance unresolved); and the period beyond that is favourable again (repricing earned through, health near breakeven, a 70% payout, an expense ratio below 20%, and a multiple at the 10th percentile that already prices the risks). For Richard this means the case is not one of positive expectation gaps today; it is one of a cheap, well-capitalised, predictable business that will go through a visible air pocket in 2027 which the market has not modelled, and the attractive entry is more likely to be after the Q1 or H1 27 prints than before them, unless the February 2027 capital statement or a Mutua retreat changes the sequence.

  • Guidance (I-01, 55). Met throughout 55%, raised 15%, reduced 30%; the reduction risk sits in the H1 27 to Q3 27 events; the payout is the item most likely to be raised, but from a solvency position at the 180% reference rather than above it, which rules out a buyback and limits the step to 65% to 70%.
  • Consensus (I-02, 40). FY26 up 2% to 4% by February; FY27 and FY28 cut 4% to 8% and 3% to 6% between February and October 2027 (55%); DPS up; breadth positive then negative. The dispersion among the five brokers is narrow because they share one assumption (a flat CR), which makes the eventual revision correlated across houses.
  • Narrative (I-03, 48). Negatives well known and in the multiple (Mutua, Baremo-as-risk, shoppable earnings, AI agents, float); the 2027 scissors known as a fact and not as an earnings effect; the positives (benign Baremo arithmetic, payout capacity, margin plateau, 2028 recovery) unarticulated; the medium-term soft-market threat from Mapfre and AXA unconnected to the company.
  • How this reconciles with the model's return. The model's total shareholder return of +21.6% to December 2027 (+18.0% annualised) and +33.6% to June 2029 at the guideline 12.38x multiple is built on our below-consensus earnings; it needs no consensus upgrade, only delivery of a flat FY27 and a FY28 recovery, the dividends (8.8 points of the 18-month return) and a multiple that holds at about the cohort's current depressed level. The risk to that return in the next 18 months is the path, not the destination: consensus cuts in a thinly covered, 74%-idiosyncratic stock with weak momentum can take the multiple below 10x (the five-year low is 9.44x) before the recovery is visible, which is why the downside case in the first set (about EUR 0.78 to 0.82 at the second set's downside EPS) and the drawdown precedents (Sabre, Direct Line) belong in the memo's riskiness section.
  • The three things that would change this judgement. (1) A Mutua retreat in March 2027: the narrative's main negative would flip and the 2027 scissors would close faster through price. (2) A capital-policy statement in February 2027 that sets a payout range of 70% plus on the parent's profit: the DPS narrative would lead the EPS narrative. (3) A Q1 27 motor loss ratio up more than 1.5 points year on year: the downside case, and a consensus cut of 15% to 20% rather than 4% to 8%.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

C+553rd of 8

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: company guidance is more likely to be met than moved at every one of the six earnings events in the next 18 months, because it is verbal, outcome-only and wide ("a CR in the low 90s"), because the company is already inside it (91.3% for nine months) and because our base case stays inside it in FY27 (92.0%); the probabilities over the whole window are about 55% that the CR guidance is simply reiterated throughout, 15% that it is raised in words (most likely at the FY26 results in late February 2027, after a FY26 CR of about 91.2% to 91.4%), and 30% that it is lowered at some point (most likely at the H1 27 or Q3 27 results, if the 2027 price-cost scissors and a normal weather year push the quarterly CR to 93% or above). The milestones are different: net profit above EUR 100m is met at the FY26 results (95%), 4 million customers around the H1 27 results (85%), and the expense ratio below 20% is now a 2027 event rather than a "medium-term" one (60%), so the next phase has no numerical target left and management will have to restate its ambitions; the most likely new statement is a higher payout (a step to 65% to 70% of profit at the FY26 or FY27 results, 50%) rather than a numerical earnings or premium target (20%) or a buyback (under 10%, because solvency sits at the 180% reference after the planned dividend and Cartival needs cash). For 18 months to three years out there is no formal guidance to move; the 2026 to 2028 incentive plan implies internal targets, and a capital markets day or a 2028 to 2030 plan would be the vehicle for raising them, which the company has never held.

Why "met" dominates even in our below-consensus base. The second set's central point stands: the gap in 2027 is between consensus (which implies a CR of about 91.2%) and delivery (92.0%), not between guidance and delivery. "Low 90s" accommodates 92.0% and even 92.5%; the company has moved its words only after delivery (94% to 95%, then "92% too good to be true", then "comfortable with 92% to 93%", then "low 90s") and has never pre-announced; and management holds the two buffers (marketing, about 1 to 1.5 points; reserve prudence) and the incentive (50% of the long-term plan on the average CR) to keep the reported ratio inside the phrase. A guidance cut therefore requires the downside case, not the base.

What would drive a raise. (1) The Baremo confirmed at 3.3% to 3.5% on 15 December (now likely), removing the main stated reason for caution; (2) a Q4 26 CR at or below 91.5% with the expense ratio about 20.2% for the year, which would let the CFO say the sub-20% expense ratio is a 2027 target; (3) Mutua narrowing its freeze in its March 2027 announcement (no evidence yet); (4) the Solvency II revision adding about 3 points on 30 January, which would let the board set the final dividend at the upper end. The raise would be in words ("around 91% to 92%") and in the payout, not in a numerical plan.

What would drive a reduction. (1) Spanish CPI staying at 4% to 5% through H1 27 with parts at 5% plus, so that the claims cost per policy runs 4.5% against an earned price of about 2%; (2) Mutua extending the 50% freeze into 2027, which would make a 3% to 4% written increase cost more volume than the model assumes and tempt management to hold price (the 2022 error); (3) a normal or bad weather year in home (87% to 90% plus); (4) a precautionary reserve addition of the kind Mapfre made in Q3 26 (2 to 3 points of the claims ratio) ahead of a 3% plus 2028 Baremo; (5) a severe-claims quarter like Q3 25. Two of these together would take a half-year CR to 93% and the guidance to a new phrase.

The capital statement at the FY26 results (the item the market will trade on). Solvency 191.5% in September, about 180% to 185% at year-end after a final dividend of about EUR 45m (total EUR 63m, 60% of consolidated profit, 80% or so of the parent's individual profit), plus about 3 points from January. The board's reference is "about 180%", so a 60% payout is consistent with policy and a step to 70% would take the ratio below the reference unless rates reverse. Probabilities at the February 2027 event: total FY26 dividend EUR 58m to 65m (55% to 60% payout) 60%; EUR 65m to 75m (65% to 70%) 25%; held at EUR 45m 10%; a buyback or special dividend 5%. The second set's "natural moment for a capital policy update" is still February 2027, but the content is more likely a restated dividend policy (a payout range on the parent-company profit) than a step change.

Secondary: 18 months to three years out. There is no mid-term numerical guidance to raise or cut. The implicit ambitions (expense ratio below 20%, health breakeven "late 2027", solvency about 180%, "outgrow the market") are, in our base, met (expense ratio), met late (health breakeven in FY29 at 100%), met (solvency at the reference) and met (growth 6% to 7% against a market of 4% to 6%). The probability that management sets a new numerical plan (a capital markets day, a 2028 to 2030 CR and payout framework) within three years is about 35%; the Admiral precedent (a revised capital-allocation policy with buybacks in late 2025) and the Mapfre precedent (targets raised in March 2026) show how peers do it, and the thin coverage and 10th-percentile multiple give management a reason. If a plan is set it is more likely to be set below our base on growth (management will not commit to 7%) and at our base on margin ("low 90s" or "91% to 93%"), which would read as conservative.

Score. The gap between our assessment and what guidance reflects is slightly favourable: guidance is wide, will be met in our base, is conservative for FY26, and leaves the payout as the one item likely to be raised; it is not favourable enough to generate upgrades to consensus, because it is also what consensus already assumes.

I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

D406th of 8

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: consensus will move in two directions in sequence: FY26 estimates are likely to be upgraded by 2% to 4% between now and February 2027 (nine-month net income of EUR 80.4m against a FactSet median of EUR 103m for the year; three of the five visible brokers last updated in July), with net breadth positive; FY27 and FY28 estimates are likely to be cut by 4% to 8% and 3% to 6% respectively between the FY26 results and the Q3 27 results (probability about 55%, against 30% held within plus or minus 2% and 15% upgraded), with net breadth turning negative from the Q1 27 print, because consensus carries the H1 26 combined ratio (about 91.2%) into 2027 while our base has 92.0% and the probability-weighted view about 92.2%. Dividend per share consensus (EUR 0.055, 0.065, 0.069) is below our 0.058, 0.063, 0.075 for FY26 and FY28 and will probably be raised for FY26 in February 2027 and for FY28 later, so the stock may see EPS cuts and DPS upgrades at the same time, which is the signature of a company whose payout rises as its growth slows. The net breadth of revisions, already weak (buy share down 10 points in three months to 57%; target prices -3% in a month; momentum score -2), will improve into February and deteriorate from April. For 18 months to three years out, FY28 (three brokers) is likely to be rebased with FY27 and FY29 to be initiated near our base (EUR 121m), so the secondary consensus is more likely to be cut then held than raised.

Where consensus stands, broker by broker, against our base (FactSet, 25-Oct-26; our base after the FY26 change).

Probabilities for FY27 consensus net income over the 18 months as a whole: cut by more than 2% 55%; held within plus or minus 2% 30%; raised by more than 2% 15%. The "held" case requires the upside CR case (91.0% to 91.3%: benign weather, Mutua narrowing, parts inflation fading), which I give 25%; "raised" requires it plus faster volume. The "cut" case is simply our base arriving.

The breadth mechanics with five brokers. Each broker is 20% of the breadth; a single downgrade moves the buy share from 57% to 43%; a single estimate cut moves the net revision count from 0 to -1. The FactSet series shows the pattern already: FY27 EPS up 1.4% in twelve months while target prices fell 3% in a month and the buy share fell 10 points, that is, numbers nudged up by beats while conviction fell on the margin-peak worry. The I1 report's point that consensus is "highly sensitive to single-analyst revisions" cuts both ways: one Spanish house moving its FY27 CR from 91% to 92% would by itself cut the median by about 5%.

What would make consensus rise instead. (1) Q4 26 and Q1 27 CRs at or below 91.5% with policy growth holding at 6% plus, which would validate the brokers' flat-CR assumption for another two quarters; (2) a payout step to 70% at the FY26 results (DPS consensus would jump; EPS unchanged); (3) a Mutua retreat in March 2027; (4) a benign Baremo at or below 3.2%. The first is the only one that moves EPS, and it is the upside case.

What would make the cuts deeper than 4% to 8%. The downside case (CR 93.5% to 94%, net income EUR 88m to 92m) would cut FY27 consensus by 20% and FY28 by 10% to 15%, with the breadth entirely negative and at least two of the four Buys moving to Hold. The precedents in the D2 report (Sabre's 62% cut to 2022 EPS in one note; Direct Line's dividend cut; the Spanish sector's 2023 CR of 102% against stable forecasts) show that forecasts built on a flat CR into a claims-inflation step are revised late and sharply; the mitigation here is that the step is smaller (3.3% to 3.5% Baremo, not 8.5%) and the start point stronger (91.3%).

Secondary: 18 months to three years out. FY28 consensus (EUR 122m, three brokers; EPS 0.115 on four) is 4.5% above our EUR 116.6m and will be rebased when FY27 is; FY29 will be initiated during 2027 at about 5% to 8% growth on the rebased FY28, which lands near our EUR 121m. The secondary consensus is therefore more likely to be cut (to our level) and then held than raised; the items that could raise it are the ones the market does not yet model (health breakeven, a 70% payout, the expense ratio below 20%), and they are worth 5% to 8% of FY28 EPS in aggregate.

Score. The gap is unfavourable in the near term: our FY27 and FY28 are below consensus and the cuts are likely to arrive in the middle of the window; it is favourable on FY26, on dividends and on the far end, which cushions but does not reverse the reading.

D-0750
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

C503rd of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: day to day the share price is mostly idiosyncratic (the dashboard's 39-stock insurance basket explains 26% of its daily variance over the latest six-month window, with a beta of 1.02 and a correlation of 0.51; through 2025 and the first half of 2026 it explained 0% to 11%), but the level of the multiple is cohort-driven: Linea Directa sits at the 8th to 10th percentile of its own ten-year 24-month-forward P/E range (10.9x to 11.0x) alongside every retail personal-lines peer (Admiral 13.0x at the 9th percentile, Tryg 13.1x at the 4th, Sampo 13.5x at the 8th, Gjensidige 14.5x at the 18th) while the composites and life insurers sit at the top of theirs (Allianz 95th, Generali 98th, Mapfre 85th, Unipol 96th, Swiss Life 100th). The multiple is therefore about two-thirds industry-driven (the whole motor and retail P&C cohort has been de-rated on a peaked cycle, shoppable earnings and AI-agent fears) and about one-third company-driven (a further 2 to 3 turns of discount to the cohort for size, float, Mutua, disclosure and the 2023 memory). The rise in co-movement since August 2026 (R-squared from 6% in April to 31% in September) coincides with the sector-wide AI-agent narrative and the risk-off regime, which means the stock has recently been trading more on the cohort's story than on its own prints; the six-month return of +3.4% against the basket's +12.4% shows that when it does move with the sector it has lagged it.

The co-movement evidence (the co-movement table, computed 3-Nov-26).

  • Latest 126-day window (12-May-26 to 3-Nov-26): correlation 0.51, beta 1.02, R-squared 26%, idiosyncratic share 74%.
  • The month-end series: R-squared 6% (December 2024), 9% to 11% through mid-2025, falling to 0% to 2% from October 2025 to March 2026 (the period of the H1 and FY25 beats, when the stock traded on its own numbers), then rising to 6% (April), 11% (June), 16% (July), 22% (August), 31% (September), 26% (October and November). Beta moved from 0.13 to 1.04 over the same run.
  • By peer, the highest correlations are with large composites and life names (Allianz 0.42, Zurich 0.42, Legal and General 0.40, Standard Life 0.40, Swiss Life 0.39, PZU 0.39), with Mapfre at 0.37 and the direct motor and Nordic P&C peers lower (Admiral 0.19, Tryg 0.32, Gjensidige 0.30, Sampo 0.25). The stock co-moves with the sector as a financial, not with its business-model peers as a motor insurer, consistent with a holder base that treats it as a small Spanish financial and with the fragility tilt (small, illiquid names sold in risk-off).
  • Returns: six months +3.4% against the basket +12.4%; twelve months +9.1% against the sector median of about +11.7% (first set); relative strength at the 50th percentile; technical momentum graded D; 50-day average 1.28 above the price of 1.226; the 200-day rising in only four of the last twelve months.

The multiple: cohort against company.

Linea Directa Retail P&C cohort (Admiral, Tryg, Sampo, Gjensidige, Alm. Brand) Composites and life (Allianz, Generali, Mapfre, Unipol, AXA, Zurich)
P/E now (dashboard) 11.0x 12.9x to 14.5x 8.8x to 12.4x
Percentile of own ten-year range 10 4 to 34 (Protector at 99 is the exception) 75 to 98
Sell-side and technical momentum (pinned) D, D C to D, B to D B to C, B

Reading: the cohort trades at the bottom of its history (a sector-level judgement that motor and retail P&C margins have peaked and that earnings can be shopped, reinforced by the AI-agent notes of September and October 2026), and Linea Directa trades 2 to 3 turns below the cohort. The discount to the cohort is the company-driven part: market value of EUR 1.3bn against Admiral's about GBP 11bn, free float about 30% to 38%, USD 1.1m to 1.3m a day traded, five to six covering brokers, a CEO absent from Q&A, a mutual leader freezing prices in its home market, and a 2023 loss that the cohort's leaders did not suffer. The first set's C-50 (a justified 12x to 14x on a normalised CR of 92% to 93%) and the model's guideline fair multiple (12.38x) both sit at the cohort's current level, which is itself depressed: the guideline does not ask the cohort to re-rate, only Linea Directa to close part of its discount to it.

Why this matters for predictability and for the case.

  • The forecasts matter for the price. With 74% of daily variance idiosyncratic and coverage thin, prints move the stock: the beats of late 2025 and early 2026 coincided with the lowest co-movement in the series. The model's base (a FY26 beat, then a flat FY27 below consensus) therefore predicts the direction of the company-driven part of the price with reasonable confidence: supportive into February 2027, adverse from April to October 2027.
  • The cohort sets the ceiling. A re-rating of Linea Directa to the guideline 12.38x needs either the cohort to re-rate (a sector call outside this work: it would need the AI-agent and peaked-cycle narratives to fade) or the company to close its own discount (capital return, disclosure, a CEO-led investor day, proof through the 2027 Baremo test). None of the company-driven catalysts is dated.
  • The recent rise in co-movement is a warning: the stock is being traded as part of a de-rated cohort in a risk-off tape, so sector sentiment shocks (an AI-agent headline, a UK motor profit warning, a Nordic P&C de-rating) will move it more than a year ago, whatever its own numbers. That lowers the predictability of the price, not of the earnings.
  • The model's return at the guideline multiple (+21.6% to December 2027, of which 8.8 points are dividends) is consistent with this: earnings plus dividends with a small re-rating, not a multiple story.
