Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

C+556th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the precedents say motor insurers outperform when rate runs ahead of claims inflation and earned margins are still rising (about six quarters after the rate turn), and underperform when repricing has peaked, when claims inflation surprises, when reserves disappoint, or when a terminal-value fear takes hold; Linea Directa fits the "repricing has peaked, margin stable, share de-rated" pattern, which historically produces dividend-plus-growth returns rather than a re-rating, unless a catalyst (capital return, bid, proof of durable margins through an inflation test) arrives.

  • Rules of thumb (Insurance Sector Digest). "The motor cycle is the cleanest cycle in the Sector: a year of rate rises above claims inflation gives about six quarters of rising earned margin; buy before the print, leave a market whose repricing has peaked even if its shares are down." Linea Directa's own repricing peaked in 2024; its earned-margin tailwind is largely used up by end-2026. The digest explicitly says "treat Spain as a stable rather than expanding margin".
  • Outperformance precedents. Admiral over two decades (structural cost advantage, a 20-point CR advantage, 53% average ROE, capital returned); Mapfre +53% in the twelve months to April 2026 on a turnaround with repeated target upgrades; Unipol +38% on motor repricing plus corporate change. All combined margin gains with upgrades and capital returns.
  • Underperformance precedents. Linea Directa itself in 2022 to 2023 (pricing lag in an inflation shock, a loss, dividend suspended); Direct Line (lost discipline, then acquired by Aviva); Admiral's 45% relative de-rating on autonomous-vehicle fears despite rising earnings; Nordic P&C (-6.9% over three months) on a peaked cycle plus shopping-agent fears; UK motor in 2025 to 2026 (rate cuts, then losses).
  • Where Linea Directa fits. It has the cost-leader profile (Admiral-like) but without Admiral's capital-light quota-share structure (ROE 23% against about 50%) and without scale. Its share price (+9.1% over twelve months, against the sector median +11.7% and the cohort +10.7%) has lagged its earnings (+19% in H1 26), so the multiple compressed to the 10th percentile. This matches the "peaked repricing" pattern, not a broken franchise.
  • What history says re-rates such a stock. Proof that margins hold through the next inflation step (as Admiral's did between 2010 and 2020), a step-up in capital returns, or a bid. Absent these, expect total returns of about earnings growth plus dividend yield (roughly 8% to 10% plus 4% to 5%).

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

A561st of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: on the multiple alone the downside is limited (about 10% to 15%) because the stock is already at the 8th to 10th percentile of its own range; the real downside is earnings-driven: in a 2027 claims-inflation step the shares could fall to about EUR 0.85 to 0.95 (23% to 31% below EUR 1.23); the Risks report's EUR 0.40 case requires capitalising a trough as permanent and is implausible.

  • Trough multiple. Five-year low of the 24-month-forward P/E 9.44x; ten-year 10th percentile 11.0x (the dashboard's "crash" band); Mapfre traded at about 9.3x in past downturns. A fair trough multiple is about 9.5x to 10x.
  • Case 1, multiple only. 9.5x to 10x on today's 24-month-forward EPS of about EUR 0.111: EUR 1.05 to 1.11, a fall of 10% to 15%.
  • Case 2, claims-inflation step (CR up 2 to 3 points in FY27, recovering in FY28). Forward EPS cut to about EUR 0.090 to 0.095; at 9.5x to 10x: EUR 0.86 to 0.95, a fall of 23% to 30%.
  • Case 3, 2022 to 2023 rerun (CR above 100% for a year). The market would capitalise recovery earnings of about EUR 0.08 at about 9.5x to 10x: about EUR 0.76 to 0.80, a fall of about 35% to 38%. The Risks report's case (EPS EUR 0.04 at 10x, EUR 0.40) assumes the trough is permanent; with solvency at 196% and the 2024 recovery record, that is not a fair trough.
  • Cushions. A dividend yield of about 4.5% (FY26e) to 5.3% (FY27e); a 52-week low of EUR 1.04; controlling shareholders who have been buying.
  • For the second set: the probability-weighted downside is dominated by Case 2; Case 1 alone is small.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

F4815th of 19

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: the narrative ahead is "steady compounding confirmed, quarter by quarter", not a new story: the dated positives are confirmations of known trends (growth, profit milestones, dividends), and the most important dated event in the next six months, the 2027 Baremo update, is a risk rather than a catalyst; a re-rating from the 10th percentile needs either proof that the CR holds through the 2027 inflation step (visible from H1 27) or a step in capital returns, both outside the next six months unless management chooses to act at FY26 results.

  • For the fourth set: the market narrative (margin peak, shoppable earnings, a mutual competitor, a small illiquid stock) is consistent with the evidence on the margin trend but understates the cost advantage, the capital capacity and the gap between today's margin and the pre-2022 norm.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–70

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the two earlier sets are highly congruent, and the Q3 26 results have resolved almost every point on which they differed in the second set's favour; the base case they share (a cost-advantaged direct insurer compounding volume at about 7% a year with the combined ratio held in a band of 91% to 92%, earnings about flat in FY27 and growing again in FY28, and the payout rising as growth slows) is supported from four independent directions (the external forces, the company's own behaviour and incentives, the precedents, and the half-year calibration of the model). The three places where the sets jar are the expense ratio (both too conservative, now corrected), the size of the capital surplus (the second set too generous, corrected above), and the first set's "flat CR in FY27" against the second set's "+0.6 points" (the second set's version is the one the Q3 data and the Baremo formula support). None of the differences changes the direction of the case; they change its arithmetic by a few percent.

Where the sets support each other (the load-bearing agreements).

  • The phase of the company. The first set's single most important idea (margin recovery has given way to volume-for-margin, pricing at about 2% against a market at about 6%) is the premise of the second set's model (written motor price about 1.5% in 2026, policies +7.3%). Q3 26 confirms it: motor average premium about +1.5%, policies +7.9%, group CR 91.7% with the loss ratio flat underneath. Three independent lines of evidence point the same way: the incentive structure (70% of annual targets on written premiums; 50% of the long-term plan on the average CR, 40% on premium growth against the sector), management's words ("we do not need to deteriorate combined ratios to drive commercial volume growth"; "leverage cost efficiency to offer competitive pricing") and the observed numbers.
  • The one risk. Both sets name the same dominant risk (a claims-inflation step in 2027 while the company prices below the market) and the same dated test (the 2027 Baremo update). The first set said "likely higher than 2.7%"; the second set said about 3.5%; the pension formula now gives about 3.3% to 3.5%. The sets agree, and the world has delivered the number they expected.
  • The margin as plateau, not peak. The first set (C-53) showed the net margin of 8.5% to 9% is below the 12% to 15% of 2019 to 2021 and the CR of about 91% is near the historic 90% target; the second set (P-08, P-09) showed the pre-2022 CR was about 86% to 88% and FY30 net income is still below the FY20 peak on 70% more revenue. The market's framing ("margin peak") is contradicted consistently by both sets, which matters for the fourth set.
  • Capital policy. Both sets: dividends, no buyback in the base case, payout rising with slowing growth, controlling shareholders passive. The OWN-4 report adds the reason (Cartival's cash needs; management "extremely cautious"), which strengthens the agreement.
  • Management. Both sets: operator-type, credible after delivery, late in 2022, fast in 2023 to 2024, thin on disclosure and investor access. The Q3 26 call (no evidence of a change in access) and the slight-slowdown comment on acquisition fit that picture of candour without building blocks.
  • The model's calibration. The second set reproduced H1 26 exactly; the Q3 print (nine-month net income EUR 80.4m against the second set's expected EUR 77m to 80m; GWP +8.9% against expected +8.5% to 9%; Q3 CR 91.7% against expected about 91% to 92%) sits inside every range the second set set in advance. That is the strongest congruence test available: the model predicted the next print before it happened.

Where the sets jar, and how each is resolved.

  1. Source of the H1 26 margin gain. First set: selection, frequency and expense. Second set: home weather and health, with motor flat. Q3 26: nine-month motor loss ratio 73.3% against 74.3%, but about 0.75 points of that is the Q3 25 severe claims, so motor is flat to marginally better; home 86.8% (benign); health -6.9 points. The second set is right, and the memo should say the motor loss ratio has not improved in 2026.
  2. Expense ratio. First set: "below 20% not evidenced on a full-year basis"; second set: reached after FY30. Q3 26: 19.4%; nine months 20.0%; motor expense ratio 18.0%. Both sets under-called the operating leverage that the company is retaining in 2026. The resolution is in the second set's own mechanism: the leverage is a buffer management can spend; in 2026, with the loss ratio flat, it has not needed to spend it. That is the detail that raises FY26 and that makes FY27 more robust than the second set's arithmetic implies, because a company at 20.0% has 1 to 1.5 points of marketing it can trim before the reported CR moves.
  3. FY27 CR. First set: broadly flat; second set: +0.6 points to 92.0%. The Q3 evidence cuts both ways (motor loss ratio not improving, written price still about 1.5%, parts +5.1%, Mapfre strengthening reserves and turning to volume: all for the second set; expense ratio and health better than modelled: against it). I keep 92.0% as the base, with the reasons in D-04. The first set's "flat" is now the upside case, not the base.
  4. Capital surplus. Second set: about 197% through the forecast with EUR 45m to 60m spare. Q3 26: 191.5% after a EUR 27m valuation hit; about 180% to 185% at end-2026 after the final dividend. The second set over-stated the cushion because it did not model the rate sensitivity it had itself quoted (-9.6 points per 100 basis points). The first set's C-19 (a 60% payout holds the ratio flat; 75% lets it drift) is closer to right. Resolution: payout path kept, buyback option removed, the ratio's new floor is the 180% reference itself.
  5. Downside share price. First set C-83 Case 2 (EUR 0.86 to 0.95) and the second set's downside EPS (EUR 0.082) do not use the same earnings; see reading note 4. Resolution: the second set's EPS at the first set's trough multiple.
  6. Investment yield. First set: rising with reinvestment. Second set: falling (2.75% at June). Q3 26: total return 3.14% for nine months against 3.31%, reinvestment yield 2.62% rising 13 basis points, financial result +14.1% on a larger portfolio. Both are partly right: the yield on the book is drifting down, the euros are rising with the portfolio. The second set's modelling choice (growth from size, not yield) is the right one and is unchanged.

One tension that is in the world, not in the answers. Both sets say inflation hurts Linea Directa's margin (C-05, P-04) and helps its volume (C-08, P-04's "umbrella widens in an inflation step"). These are not contradictory: the second set resolves them by timing (the margin effect lands in 2027 through the earned-price lag; the volume effect persists while incumbents raise prices and Mutua is pushed toward its 97% ceiling). The Consumer should present them as one mechanism with two lags, not as two views.

What congruence does for predictability. When several independent analyses, built on different evidence, land on the same base case and the first out-of-sample print lands inside their ranges, the risk that the forecasts rest on a misreading of the business is low. The residual risk is not that the analysts disagree but that they share a blind spot; the candidates are (a) reserve development (neither set could see it; the OWN-3 report's EUR 15.9m surplus is the first number), (b) the competitive response of Mapfre and AXA in 2027 (the OWN-6 report is the first evidence that the soft-market phase may begin in 2027 rather than 2028), and (c) the written price the company actually charges at the January 2027 renewal, which neither set can observe directly. All three are tracked in D-05 and researched in the requests.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

C-484th of 8

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the narrative around Linea Directa is thin, Spanish and headline-driven, and it articulates the negatives of our assessment more fully than the positives: the dynamics that are well known are Mutua's tariff freeze (the most discussed single item, in the Spanish press and on the company's own calls), the Baremo as a risk (generic in broker and press commentary), the strength of the balance sheet, Bankinter's 17.42% stake as an overhang (bank analysts at Jefferies, Deutsche Bank and Rothschild Redburn), and, at sector level, the AI shopping-agent and peaked-cycle stories (Morgan Stanley on the Nordics in September 2026, DNB Carnegie in October, Goldman Sachs on Tuio and the Spanish brokers in February 2026); the dynamics that are known but not modelled are the company's 2% pricing against a 6% market (said by the CFO in April 2026, not carried into FY27 consensus), the normal home combined ratio of 90% to 91% (said by the CFO in February 2026, not carried into consensus) and health breakeven (assumed "late 2027" without a plan); and the dynamics discussed by no one are the 2027 earned-price scissors as a quantified FY27 effect, the flat motor loss ratio beneath the group figure, the reserve confidence level at the 85th percentile, the benign arithmetic of the pension-linked Baremo formula (3.33% against fears of 4% plus), the capacity for a 65% to 70% payout, the "margin plateau below the company's own history, not a peak" framing, and, for 18 months to three years out, the soft-market turn signalled by Mapfre's 2027 to 2029 volume plan and AXA's double-digit direct-growth target for Spain. Investor commentary on Twitter is effectively absent for a EUR 1.3bn Spanish small-cap; the AlphaSense corpus holds no individual broker notes on the company, so the sell-side narrative must be inferred from consensus numbers, ratings and call questions, which themselves show a market that is positioned for a flat combined ratio and a mutual price war, that is, long on the near-term earnings risk and short on the medium-term capital and recovery story.

Coverage, as far as it can be established. Brokers with estimates in FactSet: UBS, Bestinver, JB Capital, Renta 4, Santander and one restricted house (eight firms in total at end-2025). Regular participants on the calls (I2 report): CaixaBank, JB Capital, Alantra and Santander. Bank analysts who discuss the company through Bankinter: Jefferies, Deutsche Bank, Rothschild Redburn. AlphaSense holds none of the covering brokers' notes on Linea Directa itself, and the I2 report's claim to cover "about 85%" of houses rests on call transcripts rather than research documents; the Consumer should treat the sell-side narrative below as inferred, and the first research request asks for the notes themselves.

14. (Secondary) The margin as a plateau below the company's own history (pre-2022 CR 86% to 88%); FY30 net income still below FY20 Not articulated anywhere; the market's phrase is "margin peak" Not discussed Unknown Favourable: the strongest unarticulated positive, because it reframes the 10th-percentile multiple

The shape of the narrative, in one paragraph. The market's story is: a well-run, cheap, over-capitalised direct insurer whose margin has peaked, whose growth is bought with low prices under a mutual that will not raise them, whose earnings can be shopped by comparison sites and soon by AI agents, and whose shares are small, illiquid and partly owned by a bank that might sell. Every element of that story is in our assessment too. What the story leaves out is the arithmetic that follows from it: that the low prices of 2026 cost about 0.6 points of CR in 2027 (so consensus is too high), that the Baremo formula has already delivered a benign 2027 number (so the feared shock is smaller than the fear), that the payout can rise to 65% to 70% as growth slows (so the dividend yield rises toward 5% to 6% on our FY28 DPS), that the margin is 4 to 6 points below the company's own history (so "peak" is the wrong word), and that the incumbents' turn to volume in 2027 to 2029 is the real medium-term threat (which nobody has yet connected to Linea Directa). The narrative is therefore accurate but incomplete, and the incompleteness is asymmetric by horizon: the unknown negative (the scissors) lands within six to twelve months; the unknown positives (Baremo arithmetic, payout, plateau) land within three to four months or over three years.

Peers' narratives, for the read-across. Admiral: the autonomous-vehicle de-rating and the UK soft market, now "Traffic has cleared" (Morgan Stanley upgrade, September 2026): a cohort whose narrative turned on reserve releases and pricing discipline. Nordic P&C (Gjensidige, Tryg): "Northern Lights are fading" (Morgan Stanley, July 2026) and the AI-agent test; the cohort's multiple at the 4th to 18th percentile. Sabre: "growth momentum slows" (Jefferies, October 2026, estimates cut 5% to 7%). Mapfre: the upgrade story (targets raised March 2026; motor earnings doubled) with the Q3 26 reserve addition as the first blemish. Unipol: Italian motor pricing ahead of inflation. Mutua: the press lionises the freeze. Bankinter: the stake is a footnote in a "premium can return" narrative. Linea Directa sits in the Nordic and Admiral cohort's narrative on the multiple (shoppable, peaked) and in the Spanish press narrative on competition (Mutua), with no narrative of its own beyond the quarterly beat headline ("gana 80,4 millones, un 34,8% más").

Score. The gap is close to neutral: the market knows the negatives and has priced them in the multiple but not in the estimates; it does not know the positives, which are smaller in EPS terms but earlier in time. The unarticulated items favour the long case over three years and the short case over the next twelve months.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 25 October 2026, 21:10 to 21:29 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Linea Directa Aseguradora (LDA-ES): first set of questions, foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-10-25 by the Investment Analyst answering the first set, for the Investment Analyst building the operating and financial model (second set).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer

What the company is. A pure direct-to-consumer Spanish personal-lines insurer (telephone, web, app; no branches, no brokers; one site at Tres Cantos, Madrid). Gross written premiums (GWP) of EUR 1,134.7m in FY25: motor 81.4% (EUR 924.0m), home 14.8% (EUR 167.8m), health under the Vivaz brand 3.4% (EUR 38.8m), other lines 0.3%. Spain is over 99.9% of premiums. Owns its repair centres (CAR, Madrid and Barcelona). Spun off from Bankinter in April 2021.

The figures used throughout (sources in brackets).

  • FY25: GWP +11.3%; insurance revenue EUR 1,122m (FactSet); combined ratio (CR) 92.6% = loss ratio 71.5% + expense ratio 21.1%; net income EUR 85.7m (+33.5%); EPS EUR 0.079; return on average equity 22.9%; dividends EUR 45m (53% payout); Solvency II 182.6% after the final dividend (company, February 2026).
  • History of the CR: FY22 96.3%, FY23 104.1% (net loss EUR 4.4m), FY24 94.7%, FY25 92.6%. Net income FY19 to FY25 (FactSet, EUR m): 107, 135, 110, 63.1, -4.4, 64.2, 85.7.
  • H1 26 (reported 27-Jul-26): GWP EUR 609.3m (+9.2% against a Spanish non-life market of +5.5%); net income EUR 52.1m (+19.0%); CR 91.1% (loss ratio 70.9%, expense ratio 20.2%); Q1 CR 91.7% (loss ratio 72.7%, expense ratio 19.0%); Q2 CR 90.6%; Solvency II 196.3% after the EUR 18m interim dividend; 3.86m policyholders (+7.8%). Segment CR in H1 26: motor 91.1%, home 86.6%, health 125.1%.
  • Derived by this analyst (approximate, assumes similar earned premium in Q1 and Q2): Q2 26 GWP +8.2% (EUR 306.1m against EUR 283.0m), motor +9.1%, home +1.7%; Q2 net income +24.8%; Q2 loss ratio about 69.1% (Q2 25 about 70.9%); Q2 expense ratio about 21.4% (Q2 25 about 21.4%). The whole H1 expense-ratio improvement was in Q1, which management itself said was flattered by seasonally low marketing and IT spend; the Q2 improvement came from the loss ratio. This matters for the model and recurs below.
  • Average motor premium: motor GWP +9.8% against motor policies +8.5% in H1 26, so the average premium rose only about 1.2%; management cited average premium increases of about 2% on new business and renewals in Q1 26, against a market average of about 6%.
  • FactSet consensus (median, four to five brokers): net income EUR 103m / 114m / 122m for FY26 / FY27 / FY28; EPS EUR 0.094 / 0.103 / 0.115; revenue EUR 1,233m / 1,297m / 1,413m; dividend per share EUR 0.055 / 0.065 / 0.069. Consensus FY26 implies H2 26 net income of about EUR 51m against EUR 41.9m in H2 25.
  • Valuation: price EUR 1.226 (23-Oct-26); market capitalisation about EUR 1,338m; 24-month-forward P/E 10.87x, 8th percentile of five years (range 9.44x to 15.90x, median 12.65x) and 10th percentile of ten years. Next results 28-Oct-26.
  • Earnings sensitivity: a 1 percentage point move in the CR moves net profit by about EUR 7.8m (company 2025 sensitivity; the July memo cited EUR 10.49m), that is about 8% to 10% of FY26 consensus net income.

Scores. Each answer ends with a score from 1 to 100: how positive the thing asked about is for an investment case in the company (50 neutral).

The single most important idea to carry into the model. Linea Directa has moved from a margin-recovery phase (FY23 to H1 26, driven by its own heavy repricing in 2023 to 2024) into a volume-for-margin phase in which it deliberately prices below the market (about 2% against about 6%) and uses its cost advantage and its restored margin to take share. The base case is therefore above-market volume growth at a broadly flat combined ratio in the low 90s, not further large margin gains. The main threat to that base case is a renewed claims-inflation step-up in 2027 (energy-led Spanish inflation feeding the Baremo bodily-injury scale and repair costs) arriving while the company is pricing below the market, which is the same configuration that produced the 2022 to 2023 losses, although this time from a much stronger starting margin and with latent pricing headroom.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Linea Directa Aseguradora (LDA-ES): a Spanish pure direct-to-consumer personal-lines insurer selling motor (about 81% of premiums), home (about 15%) and health under the Vivaz brand (about 3%) insurance, plus small pet and SME lines, through telephone, web and app from a single site, with its own repair centres (Centro Avanzado de Reparaciones) and no branches or brokers.

Request: T-P1

Peers: Mapfre (MAP-ES; the largest listed Spanish motor and home competitor, branch and agent model, and owner of the direct brand Verti); Mutua Madrileña (private mutual, Spain's largest auto insurer, investing in AI and parts distribution); Grupo Catalana Occidente (listed Spanish multi-line insurer, agent-led); Admiral Group (ADM-GB; the European benchmark direct motor cost leader, also present in Spain as Admiral Seguros); Sabre Insurance (SBRE-GB; UK single-line motor insurer); Direct Line Insurance Group (now part of Aviva; the precedent of a direct motor insurer whose cost programmes and margins failed); Allianz (ALV-DE; Allianz Seguros in Spain and the low-cost platform Allianz Direct); Generali (G-IT; owner of the Spanish direct insurer Génesis). Inputs: How much of Linea Directa's operating cost base (single-site staff, technology, mass-media and search marketing, acquisition, claims handling) is fixed and how much varies with policies and premiums; how much of the fall in the company's expense ratio from 22.1% (FY24) to 21.1% (FY25) and 20.2% (H1 2026, company results of 27-Jul-26) came from premium growth on a fixed base and how much from cost actions; what the CFO's statement in the Q1 2026 call (27-Apr-26) that the quarter benefited from seasonally lower marketing, media, IT and acquisition costs, and that technology expenses should rise, implies for the quarterly and full-year expense ratio, including the Q2 2026 expense ratio against Q2 2025; the building blocks and timeline behind management's medium-term target of an expense ratio below 20%; how Spanish motor insurers' combined ratios moved with volume and pricing in past cycles, dated with figures (the flat average premiums of 2015 to 2022, the 2016 Baremo reform, the 2022 to 2023 claims-inflation shock and the 2024 to 2026 recovery), and how quickly direct insurers restored margins compared with agent-based ones; whether customer redirection to the company's owned and partner repair network has recovered since the 50% reported for 2022 (from 71% in 2021), and what evidence supports the company's claim of an average repair cost about EUR 200 below the Spanish market; the cost per acquired customer and its trend as search-engine and comparison-site costs rise; cost programmes at the named peers, what was promised and what was delivered and over what period (Admiral's target of GBP 100m of AI efficiencies by 2028, Allianz's productivity target of 30 basis points a year, Mapfre's expense-ratio target below 28%, Direct Line's cost programmes before its acquisition); which cost lines proved cuttable (marketing, technology, claims handling, staff) and what happened to growth and retention when marketing was cut; the cost burden of DORA, the EU AI Act and CSRD for a mid-sized insurer; and the operating leverage in the health line given its 50% quota share, whose reinsurance commissions were removed by a 2023 addendum. Focus: How much fixed-cost operating leverage the single-site direct model really carries as premiums grow, how much of the recent expense-ratio improvement is structural rather than seasonal or reinvested in lower prices, and how wide the range of combined-ratio outcomes is when Spanish motor claims inflation shifts.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Mutua Madrileña (private mutual and Spain's auto leader, which froze tariffs for a large share of its auto customers in 2025 and 2026); Mapfre (MAP-ES; repriced and shed unprofitable motor volume in 2025 and 2026); Grupo Catalana Occidente (raised motor premiums in 2025 without a reported rise in cancellations); Allianz (ALV-DE; Allianz Seguros' renewal price increases in Spain); AXA (CS-FR; AXA Seguros and the direct brand Direct Seguros in Spain); Admiral Group (ADM-GB; UK direct motor pricing through the 2022 to 2026 cycle and its Spanish operation); Sabre Insurance (SBRE-GB; UK motor pricing discipline); Direct Line Insurance Group (now Aviva; the UK precedent of pricing behaviour in a soft market); Unipol (UNI-IT; Italian motor pricing against inflation). Operating conditions: a renewed rise in Spanish claims inflation (energy-led consumer inflation feeding repair parts, labour and the annual update of the Baremo bodily-injury compensation scale); a large mutual competitor freezing prices while the rest of the market raises them; a soft market once industry margins recover; a household affordability squeeze or recession that drives shopping and trading down to minimum cover; a rising share of policies bought through price comparison sites and AI shopping agents; and a major competitor shedding volume to restore margins. Inputs: Linea Directa's average premium change by line (motor, home, health) for each year from 2021 to 2026, split between new business and renewals where available, against the Spanish market average reported by ICEA and against named peers; the gap between the company's average motor price increase of about 2% and the market's of about 6% cited in its Q1 2026 call (27-Apr-26), and whether the gap has changed since; the response of customer numbers and churn to the company's 2023 to 2024 price increases (the company reported a temporary 4.5% fall in customers in early 2024 and motor churn close to 14% in Q1 2026) and the implied price elasticity; how long the company took to pass claims inflation into prices in 2022 to 2023 compared with Mapfre, Mutua, Allianz and Grupo Catalana Occidente; how the Baremo update is set (its link to pension revaluation and consumer inflation), when the 2027 update will be published, and how updates affect open claims as well as new business; why home premiums grew 2.0% while home policies grew 3.7% in H1 2026 (company results, 27-Jul-26), and whether home average premiums are falling; health pricing after the switch of the medical network to HNA in July 2026; Mutua's stated policy of freezing tariffs (for 30% of auto policyholders in 2025 and for up to 50% in 2026, per CE Noticias Financieras of 22-Jul-26) and its stated combined-ratio ceiling of about 97%, and how far other insurers have followed or ignored it; what brokers, comparison sites (Rastreator, Acierto) and expert calls say about price elasticity and switching on aggregators and AI agents in Spain; any Spanish regulatory constraint on renewal pricing comparable with the UK ban on price walking; and the UK precedents of 2022 to 2026 (rate increases ahead of claims inflation, then the 2025 rate cuts and the losses that followed) as a guide to how direct insurers behave in a soft market. Focus: Whether Linea Directa can raise motor prices toward the market's level if claims inflation re-accelerates in 2027 without losing the above-market volume growth that underpins its case, how quickly it can do so, and how its pricing power holds up while it prices below the market and a mutual competitor freezes tariffs.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: The case rests on a reinforcing loop (low costs fund low prices that win customers whose premiums spread fixed costs further) and on balancing loops (customers won on price leave when prices rise; competitors respond to share loss; growth consumes capital and may dilute risk selection), and which of these dominates decides whether growth at a stable margin can persist. Peers: Admiral Group (ADM-GB; the cost-led direct motor loop in the UK over two decades); Progressive (PGR-US) and GEICO (part of Berkshire Hathaway; the canonical US direct motor cost-and-scale loops); Mutua Madrileña (the main competitive response in Spain, through tariff freezes and AI pricing); Mapfre (MAP-ES; the incumbent response through offices, bank and retail partnerships and technology spending); Direct Line Insurance Group (now Aviva; a loop that reversed); Sabre Insurance (SBRE-GB; a margin-first, volume-second UK motor insurer). Inputs: Start from these possible dynamics: the loop from a lower expense ratio to lower prices to more policies to lower unit costs, and the evidence for it at Linea Directa in 2024 to 2026 (customer base up 8.5% in 2025 to 3.73 million and to 3.86 million in June 2026, company releases); the loop from more customers to more claims data to better risk selection to a lower loss ratio; the cross-selling loop from the motor base into home, health and new lines, and evidence that multi-product customers retain better (Admiral reports a retention benefit for its MultiCover customers); the balancing loop in which customers acquired on price churn when prices rise (the 2023 to 2024 precedent at Linea Directa); the balancing loop in which share gains provoke competitor responses (Mutua's freezes, Mapfre's new offices and its Carrefour and Santander partnerships); the balancing loop in which premium growth raises the Solvency II capital requirement; the balancing loop in which fast growth lowers the quality of new business, and whether new-business loss ratios at direct insurers run above renewal loss ratios; the loop between rising search and comparison-site costs and the cost of growth; and how lower reserve prudence (management's stated move of the provisioning confidence level from the 95th back to the 85th percentile) interacts with reported margins over a cycle. Focus: Which loop is likely to dominate for Linea Directa over the next 18 months to four years: the cost-price-volume loop that would let it keep growing above the Spanish market at a stable margin, or the balancing loops of price-sensitive churn, competitor response and selection dilution that would cap its growth or its margin, and what past cases show about how long such loops run and what stops them.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Linea Directa gives no numerical earnings, premium or payout guidance and does not disclose claims frequency, severity or prior-year reserve development, so the inputs to its earnings per share must be established from the evidence before they can be modelled. Peers: Mapfre (MAP-ES; Iberian motor and home combined ratios and expense ratio as the local benchmark); Mutua Madrileña (combined-ratio policy and growth); Grupo Catalana Occidente (Spanish non-life combined ratios and investment yields); Admiral Group (ADM-GB; reserve releases as a share of premium and their effect on reported combined ratios); Sabre Insurance (SBRE-GB; single-line motor earnings drivers); Unipol (UNI-IT; motor pricing against inflation and reserving prudence); Allianz (ALV-DE; Allianz Direct expense ratio). Inputs: Earned premium growth by segment against written premium growth, and the lag between them; the drivers of the motor loss ratio (claims frequency, average claim cost, Baremo updates, parts and labour inflation, electric-vehicle repair costs) and their recent trend; the contribution of prior-year reserve releases and of the change in the provisioning confidence level to the combined ratios of FY24, FY25 and H1 2026; the path of the expense ratio against the target below 20%; the health combined ratio path to breakeven (125.1% in H1 2026, company results of 27-Jul-26), the terms of the 50% health quota share running to December 2029, and the expected effect of the HNA network; the normal home combined ratio across weather years and the role of Spain's Insurance Compensation Consortium; investment income (a fixed-income portfolio of about EUR 1.05bn at 30-Jun-26, its yield, duration and reinvestment rates, and the resumption of rental income from the renovated Madrid building, which management expects from June 2027); the tax rate and the outcome of the 2020 to 2022 tax audits; capital and payout (Solvency II ratio of 196.3% at 30-Jun-26, the sensitivity of the capital requirement to premium growth, the effect of the revised Solvency II rules from January 2027 on a short-tail non-life insurer, the dividend policy and any consideration of buybacks); the share count and employee share plans; and what the named brokers (UBS, JB Capital, Bestinver, Renta 4, Santander) assume for combined ratio, premium growth and payout, and how far those assumptions differ from management's commentary. Focus: What combined ratio, earned premium growth, investment yield and payout the evidence supports for 2027 and 2028, and how much of the 2024 to 2026 improvement in the combined ratio came from reserve releases or the lower provisioning level rather than from current-year underwriting.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 26 October 2026, 09:37 to 09:52 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Linea Directa Aseguradora (LDA-ES): second set of questions, the operating and financial model for FY26 to FY30

Written 2026-10-26 by the Investment Analyst answering the second set, for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets (predictability; guidance, consensus and narrative).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer

The central conclusion in one paragraph. Linea Directa's revenue is highly forecastable and its margin is not. Revenue (earned premium) will grow about 10% in FY26, 7.4% in FY27 and 5.5% to 7% a year to FY30, because the earned line lags the written line by about six months and the written line (about 8% now) is decelerating slowly and predictably as policy growth slows and average price rises. The combined ratio (CR) is the one variable that matters, and my base case is that it rises from about 91.4% in FY26 to about 92.0% in FY27 before easing to about 91.7% in FY28 and drifting toward 92% to 92.2% by FY29 to FY30. That FY27 step is not a crisis; it is the arithmetic of a company that priced at about 1% to 2% in 2026 while claims costs ran at 3% or more, with a home book that has had two unusually benign weather years. The result is that FY27 net income is close to flat (about EUR 106m against about EUR 104m in FY26), about 7% below the FactSet consensus of EUR 114m, before growth resumes at 7% to 10% in FY28 and slows to 3% to 4% a year in FY29 to FY30. Five-year EPS growth from FY25 (EUR 0.079) to FY30 (about EUR 0.114) is about 7.7% a year, with ROE of 22% to 25% and a payout rising from 53% to about 70%.

The base-case numbers (my model, EUR m; FactSet revenue basis, explained below).

Four basis traps the Consumer must not fall into.

  1. Consensus "revenue" is gross written premiums, not FactSet's revenue line. FactSet's brokers'-basis revenue actuals are EUR 973m (FY23), 1,020m (FY24) and 1,130m (FY25), which match reported GWP (about EUR 973m, 1,019.5m and 1,134.7m). FactSet's history revenue (EUR 1,122m in FY25) is earned premium (EUR 1,076.7m, which equals the sum of FactSet's four segment lines) plus about EUR 45m of investment and other income. So consensus FY26 revenue of EUR 1,233m is GWP growth of +8.7%, and FY27's EUR 1,297m is GWP growth of only +5.2%. The first set's C-67 table read these consensus figures against the earned history; that mixed two bases. My GWP (EUR 1,229m, 1,316m, 1,403m) is in line with consensus for FY26, 1.5% above it for FY27 and 0.7% below it for FY28.
  2. The brokers'-basis "EBITDA" (EUR -38.2m, 51.2m, 77.4m for FY23 to FY25) is the technical result (earned premium times one minus the CR), and the brokers' "EBIT" (EUR 136m, 152m) is close to pre-tax profit. In this form, operating profit is pre-tax profit, the investment result sits inside it, and net financial items are about zero (the company has EUR 1.6m of lease debt and no borrowings).
  3. FactSet's consensus debt of EUR 75.5m in FY26 to FY28 is a broker artefact; the company has no financial debt.
  4. FactSet dates dividends by payment period. My dividend per share is the dividend declared for each fiscal year.

Calibration. The model reproduces H1 26 exactly: earned premium of about EUR 575m at a CR of 91.1% gives a technical result of about EUR 51m; add the investment result of EUR 21.8m and other items of about EUR -3.5m to get pre-tax profit of about EUR 69.5m, and net income of EUR 52.1m at a 25% tax rate. On FY27 earned premium of about EUR 1,275m, each 1 point of CR is worth about EUR 12.8m of pre-tax profit and EUR 9.6m of net income (about 9% of FY27 net income); the company's own 2025 sensitivity (EUR 7.8m) was struck on a smaller premium base.

Where I disagree with, or correct, the first set (each is used below).

  • The source of the H1 26 margin gain. The first set read the CR improvement (92.3% to 91.1%) as coming from selection, frequency and expenses. The segment data show the motor loss ratio was flat (72.9% in H1 26 against 72.8% in H1 25); the group loss-ratio gain came from home (loss ratio down 3.2 points to 58.3% on benign weather) and health (CR down 8.6 points), and the expense gain came entirely in Q1. Motor, 81% of the book, is not improving; it is holding. That changes the FY27 call from "flat CR" to "CR up about 0.6 points".
  • The HNA health network. The agreement was signed in July 2026, but the new network takes effect in Q1 2027 (CE Noticias Financieras, 29-Jul-26), so its claims-cost benefit falls in 2027, not H2 26.
  • The health quota share. The first set said it has paid no commissions since a 2023 addendum. The 2025 annual report (6-Apr-26) says a 2024 addendum established a management-expenses commission and profit sharing on positive technical results; the health expense ratio fell from 50.1% (H1 25) to 41.3% (H1 26). Profit sharing means part of any health improvement is shared with Munich Re as the line nears breakeven.
  • Investment yield. The first set expected the yield to rise as the portfolio rolls. The latest data show the opposite: the trailing twelve-month portfolio return fell to 2.75% at June 2026 from 3.12% for FY25, and duration was shortened from 3.86 years to 3.53 years. Investment income growth in the model comes from the size of the portfolio and the return of rental income, not from yield.
  • Capital figures. At 30-Jun-26 eligible own funds were EUR 491.5m against a solvency capital requirement (SCR) of EUR 250.3m (196.3%); the first set derived an SCR of about EUR 240m from the year-end figures.
  • Two AlphaSense errors to avoid repeating. (1) The operating-leverage report says Linea Directa is exempt from the sustainability reporting directive after the Omnibus I package; it is not, because the new thresholds are more than 1,000 employees and more than EUR 450m of turnover and Linea Directa has about 2,400 employees and EUR 1.1bn of premiums. (2) The EPS-bridge report quotes CAR repair costs rising 2% against 6% for the market as current evidence; the quote is from the Q3 2021 call. It also quotes a "94% to 95%" CR target from October 2025, which has since been replaced by "low 90s".

Scores. Each answer ends with a score from 1 to 100: how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case (50 neutral).

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

The company reports gross written premiums (GWP) and the combined ratio (CR) for motor, home and health each quarter; FactSet's segment lines are earned premium (FY25: motor 873, home 164, health 37.0, other 2.8, total 1,076.8). Health CRs are net of the 50% quota share with Munich Re (to December 2029), so health carries about 2% of the group CR weight although it is about 3.5% of GWP.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue (FactSet basis: earned premium plus investment and other income). Earned premium lags written by about six months (triangular weighting: an earned half reflects about a quarter of the written growth two halves earlier, half of the previous half and a quarter of the current half). Written growth slows from 9.2% (H1 26) to about 7.5% (H2 26), 7.0% (FY27), 6.6% (FY28), 5.8% (FY29) and 5.4% (FY30), so earned premium grows 10.3%, 7.4%, 6.8%, 6.0% and 5.5%. Policies: 3.99m (FY26), 4.17m, 4.32m, 4.47m, 4.61m (net additions fall from about 278,000 a year to about 180,000 as motor prices rise); average premium +1.0%, +2.1%, +2.9%, +2.3%, +2.1%.
  • Operating profit (= pre-tax profit) = earned premium times (1 minus CR) + investment result + other items. Technical result 102.6, 102.0, 113.0, 116.9, 118.8; investment result 43.5, 46.5, 50.0, 52.5, 55.0 (portfolio of about EUR 1.26bn growing 5% to 6% a year at a flat return; trailing return fell to 2.75% in June 2026; rental income from the renovated Madrid building resumes by mid-2027, about EUR 1.5m in FY27 and EUR 3m to 3.5m a year after); other non-technical items -7.0, -7.0, -7.5, -7.5, -8.0. Pre-tax profit 139.1, 141.5, 155.5, 161.9, 165.8. The model reproduces H1 26 (pre-tax about 69.5, net 52.1).
  • CR (the swing driver: 1 point is about EUR 12.8m pre-tax, EUR 9.6m net, in FY27). FY26 91.4% (H1 91.1%, H2 91.6%). FY27 92.0% (H1 91.6%, H2 92.4%): motor +0.5 point (2027 earned price of about +2% set by 2026's 1% to 2% written price, against claims cost per policy of +3.5% to +4.5% with a 2027 Baremo update of about 3.5% against 2.7%, net of new-business seasoning), home +1.7 points (weather normalising), health -8 points (HNA network from Q1 2027). FY28 91.7% (repricing earns through, health 106%). FY29 to FY30 drift to 92.2% as the market softens. Expense ratio 20.6%, 20.5%, 20.3%, 20.2%, 20.1%.
  • Net financial items are zero: interest is part of the business, the investment result is inside operating profit, and the only debt is EUR 1.6m of leases.
  • Tax 25.0% (Spanish statutory; FY25 24.9%); the 2020 to 2022 tax audits are not modelled.
  • Dividend per share is the dividend declared for the year: payout 60% (FY26; the first interim of EUR 0.016548 was paid 7-Jul-26), 65% (FY27), 70% (FY28 to FY30). Solvency stays about 196% (own funds EUR 491.5m against an SCR of EUR 250.3m at 30-Jun-26; retention of about EUR 35m a year matches SCR growth at 7% premium growth).
  • Share count flat at 1,088.4m; treasury purchases for employee plans (about EUR 1m a year) are re-delivered, and the liquidity contract nets to zero, so buybacks less issuance are zero. No acquisitions or disposals (management says none are planned).
  • Half-years (Part 3). H2 26 revenue +9.5% (against 580), margin 10.95% (CR 91.6%, net income about 52.2 against 41.9 in H2 25, which carried about EUR 5m of severe Q3 claims). H1 27 +7.7%, 11.07% (CR 91.6%, seasonal Q1 expense benefit). H2 27 +7.1%, 10.31% (CR 92.4%: the severity step, autumn weather and the slow 2026 pricing all land in this half).
  • Probability-weighted FY27 (25% upside at CR 91.0%, net income about 116; 50% base at 92.0%, about 106; 25% downside at 93.8%, about 89): about 104. The skew is negative.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

  • Guidance is verbal and outcome-only: a CR in the "low 90s" (Q1 26 call, 27-Apr-26, after "comfortable with 92% to 93%" in February 2026); an expense ratio below 20% over the medium term; more than 4 million customers; net profit above EUR 100m; solvency not below 150% with about 180% as the dividend reference; no premium, EPS or payout guidance ("we aim to outgrow the market").
  • My inputs meet the CR guidance in every year (91.4% to 92.2%), pass EUR 100m of net profit in FY26 and 4 million customers in Q1 27, and keep solvency near 196%. They do not reach an expense ratio below 20% before FY30 (20.1%), because I assume the operating leverage is reinvested in price, marketing and technology, as Q2 26 (flat year on year at about 21.4%) suggests.
  • Against consensus (FactSet median, 4 to 5 brokers): net income 104.3 against 103 (FY26), 106.1 against 114 (FY27, -7%), 116.6 against 122 (FY28, -4%); EPS 0.096 against 0.094, 0.098 against 0.103, 0.107 against 0.115 (the FY28 EPS consensus rests on four brokers and is higher than its own net-income line implies). GWP 1,229 against 1,233, 1,316 against 1,297 (+1.5%), 1,403 against 1,413. Dividend per share 0.058 against 0.055 (consensus is dated by payment period). The whole FY27 earnings gap is the CR: consensus implies about 91.2% on my volume; I use 92.0%.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Revenue. The model's revenue history is FactSet's FF_SALES: FY25 1,122 = earned premium 1,076.7 + about 45 of investment and other income. The brokers' revenue is gross written premiums: FactSet's brokers'-basis actuals (973, 1,020, 1,130 for FY23 to FY25) match reported GWP (about 973, 1,019.5, 1,134.7). So consensus revenue of 1,233 / 1,297 / 1,413 is GWP growth of +8.7%, +5.2%, +8.9%, and must be compared with my GWP (1,229 / 1,316 / 1,403), not with my revenue input (1,234 / 1,325 / 1,416, which by coincidence is close to consensus in FY26).
  • Operating profit. The brokers'-basis "EBITDA" (-38.2, 51.2, 77.4 for FY23 to FY25) is the technical (underwriting) result; the brokers' "EBIT" (mean 136 for FY26, 152 for FY27) and consensus pre-tax profit (136, 149, 163) are pre-tax profit, which is this form's operating profit. My operating profit: 139.1, 141.5, 155.5.
  • Net income and EPS. The company reports no adjusted earnings; reported, FactSet-adjusted and brokers' EPS were identical for FY25 (0.079). EPS uses 1,088.4m shares. There is no bridge item other than the tax rate (consensus implies about 24% to 25%).
  • Debt. FactSet consensus shows 75.5 of debt for FY26 to FY28; the company has no financial debt (1.6 of leases at 30-Jun-26). Ignore it.
  • Dividends. FactSet's FY25 dividend per share (0.034) is the payment-period figure; the company declared EUR 45m (about 0.041) for FY25. My inputs are declared-for-the-year.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Linea Directa Aseguradora (LDA-ES): a Spanish pure direct-to-consumer personal-lines insurer selling through telephone, web and app from a single site in Tres Cantos, Madrid, with no branches or brokers and its own repair centres (CAR); segments are motor (about 81% of gross written premiums), home (about 15%), health (about 3%, formerly the Vivaz brand, 50% ceded to Munich Re under a quota share to December 2029) and small pet, SME and mobility lines; Spain is over 99.9% of premiums.

Request: T-D1

Peers: Mapfre (MAP-ES; the largest listed Spanish motor and home insurer, agent and bank distribution, owner of the direct brand Verti); Mutua Madrileña (private mutual, Spain's largest auto insurer, freezing tariffs for 50% of auto customers in 2026); Grupo Catalana Occidente (GCO-ES; agent-led Spanish multiline, raised motor premiums without higher cancellations); Admiral Group (ADM-GB; the European direct motor cost leader, present in Spain through Admiral Seguros); Allianz (ALV-DE; Allianz Seguros in Spain and the low-cost platform Allianz Direct); AXA (CS-FR; AXA Seguros and Direct Seguros in Spain); Generali (G-IT; owner of the Spanish direct insurer Génesis); Sabre Insurance (SBRE-GB; margin-first UK motor insurer); Direct Line Insurance Group (now Aviva; a direct motor insurer whose pricing and cost record failed); Unipol (UNI-IT; Italian motor pricing against inflation) Operating conditions: a renewed rise in Spanish claims inflation (energy-led consumer inflation of 4.5% in August 2026 feeding parts, labour and the annual Baremo bodily-injury scale); a mutual competitor freezing tariffs while the rest of the market raises them; a soft market once sector motor margins recover; a benign weather year against a storm or DANA year in home; a household affordability squeeze that drives shopping and trading down; rising use of price comparison sites and AI shopping agents Inputs: Can Linea Directa raise its average motor premium from the about 2% it cited in the Q1 2026 call (27-Apr-26) toward the market's about 6% within two to four quarters, and what did the same move cost it in customers in 2023 to 2024 (a 4.2% fall in policyholders in 2023), compared with Mapfre, Mutua, Allianz and Grupo Catalana Occidente in the same period? Is the motor loss ratio stable or improving underneath the group figures (72.9% in H1 2026 against 72.8% in H1 2025, interim report of 24-Jul-26), and how much of the H1 2026 group combined-ratio improvement (92.3% to 91.1%) came from home weather (home loss ratio down 3.2 points to 58.3%) and health rather than motor? Is the health line's path to breakeven credible (combined ratio 140.3% in 2024, 125.7% in 2025, 125.1% in H1 2026; the HNA medical network replacing DKV from Q1 2027; a 2024 reinsurance addendum with a management-expenses commission and profit sharing), and how have other small Spanish health books fared? Is the medium-term expense ratio target below 20% (21.1% in FY25, 20.2% in H1 2026, with Q1 2026 helped by seasonally low marketing and IT spend per the CFO) achievable while technology and compliance spend rises? Has any direct insurer kept growing above its market while repricing upward into an inflation step, and what distinguished it? Focus: Whether the business can do in 2027 what it did in 2023 to 2024 (reprice toward the market fast enough to protect its margin) without losing the above-market volume growth it has had since 2025, given that its motor loss ratio is already flat and its 2026 pricing has run below the market.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Mapfre (MAP-ES; Iberian motor and home combined ratios, the local benchmark); Mutua Madrileña (motor combined-ratio ceiling of 97% and tariff freezes); Grupo Catalana Occidente (GCO-ES; Spanish motor and multi-risk combined ratios); Admiral Group (ADM-GB; dispersion of direct motor outcomes through the 2022 to 2026 UK cycle, and reserve releases as a share of premium); Sabre Insurance (SBRE-GB; single-line motor earnings volatility); Direct Line Insurance Group (now Aviva; forecast misses in the 2022 to 2023 inflation shock); Unipol (UNI-IT; Italian motor combined ratio through inflation) Operating conditions: a Baremo update above or below the prior year's 2.7%; parts, paint and labour inflation rising with energy prices; normal against benign home weather; Spain's Insurance Compensation Consortium absorbing extraordinary but not ordinary weather losses; falling or rising reinvestment yields on a fixed-income portfolio of about EUR 1.06bn with a duration of 3.53 years (June 2026); a soft market after margin recovery Inputs: How concentrated are the drivers of this company's earnings change from FY23 to FY26 and forward: the motor combined ratio (motor is about 81% of premiums; the company's own sensitivity puts 1 point of combined ratio at about EUR 7.8m of net profit for 2025), home weather, the health deficit (EUR 2.8m technical loss in H1 2026), investment income (total return 2.75% for the twelve months to June 2026 against 3.12% for FY25) and the return of rental income from the renovated Madrid building by June 2027? Historically, how wide has the year-on-year range of Spanish motor insurers' combined ratios been in the year after their average written price fell below claims inflation (2015 to 2022 flat premiums, the 2016 Baremo reform, 2022 to 2023)? How much did reserve development contribute to Linea Directa's combined ratios in FY24, FY25 and H1 2026 after the provisioning confidence level returned from the 95th to the 85th percentile, and what do its Solvency and Financial Condition Reports show? How often have sell-side forecasts for Spanish and UK motor insurers that assumed a stable combined ratio into a claims-inflation step been met, and by how much were they missed? Focus: How wide and how skewed the range of FY27 combined ratios and earnings is for this company, given that almost all of its earnings variance sits in one ratio and Spanish consumer inflation was 4.5% in August 2026.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Mapfre (MAP-ES; raised its 2026 combined-ratio forecast range in March 2026; read-across on Spanish motor pricing and claims); Grupo Catalana Occidente (GCO-ES; Spanish motor and home results); Mutua Madrileña (non-listed; published half-year results and tariff policy); Admiral Group (ADM-GB; direct motor guidance and consensus behaviour through a claims-inflation step); Sabre Insurance (SBRE-GB; guidance kept or cut as UK motor softened); Unipol (UNI-IT; motor margin guidance against inflation) Operating conditions: the 2027 Baremo update published around December 2026; continued energy-led Spanish inflation; Mutua extending or narrowing its tariff freeze; benign against normal home weather; revised Solvency II rules from 30-Jan-27 and dividend declarations in February and March 2027; thin sell-side coverage (four to five brokers with estimates) Inputs: Linea Directa's guidance is verbal: a combined ratio in the "low 90s" (Q1 2026 call, 27-Apr-26, after "comfortable with 92% to 93%" in February 2026 and "92% is too good to be true on a consistent basis" in Q3 2025), an expense ratio below 20% over the medium term, more than 4 million customers and net profit above EUR 100m, solvency not below 150% with about 180% as the dividend reference. FactSet consensus at 25-Oct-26 is net income of EUR 103m, 114m and 122m for FY26 to FY28 and revenue of EUR 1,233m, 1,297m and 1,413m; FactSet's brokers'-basis revenue actuals match gross written premiums, so what combined ratio and written-premium growth does each broker (UBS, JB Capital, Bestinver, Renta 4, Santander) assume, and how far does consensus extrapolate the H1 2026 combined ratio of 91.1%? What do the Q3 2026 results (28-Oct-26) show against Q3 2025, which carried about EUR 5m of severe bodily-injury claims? How has the company guided and been revised around past claims-inflation turns (2022 to 2023), and does it ever pre-announce? How likely is a payout increase or a first buyback at the FY26 results (late February 2027), given solvency of 196.3% at June 2026? What do peers' latest results imply for Spanish motor claims trends in H2 2026 and 2027? Focus: How likely each outcome is over the next four earnings events, for guidance (raised, met or reduced) and for FY27 and FY28 consensus net income (upgraded, held or cut), and what would have to happen for each.

Request: T-I2

Peers: Mapfre (MAP-ES; the Spanish motor narrative); Admiral Group (ADM-GB; the direct motor narrative, de-rated on autonomous-vehicle fears); Gjensidige (GJF-NO) and Tryg (TRYG-DK) (Nordic P&C narrative on a peaked cycle and AI shopping agents); Sabre Insurance (SBRE-GB; UK motor softening narrative); Unipol (UNI-IT; Italian motor narrative); Mutua Madrileña (press coverage of its tariff policy); Bankinter (BKT-ES; holder of 17.42% of Linea Directa, covered by bank analysts) Operating conditions: a risk-off market in which small, illiquid and weak-momentum stocks are de-rated; a Spanish motor market whose repricing has peaked; renewed energy-led inflation; the sector's January 2027 capital release; AI shopping-agent worries for motor insurers whose customers can shop on price Inputs: For each dynamic, how widely is it discussed by sell-side analysts and by investors: (1) the company pricing motor at about 2% against a market at about 6% (Q1 2026 call) and what that means for 2027 earned margins; (2) the flat motor loss ratio (72.9% against 72.8% in H1 2026) beneath an improving group combined ratio; (3) the home combined ratio of 86.6% in H1 2026, with benign weather cited by the company, against a CFO-described normal of 90% to 91% (February 2026); (4) the 2027 Baremo update and its link to 2026 inflation and pensions; (5) Mutua Madrileña's tariff freeze and 97% combined-ratio ceiling, and whether it can last in an inflation step; (6) health breakeven under the HNA network from Q1 2027; (7) the fall in the investment return to 2.75% and the return of rental income in 2027; (8) surplus capital (solvency 196.3%), the payout path and the absence of buybacks; (9) a possible sale of Bankinter's 17.42% stake; (10) the reserve confidence level held at the 85th percentile and undisclosed prior-year development; (11) AI shopping agents and comparison sites raising churn and acquisition costs for Spanish direct insurers; (12) the CEO's absence from analyst Q&A and the thin coverage Focus: Which of these dynamics are already in the sell-side and investor narrative and which are discussed by no one, particularly the 2027 earned-price and home-weather effects on the combined ratio and the capital-return capacity.

Request: OWN-1

Question: How is the Spanish Baremo bodily-injury compensation scale for 2027 set for motor insurers such as Linea Directa Aseguradora (LDA-ES), Mapfre and Mutua Madrileña: what exactly links it to the pension revaluation and to consumer inflation (which months' inflation, which formula), when will it be published, what public forecasts of the 2027 revaluation exist, how have past updates (4.1% in 2022, 8.5% in 2023, 3.8% in 2024, 2.8% in 2025, 2.7% in 2026) translated into Spanish motor loss ratios and reserve strengthening on open claims at Linea Directa, Mapfre and Allianz Seguros, and what share of Spanish motor claims cost is bodily injury?

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 5 November 2026, 04:51 to 05:10 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Linea Directa Aseguradora (LDA-ES): third and fourth sets of questions, predictability of the forecasts, and the gap to guidance, consensus and narrative

Written 2026-11-04 by the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo for Richard.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes for the Consumer

What is new since the second set (26-Oct-26), and what it changes. The second set was written before the Q3 26 results (28-Oct-26). The AlphaSense reports written on 3 and 4 November carry those results, and they are the most important new facts in this work:

  • Q3 26 (reported 28-Oct-26). Nine-month net income EUR 80.4m (+34.8%); nine-month GWP EUR 919.1m (+8.9%); nine-month CR 91.3% (loss ratio 71.3%, expense ratio 20.0%) against 93.4% a year earlier; Q3 standalone CR 91.7% (loss ratio 72.2%, expense ratio 19.4%); motor GWP EUR 750m (+9.5%) on 2.891m motor policies (+7.9%); home GWP EUR 128.9m (+2.6%) on about 796,000 policies (+3.5%); health GWP EUR 35.7m (+17%), health CR 124.1% for nine months and 122.3% in Q3; total policyholders 3.90m (+6.9%); Solvency II 191.5% (down from 196.3% in June because rising yields marked the bond portfolio down; own funds EUR 492.3m, SCR EUR 257.0m); total portfolio return 3.14% (reinvestment yield 2.62%, duration 3.45 years); financial investment result +14.1% to EUR 34.3m; return on average equity 25.6%. Management said on the call that customer acquisition "slowed slightly" because of the competitive environment.
  • Derived by this analyst for Q3 alone (approximate; the derivations subtract H1 from nine months and use the first set's H1 25 figures): Q3 26 GWP about EUR 310m (+8.3%, against +9.2% in H1); Q3 net income about EUR 28.3m against about EUR 15.9m (the Q3 25 quarter carried about EUR 5m of severe bodily-injury claims, 2.3 points of that quarter's motor CR); Q3 25 CR about 95.6% (loss ratio about 74.7%, expense ratio about 20.9%); so Q3 26's loss ratio improved about 2.5 points year on year of which about 1.9 points is the absence of the severe claims, and the expense ratio improved about 1.5 points. Motor loss ratio: 73.3% for nine months against 74.3%, of which about 0.75 points is the Q3 25 severe claims, so the underlying motor loss ratio is flat to about 0.25 points better, not improving materially. Motor average written premium in Q3 about +1.5% (GWP about +8.9% on policies +7.9%). Home average premium in Q3 about +0.3% (GWP about +3.8% on policies +3.5%), so home discounting is easing. Net policyholder additions: about 70,000 in Q1, 60,000 in Q2, 40,000 in Q3; the second set's 3.99m at end-2026 and "4 million in Q1 27" are now too early; 3.94m to 3.95m at end-2026 and 4 million in Q2 27 are more likely.
  • The 2027 Baremo is now nearly known. The scale follows the public pension revaluation, which is the average year-on-year CPI from December 2025 to November 2026; the preliminary estimate reported on 3-Nov-26 is 3.33%, against 2.7% for 2026; the INE confirms November CPI on 15 December. With Spanish CPI at 4.9% in September and October 2026, the final figure is likely 3.3% to 3.5%, which is the second set's central assumption (3.5%). The >4% tail for 2027 has closed; the tail has moved to the 2028 update (set by 2027 inflation, which the Bank of Spain now puts at 3.7%, BBVA at 3.2%, CaixaBank at 2.5% with upside risk above 3.0%) and to parts and labour (spare parts +5.1% year on year in September 2026, repair and maintenance +3.7%; Jefferies).
  • Peers. Mapfre strengthened Iberia motor reserves in Q3 26 by 2 to 3 points of the nine-month claims ratio as prudence against Baremo inflation, reported a Q3 standalone Iberia motor CR of 98.4%, a nine-month Iberia motor CR of about 94% to 96% (the reports cite both figures), motor premiums +1.1% with the fleet -1.2% and average premium about +3%, and said it will move from clean-up to premium and unit growth in its 2027 to 2029 plan while pricing "slightly above inflation, no significant peaks". AXA's 2027 to 2029 plan targets more than 10% a year of direct growth in Spain. Mutua's solvency is 297%; its 2025 motor CR was 96.7%; its president said in April 2026 it would extend freezes only "without jeopardising solvency and margins" and has given no trigger. Occident's motor CR was 92.8% in H1 26 on motor premiums +6.9%.
  • Capital. The FY25 dividend of EUR 45m was 70% of the parent company's individual profit (EUR 78.2m), the basis the company uses, and 53% of consolidated net income; management has historically targeted 70% to 90% of individual profit and described itself as "extremely cautious on capital returns"; Cartival (20.56%) needs cash dividends (it received EUR 129m from Bankinter and Linea Directa in 2025 to fund EUR 40m of family distributions) and Bankinter (17.42%) is a passive holder; the risk margin is EUR 34.9m, so the Solvency II revision from 30-Jan-27 (cost of capital 6% to 4.75%) releases roughly EUR 7m of own funds, about 3 points of solvency.

The cross-check in the one-page model summary. The model's net income and EPS for FY26 to FY30 (104.3, 106.2, 116.6, 121.5, 124.5; EPS 0.10, 0.10, 0.11, 0.11, 0.11) match the second set's cross-check (104.3, 106.1, 116.6, 121.4, 124.3) to within rounding, so no year is flagged and no forecast input is wrong on that test. The summary's one flag is a phasing note: "FY2026: Part 3's operating profit margin by period and Part 1's margin for the year differ; the periods were moved by +11.0 points to match the year." This is a mechanical artefact, not an input error: the model's own half-year history for this company is calculated from EBITDA, which FactSet does not hold for an insurer, so the model's H1 26 operating profit was zero and the H2 26 period had to be shifted so the two halves sum to the year. The Financial Analyst should confirm that after the shift the H2 26 period margin stands at the Part 3 figure (10.95%, now 11.21% after my change below) and that the H1 27 and H2 27 periods were not moved. I change one input (section 6, inputs-changed.md): the FY26 operating margin from 11.27% to 11.40% and the H2 26 period margin from 10.95% to 11.21%, because nine-month net income of EUR 80.4m sits at the top of the second set's expected range (EUR 77m to 80m) and a normal Q4 (about EUR 25m against EUR 26m in a benign-weather Q4 25) gives FY26 net income of about EUR 105.5m (EPS about EUR 0.097) rather than EUR 104.3m. I hold every FY27 to FY30 input, for the reasons in D-04.

Where I disagree with the first or second set (each is used below).

  1. Capital surplus (second set, P-02). The second set kept solvency at about 197% through FY26 to FY28 at a 60% to 70% payout and cited a one-off surplus of EUR 45m to 60m above 180%. The Q3 figure shows the rate sensitivity biting: own funds were flat at EUR 492m despite EUR 28m of Q3 profit (about EUR 27m of negative bond valuation). On my arithmetic the ratio at end-2026, after a final dividend of about EUR 45m (60% payout for the year), is about 180% to 185% before the January 2027 revision adds about 3 points. The 180% reference is therefore reached, not exceeded, at the planned payout; a buyback is very unlikely (also because Cartival needs cash), and the 65% and 70% payouts in FY27 to FY28 remain feasible only if the board accepts running at 180% to 185% and rates do not rise further. I keep the dividend inputs but the "latent EUR 50m return" option in P-02 should be dropped from the memo.
  2. Expense ratio (first set C-13 and C-77; second set P-01). Both sets treated the Q1 26 expense ratio as seasonal and the sub-20% target as a 2028 or later possibility. Q3 26 printed 19.4% (about 1.5 points better year on year) and nine months 20.0%; the improvement is not confined to Q1. FY26 will land near 20.2% to 20.3% rather than the modelled 20.6%, and the sub-20% target is reachable in 2027 if management chooses. The second set's mechanism (leverage is a buffer, spent to hold the reported CR) is right; its arithmetic was too conservative. This is the main reason the FY26 input rises.
  3. Customer milestones (second set P-10). 4 million customers is now a Q2 27 event, not Q1 27, and the end-2026 count is about 3.95m, not 3.99m; policy growth is decelerating slightly faster than modelled, which the FY27 revenue input (+7.4%) still accommodates because average premium is rising.
  4. The first set's downside share price (C-83, EUR 0.86 to 0.95 in a claims-inflation step) used an EPS cut to EUR 0.090 to 0.095; the second set's downside case has FY27 EPS at EUR 0.082, which at 9.5x to 10x gives EUR 0.78 to 0.82. The memo should use the second set's figure: the downside case is about 33% to 37% below EUR 1.23, not 23% to 30%.
  5. Reserving (first set C-54). The OWN-3 report finds, in the 2025 individual accounts, a prior-year motor third-party-liability run-off surplus of EUR 15.9m and a deficit of EUR 3.4m (the two figures are cited from the same accounts and are not reconciled in the report); a net surplus of that size would be about 1.5 points of the 2025 motor loss ratio. The first set's "unquantified reserve-release component" therefore has a first number against it, and it is material. I write a research request on it.

Scores. Third-set answers: how positive the thing asked about is for the predictability of the forecasts over the next 18 months (50 neutral). Fourth-set answers: how favourable the gap is between our assessment and what guidance, consensus and the narrative already reflect (50 neutral).

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.
